Variational Analysis in the Wasserstein Space
Nicolas Lanzetti ∗ Caltech lnicolas@caltech.edu, Antonio Terpin ∗ ETH Zürich aterpin@ethz.ch, Florian Dörfler ETH Zürich dorfler@ethz.ch

Abstract
We study optimization problems whereby the optimization variable is a probability measure. Since the space of probability measures is not a vector space, many classical methods for optimization (e.g., gradients) do not directly apply. Thus, one typically resorts to the abstract machinery of infinite-dimensional analysis or other ad-hoc methodologies, not tailored to the space of probability measures, which however involve projections or rely on convexity-type assumptions. We believe instead that these problems call for a comprehensive methodological framework for calculus in the space of probability measures. In this work, we combine ideas from optimal transport, variational analysis, and Wasserstein gradient flows to equip the Wasserstein space (i.e., the space of probability measures endowed with the Wasserstein distance) with a variational structure, both by combining and extending existing results and introducing novel tools. Our theoretical analysis culminates in general necessary optimality conditions. These conditions (i) resemble the optimality conditions in Euclidean spaces, such as the KKT conditions, (ii) are intuitive, informative, and easy to study, and (iii) yield closed-form solutions or can be used to design computationally attractive algorithms. We accompany our theoretical results with numerous examples and present applications to machine learning, drug discovery, and distributionally robust optimization.

1
Main result

This work considers optimization problems of the form

\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{C}}\,\mathcal{J}(\mu),

(1.1)

where
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a set of admissible probability measures and
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
is a functional to minimize. This abstract problem setting stems from the observation that numerous fields, including machine learning, robust optimization, and biology, tackle their own version of (
1.1
), but with ad-hoc methods that often rely on structural assumptions that do not hold in broader settings.
Despite the recent efforts in the literature
[
1
]
, these problems still demand a comprehensive theory for the optimization problem (
1.1
), which is the subject of this work.
Specifically,
we derive novel necessary first-order optimality conditions for (
1.1
), for arbitrary functionals and constraints. These formally resemble the rationales of Euclidean spaces (e.g., Karush–Kuhn–Tucker and Lagrange conditions) and are intuitive, informative, and easy to study.
As a byproduct of our analysis, we translate tools from variational analysis (e.g., generalized subgradients, normal cones, tangent cones, etc.) to the Wasserstein space (i.e., the space of probability measures endowed with the Wasserstein distance).
After illustrating these novel tools in numerous pedagogical examples, we tackle open problems arising in machine learning, drug discovery, and
distributionally robust optimization (DRO)
, showcasing how our conditions result either in closed-form solutions of (
1.1
) or computationally attractive algorithms.

Our main results are general first-order optimality conditions of (
1.1
):

{informaltheorem}

[First-order optimality conditions]

If
\mu^{\ast}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is an optimal solution of (
1.1
) with finite second moment and if a constraint qualification holds, then the “Wasserstein subgradients” are “aligned” with the constraints at “optimality”, i.e.,

\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})+\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu^{\ast}),

where
\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})
is the “Wasserstein subgradient” of
\mathcal{J}
at
\mu^{\ast}
,
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu^{\ast})
is the “Wasserstein normal cone” of
\mathcal{C}
at
\mu^{\ast}
and
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}
is a “null Wasserstein tangent vector” at
\mu^{\ast}
.

As corollaries of our theorem, we obtain the “Wasserstein counterparts” of Fermat’s rule in the unconstrained setting (i.e., the gradient vanishes at optimality) and the Lagrange conditions for (in)equality-constrained settings (i.e., the “gradients” of the objective and the constraint are “aligned” at optimality, see
Figure

1
).

Before diving into variational analysis in the Wasserstein space, we illustrate our optimality conditions by informally studying a simple and accessible version of (
1.1
).
For
\theta\neq 0
and
\varepsilon>0
, consider the problem

\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\;\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle\right]\qquad\text{subject to}\qquad\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[\left\|x\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\varepsilon^{2},

(1.2)

depicted in
Figure

1
for
\theta=\begin{bmatrix}1&1\end{bmatrix}^{\top}
.
To get some intuition, let us restrict to Dirac deltas of the form
\delta_{x}
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Accordingly, (
1.2
) reduces to

\inf_{\left\|x\right\|^{2}\leq\varepsilon^{2}}\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle.

This optimization problem can be studied through standard first-order optimality conditions in Euclidean spaces.
Since the gradient of the objective
\nabla{\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle}=\theta
never vanishes, the optimal solution (if it exists) lies at the boundary.
We thus seek the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda>0
such that

0=\nabla{\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle}+\lambda\nabla{\left\|x\right\|^{2}}=\theta+2\lambda x\quad\text{and}\quad\varepsilon^{2}=\left\|x\right\|^{2},

(1.3)

which yields
x=-\varepsilon\frac{\theta}{\left\|\theta\right\|}
and
\lambda=\frac{\left\|\theta\right\|}{2\varepsilon}
.
Now back to (
1.2
): After basic algebraic manipulations, our main result (stated informally above) tells us that any solution
\mu^{\ast}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of (
1.2
) satisfies the Lagrange-like condition

0=2\lambda x+\theta\quad\mu^{\ast}\text{-a.e.}\quad\text{and}\quad\varepsilon^{2}=\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\|x\right\|^{2}\right]=\frac{\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}}{4\lambda^{2}},

(1.4)

for some
\lambda\geq 0
constant across the support of
\mu^{\ast}
; cf.
Figure

1
for
\theta=\begin{bmatrix}1&1\end{bmatrix}^{\top}
.
We conclude that the mass of any candidate solution is necessarily concentrated at
-\varepsilon\frac{\theta}{\left\|\theta\right\|}
.
In particular, our optimality condition in (
1.4
) mirrors its counterpart on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
in (
1.3
).

Figure 1:
“Gradients” are “aligned” with the constraints at optimality.
The figure on the left depicts two empirical candidate solutions

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mu_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5508,0}\mu_{2}}
for (
1.1
) with
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\,|\,\mathbb{E}^{(x_{1},x_{2})\sim\mu}\left[x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}\right]\leq\varepsilon^{2}\}
(i.e., bounded second moment) and
\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{(x_{1},x_{2})\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mu}}\left[x_{1}+x_{2}\right]
, of which we show the contours and the gradient vector field.
The solid (resp. dashed) black arrows represent the gradient of the constraint function
\mathbb{E}^{(x_{1},x_{2})\sim\mu}\left[x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}\right]-\varepsilon^{2}
at
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mu_{1}}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5508,0}\mu_{2}}
).
Here,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mu_{1}}
is indeed a candidate optimal solution: The gradient of the objective is aligned with the gradient of the constraint.
For
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5508,0}\mu_{2}}
, instead, these two are not aligned. Thus,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5508,0}\mu_{2}}
cannot be optimal.
The figure on the right shows that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mu^{\ast}}
satisfies the optimality condition for (
1.1
) with
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\,|\,W_{2}\left(\mu,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\mu}}\right)\leq\varepsilon\}
(i.e., Wasserstein ball centered at
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\mu}}
of radius
\varepsilon
) and
\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{(x_{1},x_{2})\sim\mu}\left[x_{1}+x_{2}\right]
, of which the contours and the gradient vector field are shown. The black arrows connecting
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\mu}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mu^{\ast}}
represent the gradient of the constraint function
W_{2}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mu},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\mu}}\right)^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mu^{\ast}}
is optimal, the gradient of the objective and the constraint are aligned at all the “particles” of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mu^{\ast}}
.

1.1
Introduction to the broader context

Optimization problems over the space of probability measures are ubiquitous across a variety of fields.

DRO
.

DRO
has emerged as a major paradigm for decision-making under uncertainty
[
2
,
3
]
. In
DRO
, one accepts the inherent ambiguity in real-world data and other a priori assumptions in uncertainty modeling, where the true underlying probability measure is uncertain and difficult to ascertain, and seeks decisions that are robust against a range of possible probability measures. Thus,
DRO
falls within the scope of (
1.1
) where
\mathcal{J}
is a risk measure
[
4
,
5
,
6
]
and
\mathcal{C}
is a so-called ambiguity set of probability measures, often defined in terms of the Kullback-Leibler divergence
[
7
,
8
,
9
]
or an optimal transport discrepancy
[
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
]
.

Inverse problems.

In inverse problems one seeks the state of a system given some noisy observations. For instance, Bayesian inference amounts to solving

\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}-\log(p(z_i|x))\right]+\kullbackLeibler(\mu||\hat{\mu}),

(1.5)

where
z_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
are the observations,
p(z_{i}|x)
is the probability of the observation
z_{i}
given the state
x\sim\mu
, and
\kullbackLeibler
is the Kullback-Leibler divergence between a candidate posterior
\mu
and the prior
\hat{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

[
14
]
. Many inference problems (e.g., see
[
14
, Table 1]
and
[
15
,
16
]
) result from variants of (
1.5
).

Reinforcement learning.

The dual formulation
[
17
]
of the
reinforcement learning (RL)
problem seeks the optimal stationary state-action distribution
\mu^{\ast}\in\Delta(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})
, where
\mathcal{S}
is the state space,
\mathcal{A}
the action space, and
\Delta(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})
the set of stationary state-action distributions compatible with the dynamics (and constraints), that maximizes the expected reward
r:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
,

\mu^{\ast}\in\mathop{\rm arg\,max}_{\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})}\mathbb{E}^{(s,a)\sim\mu}\left[r(s,a)\right].

(1.6)

Variations of (
1.6
) yield different problem settings
[
18
,
19
,
20
]
.

Many others.

A growing number of fields are tackling optimization problems formulated in the space of probability measures, including weather forecasting
[
21
]
, single-cell perturbation responses
[
22
,
23
]
, neural network training
[
24
]
, generative models
[
25
]
, control of dynamical systems
[
26
,
27
]
, mean-field control
[
28
,
29
]
, and finance
[
30
,
2
]
, among others.

1.2
Related work

Our work studies (
1.1
) through the lens of optimal transport.
The theory of optimal transport, dating back to the seminal work of Monge
[
31
]
and Kantorovich
[
32
]
, defines a metric, the Wasserstein metric, on the space of probability measures.
While the space of probability measures is not a vector space, which makes most optimization tools in Banach spaces (e.g.,
[
33
,
34
]
) inapplicable, the theory of optimal transport enables a notion of differentiability, called Wasserstein differentiability, specifically tailored to the Wasserstein space
[
35
,
36
,
37
]
.
Wasserstein differentiability was exploited in
[
1
]
to derive first-order optimality conditions for (
1.1
) for problems with differentiable objectives and feasible sets given by sublevel sets of differentiable functionals.
In the context of optimal control,
[
38
,
39
,
29
]
use Wasserstein differentiability to derive optimality conditions for optimal control problems, whereas
[
27
]
explores the properties of the dynamic programming algorithm in the space of probability measures in discrete time. The theory has also been applied to derive algorithms to compute Wasserstein barycenters
[
40
,
41
,
42
]
, to analyze over-parametrized neural networks
[
43
,
24
]
, approximate inference
[
44
]
, and reinforcement learning
[
45
,
19
]
.

Unfortunately, many functionals over the Wasserstein space (including the squared Wasserstein distance itself) fail to be differentiable. This, together with the rigidity of the feasible sets
\mathcal{C}
considered so far, effectively hinders the deployment of these tools in many practical instances.
From a technical standpoint, these limitations are intrinsic in the choice of the variations in the Wasserstein space: Variations induced by the pushforward of (sufficiently regular)
transport maps
, as in
[
1
]
, are not expressive enough. For instance, the pushforward of an empirical probability measure is always empirical (in particular, mass cannot be split).
Thus, while attractive (e.g., perturbations can be captured by well-behaved functions, which form a vector space), a comprehensive theory of calculus requires a more general way of perturbing probability measures.

Intuitively, we can approximate a Dirac delta by Gaussians with vanishing variance. However, there is no transport map describing a variation from the Dirac delta to the approximating Gaussians. In this work, we therefore adopt a different approach and consider perturbations induced by
transport plans
. This more general approach requires us to dive into the generalized notion of Wasserstein subgradient proposed in
[
35
, Section 10.3]
and, importantly and contrary to the literature, in the generalized tangent space first introduced in
[
35
, Section 12]
.
Although these perturbations entail significant challenges (e.g., transport plans do not form a vector space), we show in this work that, if judiciously combined with traditional ideas from variational analysis
[
46
,
47
,
48
,
49
]
, they result in general necessary optimality conditions for (
1.1
).

Our approach offers several advantages over alternative methods for optimization in the space of probability measures.
First, our analysis is specifically tailored to the Wasserstein space and does not require the introduction of nonnegativity and normalization. For instance, if (
1.1
) is unconstrained, the corresponding optimality condition simply states that the gradient vanishes at optimality—just like in Euclidean settings.
Second, our optimality conditions hold in full generality, and, in particular, do not rely on convexity-type or linearity assumptions, which in some cases might allow one to study (
1.1
) through infinite-dimensional linear programming or convex analysis.
Third, analogously to the traditional Karush–Kuhn–Tucker conditions, we demonstrate that our optimality conditions can be used to both solve (
1.1
) in closed form, and devise efficient numerical methods when a closed-form solution is not available.

1.3
More details on our contributions

More specifically, our main contribution consists of four key aspects.
First, we provide a solid foundation for a theory of variational analysis in the Wasserstein space, introducing various concepts such as the generalized subgradient, normal cone, and tangent cone in the Wasserstein space.
For this, we need to resort to very general perturbations induced by transport plans. While invisible to the end user, these perturbations entail working with a tangent space which is not a linear space and carefully dealing with compactness issues.
Second, we provide closed-form and easy-to-use expressions for the subgradients of many functionals and for the normal cone to feasible sets of practical interest.
In particular, we show that the squared Wasserstein distance is not regularly subdifferentiable and only admits a generalized subgradient. This result, which we believe to be of independent interest, also confirms that a general theory of optimality conditions is required already for simple functions (in particular, the distance itself), and not only to cover all corner cases. The key technique to characterize the general subgradient of the squared Wasserstein distance involves its differentiability at regular measures together with approximation arguments, which is a promising technique to explore in future work addressing the differentiability of other functionals not covered in this work.
Third, we derive general first-order optimality conditions for (
1.1
). Inspired by classical variational analysis in Euclidean spaces, we prove our result by reformulating (
1.1
) as an optimization problem over the epigraph of
\mathcal{J}
. This way, we can establish our optimality conditions under very weak assumptions on the functionals and constraints.
Notably, as we demonstrate with several pedagogical examples, the technical complexity behind our optimality conditions is hidden from the end user, and their deployment is effectively analogous to what one would do in Euclidean spaces.
Fourth, we deploy our results to study a wide variety of optimization problems of the form (
1.1
) arising in machine learning, drug discovery, and
DRO
. Across all these settings, we show that our optimality conditions both enable novel insights and, when the problem of interest does not admit a closed-form solution, can be used to design effective computational methods.
We believe our tools enable the development of novel algorithms and results in machine learning, robust optimization, and biology, among others.

2
Subgradients and variational geometry

After recalling preliminaries in measure theory and optimal transport in

Sections

2.1
and
2.2
, we present variations in the
Wasserstein space in
Section

2.3
, introduce Wasserstein
subgradients in
Section

2.4
, and study the
variational geometry of the Wasserstein space in

Section

2.5
.

2.1
Preliminaries

All the maps considered in this work are tacitly assumed to be Borel, i.e., measurable with respect to the Borel
\sigma
-algebra.
The set of Borel probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the set of probability distributions with finite second moment. We write
\mu\ll\bar{\mu}
to indicate that
\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\bar{\mu}
, and we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{2,\mathrm{abs}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the set of absolutely continuous probability measures with respect to the Lebesgue measure with finite second moment. The support
\supp(\mu)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
of a probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the closed set

\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,|\,\mu(U)>0\text{ for each neighborhood $U$ of $x$}\right\}.
The identity map on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(x)=x
and, when clear from the context, we simply write
\mathrm{Id}
. The gradient of a function
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
\nabla{h}(x)
, and the partial derivatives of a function
c:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
at
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
are denoted
\nabla_{x}{c}(x,y)
and
\nabla_{y}{c}(x,y)
, respectively. For two expressions
f(x)
and
g(x)
, we write
f(x)=o\left(g(x)\right)
if, as
g(x)\to 0
, we have
\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\to 0
.
The
pushforward
of a probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
through a map
T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
(cf.
[
50
, Definition 1.2.2]
), denoted by
{T}_{\#}{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, is defined by
({T}_{\#}{\mu})(B)=\mu(T^{-1}(B))
for all Borel sets
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and it is a probability measure; see
[
50
, Lemma 1.2.3]
. Then, for any
{T}_{\#}{\mu}
-integrable
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, it holds

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi(y)\mathrm{d}({T}_{\#}{\mu})(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(T(x))\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
; see
[
50
, Corollary 1.2.6]
.
Finally (cf.
[
50
, Lemma 1.2.7]
), for any
T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
S:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{t}
measurable,

{(S\circ T)}_{\#}{\mu}={S}_{\#}{({T}_{\#}{\mu})}
.

2.2
Optimal transport

Given
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, their product measure is
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
. We say that
T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
transport map
from
\mu_{1}
to
\mu_{2}
if
{T}_{\#}{\mu_{1}}=\mu_{2}
or, equivalently,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi(y)\mathrm{d}({T}_{\#}{\mu_{1}})(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(T(x))\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)
for all
\phi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

[
50
, Lemma 1.2.5]
, where
C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
denotes the space of real-valued continuous bounded functions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For some
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, we denote by
\pi_{i}:(\mathbb{R}^{m})^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
the projection map on the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
component, i.e.,
\pi_{i}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})=x_{i}
. That is,
\pi_{i}
always denotes the projection onto the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
factor of the relevant product space; its precise domain and codomain are clear from the context. We use the projection map to obtain the
marginals
of a probability measure via pushforward,
{\pi_{i}}_{\#}{\mu_{1}}
. A
transport plan
between
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
is a probability measure
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
so that
{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\gamma}=\mu_{1}
and
{\pi_{2}}_{\#}{\gamma}=\mu_{2}
. A transport map may not exist between
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
(e.g., when
\mu_{1}=\delta_{x}
and
\mu_{2}
is a Gaussian distribution), but a transport plan always does (e.g., the product measure
\gamma=\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
). We collect them in the set of
transport plans

\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
. Given a lower semicontinuous function
c:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
, the optimal transport problem reads:

W_{c}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)\coloneqq\min_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}c(x,y)\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y).

(2.1)

The celebrated (quadratic) Wasserstein distance is a special case of (
2.1
) (cf.
[
50
, Definition 3.1.3]
), with
n=d
and
c(x,y)=\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}
:

W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)\coloneqq\left(\min_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(2.2)

where
\left\|\cdot\right\|
is the standard Euclidean norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We write
\Gamma_{c}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
and
\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
for the set of minimizers of (
2.1
) and (
2.2
), respectively.
The Wasserstein distance is a distance on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

[
51
, Section 6]
.
We define the Wasserstein ball of radius
\varepsilon
as

\mathbb{B}_{W_{2}}(\bar{\mu};\varepsilon)\coloneqq\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,|\,W_{2}\left(\mu,\bar{\mu}\right)<\varepsilon\right\}.

Throughout the work, we use two notions of convergence for probability measures. A sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(i) converges narrowly to
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, denoted by
\mu_{n}\rightharpoonup\mu
, if for all
\phi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}(x)\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
(cf.
[
50
, Definition 2.1.5]
) and (ii) converges in Wasserstein to
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\mu_{n}\to\mu
, if
\lim_{n\to\infty}W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\mu\right)=0
. The narrow topology is weaker than the Wasserstein topology on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Indeed, by
[
35
, Proposition 7.1.5]
,

\mu_{n}\to\mu
if and only if
\mu_{n}\rightharpoonup\mu
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}(x)\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
or, equivalently,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}(x)\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
for all real-valued continuous
\phi
with
\absolutevalue{\phi(x)}\leq C(1+\left\|x\right\|^{2})
.

2.3
Variations in the Wasserstein space

In the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a variation at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be interpreted as an “arrow”
\upsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
rooted at
x
. In the same spirit, in the Wasserstein space, a variation at
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a “(weighted) collection of arrows” for each point in the support of
\bar{\mu}

[
35
, Chapter 12]
. Formally, a
variation at
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a probability measure
\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
whose first marginal is
\bar{\mu}
(i.e.,
{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\xi}=\bar{\mu}
) (see
Figure

2
, left).
We denote the space of all variations at
\bar{\mu}
by
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\bar{\mu}}\coloneqq\{\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\,|\,{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\xi}=\bar{\mu}\}
. We can disintegrate (cf.
[
35
, Theorem 5.3.1]
)
\xi
to obtain a collection
\{\xi_{x}\}_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
where each
\xi_{x}
denotes the probability measure on the variations, i.e., the “(weighted) collection of arrows”
\bar{\mu}\text{-a.e.}

Our definition of variations is in line with the variational analysis literature
[
46
,
47
]
, where tangent vectors are expressed as the limits of scaled difference vectors.
The distance between two variations
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\bar{\mu}}
can be defined in terms of the distance between their (weighted) arrows. To do so, we have to account for the fact that different arrows starting from the same point can be coupled in different ways (cf.
Figure

2
, middle and right).
We express this coupling as a measure
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
so that
{\pi_{12}}_{\#}{\alpha}=\xi_{1}
and
{\pi_{13}}_{\#}{\alpha}=\xi_{2}
, where
(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})\in\supp(\alpha)
if and only if the arrows
\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are anchored at
\bar{x}
, and
\upsilon_{1}
and
\upsilon_{2}
are coupled. Note that
{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\alpha}={\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\xi_{1}}={\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\xi_{2}}=\bar{\mu}
, since
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
are variations at
\bar{\mu}
. We refer to
\alpha
as a
coupling
of the two variations
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
, reserving the term
transport plan
for the analogous object between two probability measures: a transport plan couples two measures, whereas a coupling couples two variations sharing the common anchor
\bar{x}
.
Then, the distance between
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
with common anchor
\bar{\mu}
amounts to the minimum distance that can be obtained among all such couplings (cf.
Figure

2
, middle):

\displaystyle W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\min_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{1}-\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(2.3)

where
\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
is the set of all couplings, as defined above, between
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
:

\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\coloneqq\left\{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\,|\,{\pi_{12}}_{\#}{\alpha}=\xi_{1},{\pi_{13}}_{\#}{\alpha}=\xi_{2}\right\}.

In view of
[
35
, Proposition 12.4.6]
,
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right)=0
implies
W_{2}\left(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right)=0
.
Similarly, we can also define the inner product and norm:

\displaystyle\left\langle\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}),

(2.4)

\displaystyle\left\|\xi_{i}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{i}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}(\bar{x},\upsilon_{i})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad i\in\{1,2\}.

(2.5)

With these definitions, the Euclidean-like identity

W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right)^{2}=\left\|\xi_{1}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}-2\left\langle\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+\left\|\xi_{2}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}
, which we exploit in our proofs, holds. The identity reveals the motivation behind the
\min
and
\max
in (
2.3
) and (
2.4
): They are the only two terms involving couplings and they appear with opposite signs. Intuitively, to minimize the distance between two variations we need to “maximally align” the arrows at each particle; cf.
Figure

2
.

In Euclidean spaces, given a variation
\upsilon
at a point
x
, we can construct a new point by altering
x
according to
\upsilon
:
y=x+\upsilon
. Analogously, given a variation
\xi
at a probability measure
\bar{\mu}
, we obtain a new probability measure by transporting, for each
x\in\supp(\bar{\mu})
, mass along the variations
\upsilon
, weighted according to
\xi
:
\mu={(\pi_{1}+\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi}
. However, unlike the Euclidean counterpart where the variation displacing
x
to
y
is
\upsilon=y-x
, in the Wasserstein space we have multiple ways of connecting
\bar{\mu}
and
\mu
, described by the transport plans
\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)
. Each of these
\gamma\in\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)
induces a variation
\xi={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma}
.
Moreover, the space of variations is not a vector space. However, we can equip it with an “almost linear” structure (see
Figure

2
for an intuition):

Definition 2.1

(An “almost linear” structure of the space of variations)
.

Given
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\bar{\mu}}
, and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
,
we define:

1.

A “local” zero:

\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\coloneqq\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{0}.

2.

A scalar multiplication
1
1

1

We highlight the distinction between our multiplication, which affects the displacement of the probability mass but not the total mass, and the multiplication in the space of signed measures, which instead merely affects the amount of probability mass at each location without displacing it. As we never operate in the space of signed measures, the notational override does not create ambiguity.
:

\tau\xi_{1}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\tau\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi_{1}}.

3.

An addition:
For
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
,

\xi_{1}\,+_{\alpha}\,\xi_{2}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}+\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha}.

Another difference is that while
\upsilon
describes a geodesic in the Euclidean space, i.e.,
\left\|\upsilon\right\|=\left\|y-x\right\|
and
\left\|\varepsilon\upsilon\right\|=\varepsilon\left\|y-x\right\|
for
\varepsilon>0
, this is not the case for the generic variation
\xi
:
\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\geq W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)
and
\left\|{(\pi_{1},\varepsilon(\pi_{2}-\pi_{1}))}_{\#}{\gamma}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\geq\varepsilon W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)
. In light of this, to define a meaningful
tangent space
, we consider only the variations that for sufficiently small “scalings” (i.e., for sufficiently small
\bar{\varepsilon}
below) are optimal and, thus, describe geodesics in the Wasserstein space:
The
tangent space

{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is
the closure with respect to
W_{\bar{\mu}}
of

\displaystyle\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})\coloneqq\biggl\{\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\bar{\mu}}\,|

\displaystyle\exists\bar{\varepsilon}>0,\forall\varepsilon\in[0,\bar{\varepsilon}),{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},{(\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi})\biggr\},

(2.6)

a definition first introduced in
[
35
, Chapter 12]
.

Figure 2:
Wasserstein geometry.
On the left, we consider example variations for
{\color[rgb]{0.5586,0.4023,0.0742}\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(denoted by a
brown square
),
{\color[rgb]{0.7188,0.207,0.1758}\xi_{1}}
in
dotted red
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\xi_{2}}
in
solid blue
. They are scaled versions of each other:
{\color[rgb]{0.7188,0.207,0.1758}\xi_{1}}=2{\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\xi_{2}}
. The other two images show two possible sums (in
dotted green
) of the variations
{\color[rgb]{0.7188,0.207,0.1758}\xi_{1}}
(in
dotted red
) and
{\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\xi_{2}}
(in
solid blue
) at
{\color[rgb]{0.5586,0.4023,0.0742}\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
: Each sum results from a different coupling
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}({\color[rgb]{0.7188,0.207,0.1758}\xi_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\xi_{2}})
between
{\color[rgb]{0.7188,0.207,0.1758}\xi_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\xi_{2}}
(i.e., a different coupling between the arrows starting from the same square). The coupling resulting in the sum on the left is the one maximizing the inner product (cf. (
2.4
)) of the two variations (and, equivalently, the one attaining the minimum in the distance between the two variations; cf. (
2.3
)).

In
Propositions

A.7
and
A.9
, we discuss several properties of this “almost linear” structure of the space of variations.

Remark
.

The sum of
n
elements
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}
results from iteratively applying
Definition

2.1
3
, and we write
\xi_{1}\,+_{\alpha}\,\ldots\,+_{\alpha}\,\xi_{n}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}+\ldots+\pi_{n+1})}_{\#}{\alpha}
for some

{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})\coloneqq\left\{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n})\,|\,{\pi_{1,i+1}}_{\#}{\alpha}=\xi_{i},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\right\}}.

Comparison with the literature.

Most of the literature uses the tangent space

\overline{\left\{\nabla{\varphi}\,|\,\varphi\in\mathrm{Cyl}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\right\}}^{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\bar{\mu})}={\overline{\left\{\varepsilon(T-\mathrm{Id})\,|\,{(\mathrm{Id},T)}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},{T}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}),\varepsilon>0\right\}}^{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\bar{\mu})},}

(2.7)

where
\mathrm{Cyl}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
is the set of smooth cylindrical functions, which in finite spaces is homeomorphic to
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the space of compactly-supported, smooth functions; cf.
[
35
, Chapter 5]
. This (simplified) tangent space was
first introduced in
[
35
, Chapter 8]
(see, e.g.,
[
1
,
29
,
38
,
39
]
and
[
35
, Theorem 8.5.1]
for the proof of the equality in (
2.7
)) and can be embedded in (
2.6
) since for
\nabla{\varphi}
with
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the tangent vector
\xi={(\mathrm{Id}_{,}\nabla{\varphi})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
belongs to
{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In fact, (i) its first marginal is
\bar{\mu}
and (ii)
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi}={(\mathrm{Id},\mathrm{Id}+\varepsilon\nabla{\varphi})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
is, for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, a transport plan. The latter follows from
\mathrm{Id}+\varepsilon\nabla{\varphi}
being the gradient of a convex function and, thus, an optimal transport map
[
52
, Theorem 1.48]
.
While attractive for its simplicity (it is a vector space), the tangent space (
2.7
) limits the perturbations to transport maps. For absolutely continuous measures, this comes with no loss of generality in view of Brenier’s theorem
[
53
]
. However, for empirical probability measures (such as those arising in data-driven applications, where one has only access to a dataset consisting of a finite number of observations), the restriction to transport maps effectively limits the possible perturbations (e.g., a Dirac delta can only be transported to another Dirac delta by a transport map).
For these reasons, we instead adopt the tangent space introduced in
[
35
, Chapter 12]
.

2.4
Wasserstein subgradients

We now define the
regular
and
general
subgradients, along the lines of
[
46
, Definition 8.3]
and
[
35
, Section 10]
, for the Wasserstein space. The definition is inspired by the Euclidean setting, where
\bar{\upsilon}
is a subgradient of
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
at
\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
if
f(x)-f(\bar{x})\geq\left\langle\bar{\upsilon},x-\bar{x}\right\rangle+o\left(\left\|x-\bar{x}\right\|\right)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. That is, if
\bar{\upsilon}
provides a linear lower bound for
f
at
\bar{x}
.
General subgradients are then defined as the limits of regular subgradients.
In the Wasserstein space, we can proceed analogously. In particular, we use the (local) inner product (
2.4
) and replace the displacement “
x-\bar{x}
” with
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma}
, where
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)
is an optimal transport plan between
\bar{\mu}
and
\mu
(which reduces to
{(\mathrm{Id},T-\mathrm{Id})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
when
\gamma
is induced by a map
T
):

Definition 2.2

(Wasserstein subgradients)
.

Consider a functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and a probability measure
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}
. We say that:

1.

\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
is a
regular subgradient of
\mathcal{J}
at
\bar{\mu}
, written
\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
, if for all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and all
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}
, it holds that

\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\geq\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right).

Then,
\mathcal{J}
is regularly subdifferentiable at
\bar{\mu}
.

2.

\bar{\xi}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\bar{\mu}}

is a
(general) subgradient of
\mathcal{J}
at
\bar{\mu}
, written
\bar{\xi}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
, if there are sequences
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
with
\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})\to\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
with
\xi_{n}\to\bar{\xi}
.
Then,
\mathcal{J}
is subdifferentiable at
\bar{\mu}
.

We consider convergence in Wasserstein for the general subgradient since the first marginals may differ (we consider sequences
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
). For this reason, the general subgradient may not be in the tangent space, but it has finite second moment.
The regular subgradient, instead, is in the tangent space by definition.
Similarly, we can then define differentiability:

Definition 2.3

(Differentiable functional)
.

A functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
is
differentiable
at
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if

-\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})\cap\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\neq\emptyset.

Intuitively,
Definition

2.3
states that
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable if there is a regular subgradient that is also a “supergradient” in the classical sense; cf.
[
1
, Definition 2.7]
. Wasserstein subgradients enjoy similar properties to their Euclidean analogues:

Proposition 2.4

(Characterization of the (sub)gradients)
.

Consider a functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and a probability measure
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}
. Then,

1.

For all
\xi_{l}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and
\xi_{u}\in-\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})
, it holds
\xi_{l}=\xi_{u}
; in particular, either
\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
or
\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})
is empty, or
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at
\bar{\mu}
.

2.

If
G:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
is a lower bound on
\mathcal{J}
, i.e.,
G(\mu)\leq\mathcal{J}(\mu)
for all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
G(\bar{\mu})=\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
, then
\hat{\partial}G(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.

If
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at
\bar{\mu}
, the following statements hold:

3.

For all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in-\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})\cap\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
,
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}
. Thus, we call this unique element the gradient of
\mathcal{J}
at
\bar{\mu}
, written
\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})
.
In particular, it satisfies
\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})=\{\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})\}\subseteq\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.

4.

A tangent element
\xi\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
is the gradient of
\mathcal{J}
at
\bar{\mu}
if and only if for all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\xi^{\prime}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})

\displaystyle={\left\langle\xi,\xi^{\prime}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)}

(2.8)

\displaystyle={\underset{{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})}}{\min}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right).}

5.

The gradient is induced by a map: there exists a Borel

\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\bar{\mu})
so that

\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})={(\mathrm{Id},\phi)}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
. In particular, (1) for every
variation
\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\bar{\mu}}
, the set

\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}))
is
a singleton, so sums with
\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})
do not depend on
the choice of the coupling; (2)

\left\langle\tau\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}),\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\tau\left\langle\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}),\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}

for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and all variations
\xi
at
\bar{\mu}
; and
(3)
\mathcal{J}
is continuous in Wasserstein at
\bar{\mu}
.

In particular,
Proposition

2.4
3
allows us to talk about
the
gradient. The (general) subgradient might not be a singleton, even for a differentiable functional; see
[
46
, Section 8.B]
for an example in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proposition

2.4
4
, instead, is the analogue of
f(y)-f(x)=\nabla{f}(x)^{\top}(y-x)+o\left(\left\|x-y\right\|\right)
: Namely, gradients provide a “linear approximation” of
\mathcal{J}
from below, with errors that are small in terms of the Wasserstein distance. In contrast to (
2.4
), in (
2.8
) the maximization over the couplings coincides with the minimization over the same set up to a
o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)
term, an important property of differentiable functions.
Finally,
Proposition

2.4
5
shows that the gradient, whenever it exists, is always “deterministic” (i.e., induced by a transport map) even at measures that are not absolutely continuous (e.g., the empirical measures arising in data-driven applications). Perhaps surprisingly, this holds although our framework deliberately admits subgradients that are genuine transport plans rather than maps (indeed, as we discuss shortly, those of the squared Wasserstein distance are of this form): differentiability nonetheless forces the gradient onto the graph of a map, recovering “for free” the map-induced form imposed by definition in
[
54
,
1
]
.

Comparison with the literature.

Our definition of regular subgradient (
Definition

2.2
1
) coincides with the definition of extended Fréchet subdifferential, introduced in
[
35
, Definition 10.3.1]
and employed, for instance, in
[
29
]
.
It is a weaker notion of subdifferentiability compared to the ones in
[
54
,
1
]
, where subgradients are of the form
{(\mathrm{Id},\phi)}_{\#}{\mu}{}
for some Borel map
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
From an optimization perspective, these definitions are however not satisfactory since, as we will discuss shortly, the squared Wasserstein distance itself fails to be subdifferentiable at measures that are not absolutely continuous.
Inspired by classical variational analysis in Euclidean spaces
[
46
]
, we therefore introduce the definition of general subgradients (
Definition

2.2
2
).
In the case of variations (and so subdifferentials) induced by transport maps, a similar definition has appeared in
[
35
, Definition 11.1.5]
.
Besides the more restrictive variations,
[
35
, Definition 11.1.5]
also uses different notions of convergence and is formulated only for the so-called strong subdifferential
[
35
, Definition 10.3.1]
, and is thus insufficient for general optimality conditions.
To the best of our knowledge, our definition is therefore novel.

2.4.1
Why is this machinery needed?

We argue that our level of sophistication is required for at least two reasons.
First, already in Euclidean spaces, variational analysis is needed to deal with nondifferentiable and nonconvex settings; the (general) subgradient usually appears in the necessary conditions, while the horizon subgradient (which we shall discuss in
Section

2.5.2
) appears in the constraint qualification (e.g., see
[
46
, Theorem 8.15]
or
[
48
, Section 5.1]
). There is no reason to expect the situation to simplify in the more general space of probability measures.
Second, the squared Wasserstein distance itself fails to be regularly subdifferentiable.
The existing theory of gradient flows
[
35
,
1
]
bypasses this complexity by resorting to absolutely continuous measures. However, this introduces a substantial practical and theoretical limitation since it excludes, among others, many data-driven settings.
By contrast, we show below that the general subgradient is always nonempty.

The squared Wasserstein distance is not regularly subdifferentiable.

To start, we study the regular subdifferentiability properties of the squared Wasserstein distance:

Proposition 2.5

(Subgradients of the squared Wasserstein distance and optimal transport plans)
.

Consider the functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
,

\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}W_{2}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)^{2}
for some fixed
\hat{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, for any
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

1.

-\mathcal{J}
is regularly subdifferentiable at
\bar{\mu}
and all
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma}
are regular subgradients of
-\mathcal{J}
at
\bar{\mu}
, where
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
.

2.

If
\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
is a singleton and induced by an optimal transport map (e.g., when
\bar{\mu}
is absolutely continuous), then
\mathcal{J}
is regularly subdifferentiable, and thus differentiable, at
\bar{\mu}
.

3.

If
\xi\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
, then
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
.

4.

If
\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
is not a singleton, then
\mathcal{J}
is not regularly subdifferentiable at
\bar{\mu}
.

When the set of optimal transport plans consists of a unique transport plan induced by an optimal transport map
T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})={(\mathrm{Id},\mathrm{Id}-T)}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
, which is formally analogous to the gradient of
x\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\left\|x-\hat{x}\right\|^{2}
being
x-\hat{x}
in Euclidean spaces (with “
\mathrm{Id}
as
x
” and “
T
as
\hat{x}
”). However, and perhaps surprisingly,

Proposition

2.5
also indicates that the squared Wasserstein distance from a reference measure
\hat{\mu}
is not regularly subdifferentiable at all measures
\bar{\mu}
for which there exist multiple optimal transport plans between
\bar{\mu}
and
\hat{\mu}
.
Instead, it is regularly subdifferentiable (and thus differentiable) whenever there is a unique optimal transport plan between
\bar{\mu}
and
\hat{\mu}
and this transport plan is induced by an optimal transport map—a result first established, together with regular subdifferentiability of
-\mathcal{J}
, in
[
35
, Theorem 10.2.6]
.
If
\bar{\mu}
is absolutely continuous there is a unique optimal transport plan induced by an optimal transport map between
\bar{\mu}
and
\hat{\mu}
; cf.
[
53
]
. Thus, the squared Wasserstein distance is differentiable at absolutely continuous measures.
This result is sharp: Even when a unique optimal transport plan exists, the squared Wasserstein distance may fail to be regularly subdifferentiable, as we illustrate in the next example.

Example 2.6

(Non regular-subdifferentiability of the squared Wasserstein distance)
.

Let
\bar{\mu}\coloneqq\delta_{(0,0)}
(
blue
in the picture) and
\hat{\mu}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\delta_{(-1,0)}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{(1,0)}
(
red
in the picture).
Applying the definition of Wasserstein distance (cf. (
2.2
)),
\mathcal{J}({\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}})\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}W_{2}\left({\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}},{\color[rgb]{0.7188,0.207,0.1758}\hat{\mu}}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{2}
, and there is a unique optimal transport plan between
\bar{\mu}
and
\hat{\mu}
; in particular,
\Gamma_{o}({\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}},{\color[rgb]{0.7188,0.207,0.1758}\hat{\mu}})=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}}\times{\color[rgb]{0.7188,0.207,0.1758}\hat{\mu}}\}
.
We now show that
\mathcal{J}(\mu)=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}\left(\mu,{\color[rgb]{0.7188,0.207,0.1758}\hat{\mu}}\right)^{2}
is not regularly subdifferentiable at
{\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}}
.
By
Proposition

2.5
3
, it suffices to prove that

\xi\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{({\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}}\times{\color[rgb]{0.7188,0.207,0.1758}\hat{\mu}})}=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{((0,0),(-1,0))}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{((0,0),(1,0))}

is not a regular subgradient of
\mathcal{J}
.

Consider
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mu_{n}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\delta_{(-\frac{1}{n},-\frac{1}{n})}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{n})}}
. Then, there is a unique optimal transport plan

\Gamma_{o}({\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mu_{n}})=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}}\times{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mu_{n}}\}
, and

\mathcal{J}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mu_{n}})=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}\left({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mu_{n}},{\color[rgb]{0.7188,0.207,0.1758}\hat{\mu}}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{2}((1-\frac{1}{n})^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}})=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}
,
and

W_{2}\left({\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mu_{n}}\right)=(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+\frac{1}{n^{2}})^{1/2}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{n}
.
We now show that such a sequence contradicts
Definition

2.2
1
by proving that, along this “direction”,
\xi
does not lower bound
\mathcal{J}
. For this, we consider the variations
\xi_{n}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}({\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mu_{n}})}
. Using the expressions of
{\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mu_{n}}
, we conclude that the only variation between
\bar{\mu}
and
\mu_{n}
is

\xi_{n}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{({\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}}\times{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mu_{n}})}=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{((0,0),(-\frac{1}{n},-\frac{1}{n}))}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{((0,0),(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{n}))}.

With this, we compute the inner product between
\xi
and
\xi_{n}
:

{\left\langle\xi,\xi_{n}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi_{n})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left\langle\upsilon,\upsilon_{n}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(x,\upsilon,\upsilon_{n}).}

The coupling
\alpha
is fully parametrized by a single coefficient
\tau\in[0,1]
, and the max reduces to a line search:

\displaystyle\left\langle\xi,\xi_{n}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\max_{\tau\in[0,1]}\frac{\tau}{2}

\displaystyle\left(\left\langle(1,0),\left(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{n}\right)\right\rangle+\left\langle(-1,0),\left(-\frac{1}{n},-\frac{1}{n}\right)\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle{+\frac{(1-\tau)}{2}\left(\left\langle(1,0),\left(-\frac{1}{n},-\frac{1}{n}\right)\right\rangle+\left\langle(-1,0),\left(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{n}\right)\right\rangle\right)=\frac{1}{n}.}

Thus,

{\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{J}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mu_{n}})-\mathcal{J}({\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}})-\left\langle\xi,\xi_{n}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mu_{n}}\right)}=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{-\frac{2}{n}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}}{\frac{\sqrt{2}}{n}}=-\sqrt{2}<0}

and so
\mathcal{J}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mu_{n}})-\mathcal{J}({\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}})<\left\langle\xi,\xi_{n}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+o\left(W_{2}\left({\color[rgb]{0.1289,0.3594,0.6875}\bar{\mu}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mu_{n}}\right)\right)
.
That is,
\mathcal{J}
is not regularly subdifferentiable at
\bar{\mu}

and, in particular, uniqueness of the optimal transport plan does not imply regular subdifferentiability.

The absence of regular subgradients prompts us to study the general subgradient of the squared Wasserstein distance. We will do so in the next section.

The (general) subgradient of the squared Wasserstein distance.

The subdifferentiability of the squared Wasserstein distance at absolutely continuous probability measures (cf.
Proposition

2.5
2
) fuels the hope that the general subgradient can be studied via a mollification argument, i.e., by approximating each probability measure via absolutely continuous ones. We illustrate the intuition by approximating a Dirac delta at 0 with Gaussian probability measures with vanishing variance:

Example 2.7

(Subgradients via Gaussian mollification)
.

Let
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
,
\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}W_{2}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)^{2}
and fix

\bar{\mu}=\delta_{0}
and

\hat{\mu}=\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{d}\right)
being the Gaussian centered in
0
with covariance matrix
I_{d}
, the identity matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.
In this case, there is a unique transport plan
\bar{\mu}\times\hat{\mu}
from
\bar{\mu}
to
\hat{\mu}
, but it is not induced by a transport map (indeed, the mass at 0 needs to be split, see the picture below for an intuition).
Here, we show that
\xi\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{(\bar{\mu}\times\hat{\mu})}
belongs to the general subgradient
\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.
To do so, we approximate
\delta_{0}
with Gaussians with vanishing variance.
Specifically, consider the sequence
\mu_{n}=\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{1}{n^{2}}I_{d}\right)
so that
W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{n}\right)=\frac{\sqrt{d}}{n}\searrow 0
and
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
and
\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})\to\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.
Since both
\hat{\mu}
and
\mu_{n}
are Gaussians, the set of optimal transport plans is a singleton
\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{n},\hat{\mu})=\{{(\mathrm{Id},T_{n})}_{\#}{\mu_{n}}\}
, and the unique optimal transport plan is induced by the optimal transport map
T_{n}(x)=nx
.
By
Proposition

2.5
,
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at
\mu_{n}
and, in particular,

\xi_{n}\coloneqq{(\mathrm{Id},\mathrm{Id}-T_{n})}_{\#}{\mu_{n}}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n}).

We now show that
\xi_{n}\to\xi
and so
\xi
is a (general) subgradient of
\mathcal{J}
at
\bar{\mu}
. For this, we observe that

\gamma_{\xi}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-T_{n}\circ\pi_{2},\pi_{2},\pi_{2}-T_{n}\circ\pi_{2})}_{\#}{(\bar{\mu}\times\mu_{n})}
is a (possibly suboptimal) transport plan between
\xi
and
\xi_{n}
.
Indeed, using
\bar{\mu}\times\hat{\mu}=\bar{\mu}\times({T_{n}}_{\#}{\mu_{n}})={(\pi_{1},T_{n}\circ\pi_{2})}_{\#}{(\bar{\mu}\times\mu_{n})}
, we can compute the first marginal as
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma_{\xi}}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-T_{n}\circ\pi_{2})}_{\#}{(\bar{\mu}\times\mu_{n})}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{{(\pi_{1},T_{n}\circ\pi_{2})}_{\#}{(\bar{\mu}\times\mu_{n})}}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{(\bar{\mu}\times\hat{\mu})}=\xi
and the second marginal as

{(\pi_{3},\pi_{4})}_{\#}{\gamma_{\xi}}={(\pi_{2},\pi_{2}-T_{n}\circ\pi_{2})}_{\#}{(\bar{\mu}\times\mu_{n})}={(\mathrm{Id},\mathrm{Id}-T_{n})}_{\#}{({\pi_{2}}_{\#}{(\bar{\mu}\times\mu_{n})})}={(\mathrm{Id},\mathrm{Id}-T_{n})}_{\#}{\mu_{n}}=\xi_{n}
.
Given this transport plan, we can bound the Wasserstein distance between
\xi
and
\xi_{n}
as

\displaystyle W_{2}\left(\xi,\xi_{n}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{1}-x_{2}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|\upsilon_{1}-\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma_{\xi}(x_{1},\upsilon_{1},x_{2},\upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\bar{x}-x_{n}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|(\bar{x}-T_{n}(x_{n}))-(x_{n}-T_{n}(x_{n}))\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}(\bar{\mu}\times\mu_{n})(\bar{x},x_{n})

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{n}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}(x_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{2d}{n^{2}},

where we used that
\bar{\mu}=\delta_{0}
and so
\bar{x}=0
for
\bar{\mu}
-almost all
\bar{x}
.
This implies that
W_{2}\left(\xi,\xi_{n}\right)\searrow 0
and so
\xi_{n}\to\xi
. Thus,
\xi\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and the squared Wasserstein distance is subdifferentiable at
\bar{\mu}
.

Since
\mathcal{P}_{2,\mathrm{abs}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is dense in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

[
42
, Theorem 2.2.7]
, we can always construct a sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2,\mathrm{abs}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
, compute
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
, which exists by absolute continuity of
\mu_{n}
, and study the limit of
\xi_{n}
. One way to construct such
\mu_{n}
is via convolution with a Gaussian kernel; see
[
35
, Lemma 7.1.10]
.
Overall, this procedure yields a sufficient characterization (for the sake of necessary optimality conditions) of the (general) subgradient of the squared Wasserstein distance:

Proposition 2.8

(General subgradient of the squared Wasserstein distance)
.

For
\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
,
\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}W_{2}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)^{2}
, we have

\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\neq\emptyset

and

\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})}
.

In particular,
Proposition

2.8
establishes subdifferentiability of the squared Wasserstein distance at
all
probability measures
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Why not mollify the optimization problem?

General subgradients are defined via a mollification argument: A variation is a general subgradient if there are regular subgradients at nearby points that converge to it in Wasserstein.
As established in
[
46
,
49
]
for Euclidean spaces and in the remainder of the paper for the Wasserstein space, general subgradients lead to a coherent framework for calculus and optimization.
However, one might wonder why not
mollify
the optimization problem itself, thereby removing the nonsmoothness, and then solve the resulting smooth problem in the hope that its solutions converge to those of the original nonsmooth problem.
Unfortunately, as we illustrate in the next example, this approach generally fails even in the Euclidean space and, thus, cannot be expected to work in the Wasserstein space either.

Example 2.9

(Non-convergence of mollified optimization problems)
.

Consider the optimization problem

\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}}x\quad\text{subject to}\quad\sqrt{\absolutevalue{x}}-x\leq 0.

x
y
x
\sqrt{\absolutevalue{x}}
\mathcal{C}

The constraint
\sqrt{\absolutevalue{x}}-x\leq 0
implies that only nonnegative
x
are feasible and, thus, the global minimizer is trivially
x=0
. The function
f(x)=x
is differentiable everywhere, but the constraint
0\geq g(x)=\sqrt{\absolutevalue{x}}-x
is not differentiable at
x=0
, so smooth first-order optimality conditions are not directly applicable. We now consider two approaches. In the first (
A1
) we mollify
g(x)
, whereas in the second (
A2
) we take the variational approach
[
46
]
.

A1
.

For
\varepsilon>0
, consider a Gaussian mollifier
\rho_{\varepsilon}
. Define the mollified constraint

0\geq g_{\varepsilon}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(x-y)\rho_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(y)\rho_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y.

Then, for all
\varepsilon>0
, we have

g_{\varepsilon}(0)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\sqrt{\absolutevalue{y}}-y)\rho_{\varepsilon}(-y)\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sqrt{\absolutevalue{y}}\rho_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}y>0.

Thus,
x=0
is not a feasible solution for any mollified problem. Moreover, we have

g_{\varepsilon}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(x-y)\rho_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sqrt{\absolutevalue{x - y}}\rho_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}y-x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sqrt{\absolutevalue{y}}\rho_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}y+g(x).

Moreover,
\absolutevalue{g_\varepsilon(x)-g(x)}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sqrt{\absolutevalue{y}}\rho_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}y\eqqcolon c_{\varepsilon}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
(since
\absolutevalue{\sqrt{\abs{x-y}}-\sqrt{\abs{x}}}\leq\sqrt{\absolutevalue{y}}
), and
g_{\varepsilon}>0
on
(-\infty,\tfrac{1}{4})
: for
x\leq 0
,
g_{\varepsilon}(x)\geq-x+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sqrt{\absolutevalue{x-y}}\rho_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}y>0
, whereas for
x\in(0,\tfrac{1}{4})
, averaging
y
with
-y
and using
\sqrt{\absolutevalue{x-y}}+\sqrt{\absolutevalue{x+y}}\geq\sqrt{2\max\{x,\absolutevalue{y}\}}\geq\sqrt{x}
gives
g_{\varepsilon}(x)\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{x}-x>0
. Hence, the feasible set of the mollified problem is contained in
[\tfrac{1}{4},+\infty)\cap\{x\in\mathbb{R}\,|\,g(x)\leq c_{\varepsilon}\}
, and the optimizer
x^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}
satisfies
x^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}\to 1
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. That is, the solutions of the mollified problems do not converge to the minimizer of the original problem. In particular, the set of candidate solutions we find with this approach does not include the global minimizer.

A2
.

The regular and general subgradients of
f
are
\hat{\partial}f(0)=\partial f(0)=\{1\}
. For the constraint set
\mathcal{C}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}\,|\,g(x)\leq 0\}
, we have
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(0)=\mathbb{R}
(
0
is an isolated feasible point),
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(1)=(-\infty,0]
, and
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(x)=\{0\}
for
x>1
. The first-order optimality conditions on a candidate solution
x^{\ast}
require that
0\in\partial f(x^{\ast})+\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(x^{\ast})
. Thus, we obtain both the candidate solutions
\{0,1\}
. Direct inspection, then, reveals the (correct) global optimum
x^{\ast}=0
. In particular, the variational approach enables us to find all the local minimizers for the original problem.

2.4.2
Examples of subgradients

Next, we study the subgradients of several functionals of interest.
We start with general optimal transport discrepancies, defined in (
2.1
), which arise, e.g., in uncertainty propagation in dynamical systems
[
55
,
27
]
and
DRO

[
11
,
3
]
. With the following result, we generalize
[
1
, Proposition 2.17]
.

Proposition 2.10

(Optimal transport discrepancy)
.

Let
\hat{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and consider
c:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
twice continuously differentiable with bounded Hessian on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and satisfying the twist condition, i.e.,
y\mapsto\nabla_{x}{c}(x,y)
is injective for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Consider the functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
,

\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq W_{c}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)
as defined in (
2.1
).
Then,

1.

\mathcal{J}
is proper, nonnegative, and continuous.

2.

At any
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\mathcal{J}
is subdifferentiable with

\emptyset\neq\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq{(\pi_{1},\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{c}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})}
, where
\Gamma_{c}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
is the set of transport plans between
\bar{\mu}
and
\hat{\mu}
that yield the minimum in the definition of
W_{c}\left(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu}\right)
.

3.

If
\Gamma_{c}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
is a singleton and induced by an optimal transport map (e.g.,
\bar{\mu}
is absolutely continuous), then

\mathcal{J}
is differentiable with

\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})={(\pi_{1},\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\gamma},
where

\gamma
is the unique element in
\Gamma_{c}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
.

As already observed for the Wasserstein distance, optimal transport discrepancies are subdifferentiable (but not regularly subdifferentiable), and their subgradient can be written in terms of the gradient of the transportation cost and the set of optimal transport plans.
As a notable special case, consider
\bar{\mu}=\delta_{\bar{x}}
and
\hat{\mu}=\delta_{\hat{x}}
. Then,
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})=W_{c}\left(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu}\right)=c(\bar{x},\hat{x})
and
\Gamma_{c}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})=\{\delta_{(\bar{x},\hat{x})}\}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable with gradient
\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})={(\pi_{1},\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\delta_{(\bar{x},\hat{x})}}=\delta_{\bar{x}}\times\delta_{\nabla_{x}{c}(\bar{x},\hat{x})}
, which is formally analogous to the standard Euclidean gradient of
x\mapsto c(x,\hat{x})
.
We now exemplify our results in the case of strictly convex transportation costs:

Example 2.11

(Strictly convex transportation cost)
.

Consider
c(x,y)=h(x-y)
for
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
strictly convex and twice continuously differentiable with bounded Hessian, with
h(0)=0
and
\nabla{h}(0)=0
(so that
c\geq 0
). Then
\nabla_{x}{c}(x,y)=\nabla{h}(x-y)
and, by strict convexity of
h
,
y\mapsto\nabla_{x}{c}(x,y)
is injective. Thus,
c
satisfies the assumptions of
Proposition

2.10
and the subgradient of

\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq W_{c}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)

at
\bar{\mu}
is

\emptyset\neq\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq{(\pi_{1},\nabla{h}\circ(\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}))}_{\#}{\Gamma_{c}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable whenever
\Gamma_{c}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
is a singleton and induced by an optimal transport map and, in particular, at all absolutely continuous measures.

Next, we recall and extend, by characterizing their general subdifferential, the existing subdifferentiability results for expected values in
[
35
,
1
]
:

Proposition 2.12

(Expected value)
.

Let
V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to(-\infty,+\infty]
be a proper, lower semicontinuous, and
\lambda
-convex (i.e.,
x\mapsto V(x)-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\|x\right\|^{2}
is convex, with
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
) function.
Consider the functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
,

\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right].

Then,

1.

\mathcal{J}
is proper and lower semicontinuous.

2.

At every
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at which
\mathcal{J}
is finite,
\xi\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
(resp.
\xi\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
)
if and only if for all
(x,y)\in\supp{(\xi)}
,
y\in\hat{\partial}V(x)
(resp.
y\in\partial V(x)
).

3.

If
V
is differentiable and
V(x)\leq C(1+\left\|x\right\|^{2})
for some
C>0
,
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at any
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})={(\mathrm{Id},\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}.

Proposition

2.12
characterizes the
general
subdifferential
\partial\mathcal{J}
, not only the regular one; as subgradients are transport plans rather than maps, this holds at every
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, including empirical (non-absolutely continuous) measures.
For differentiability, it asks only that
V
be differentiable,
\lambda
-convex, and bounded above by a quadratic, relaxing
[
1
, Proposition 2.20]
, which assumes
V\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with bounded Hessian—a special case, as any such
V
is
\lambda
-convex with
\absolutevalue{V(x)}\leq A+B\left\|x\right\|^{2}
.
Thus, the corresponding functional
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable and, in particular,
(x,y)\in\supp(\nabla{\mathcal{J}})
if and only if
y=\nabla{V}(x)
. If, instead,
V
is not bounded below by a quadratic function, then
\mathcal{J}
is not proper and might evaluate to
-\infty
(e.g.,
V(x)=-e^{x^{2}}
and
\mu=\mathcal{N}\left(0,1\right)
). Similarly, if
V
grows more than quadratically, then
\mathcal{J}
will attain
+\infty
(e.g.,
V(x)=e^{x^{2}}
and
\mu=\mathcal{N}\left(0,1\right)
) and cannot be differentiable there.

Example 2.13

(Expected values of linear functions)
.

Consider the same functional
\mathcal{J}
as in
Section

1
, namely
\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle\right]
and
V(x)\coloneqq\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle
in
Proposition

2.12
. Since
V
is a linear function, it satisfies all the assumptions of
Proposition

2.12
, and in particular
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable with
\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu)={(\mathrm{Id},\theta)}_{\#}{\mu}
; moreover, its subdifferential is the singleton
\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu)=\{\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu)\}
.

Finally, we extend the existing subdifferentiability results for interaction-type functionals
[
35
,
1
]
, which model the energy associated with the interaction between particles in a distribution; e.g., see
[
52
]
for an introduction,
[
56
]
for an example in physics, and
[
57
, Section 3.3]
for one in economics.

Proposition 2.14

(Interaction energy)
.

Let
U:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a twice continuously differentiable
function with bounded Hessian
and consider the functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
,

\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{(x,y)\sim\mu\times\mu}\left[U(x-y)\right].

Then,

1.

\mathcal{J}
is proper and continuous.

2.

\mathcal{J}
is differentiable and its gradient at

\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is

\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})={(\mathrm{Id},T_{\bar{\mu}})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}},\quad\text{where}\quad{T_{\bar{\mu}}(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigl(\nabla{U}(x-y)-\nabla{U}(y-x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(y).}

3.

If, in addition,
U
is even, then
\nabla{U}
is odd and

{T_{\bar{\mu}}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla{U}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(y)=(\nabla{U}\ast\bar{\mu})(x),}

where
\ast
denotes the
convolution
operation. Consequently,

{\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})={(\mathrm{Id},(\nabla{U}\ast\bar{\mu}))}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}.}

We exemplify the result in the case of quadratic functions:

Example 2.15

(Quadratic interaction energies)
.

Consider
U(z)=\frac{1}{2}\left\langle Qz,z\right\rangle
for symmetric
2
2

2

Otherwise, the term
\frac{1}{2}(Q+Q^{\top})
appears in the expression of the subgradient.

Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. Then,
U
is twice continuously differentiable with
\nabla{U}(z)=Qz
and constant (and, thus, bounded) Hessian
Q
. In virtue of
Proposition

2.14
, the functional
\mathcal{J}(\mu)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{(x,y)\sim\mu\times\mu}\left[U(x-y)\right]
is differentiable with

\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu)={(\mathrm{Id},Q(\mathrm{Id}-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[x\right]))}_{\#}{\mu}.

2.4.3
Subdifferential calculus

We now show some useful calculus rules in the Wasserstein space. More can be studied, and we expect this to be a source of interesting future work.

Proposition 2.16

(Subdifferential calculus)
.

Consider the proper functionals
\mathcal{J}_{1},\mathcal{J}_{2}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
,
g:\bar{\mathbb{R}}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
with
g(+\infty)=+\infty
, and
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
so that
\mathcal{J}_{i}(\bar{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}
,
g
is continuously differentiable at
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}
with
g(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}))\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathcal{J}_{1}
continuous in Wasserstein at
\bar{\mu}
. Then, the following rules hold:

1.

Sum rule:

\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})\supseteq\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})+\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})\coloneqq\left\{\xi_{1}\,+_{\alpha}\,\xi_{2}\,|\,\xi_{i}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{i}(\bar{\mu}),\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\right\}.

In particular, if
\mathcal{J}_{2}
is differentiable on a Wasserstein
neighborhood of
\bar{\mu}
with
\mu\mapsto\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\mu)

continuous at
\bar{\mu}
(convergence in Wasserstein in the argument and in the value), then

\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})=\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})+\{\nabla{\mathcal{J}}_{2}(\bar{\mu})\}

and, thus,

\partial(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})=\partial\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})+\left\{\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})\right\}.

2.

Chain rule: If
g^{\prime}(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}))>0
, then

\hat{\partial}(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})=g^{\prime}(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})){\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})}\coloneqq\{g^{\prime}(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}))\xi\,|\,\xi\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})\}
and, if
in addition
\mathcal{J}_{1}
is continuous in Wasserstein on a neighborhood of

\bar{\mu}
,

\partial(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})=g^{\prime}(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})){\partial\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})}.

3.

Chain rule (the differentiable case): If
\mathcal{J}_{1}
is differentiable at
\bar{\mu}
, then
g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1}
is differentiable at
\bar{\mu}
and

\nabla{(}g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})=g^{\prime}(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}))\nabla{\mathcal{J}}_{1}(\bar{\mu}).

This result generalizes
[
1
, Propositions 2.15 and 2.16]
to our more general subgradients, while relaxing some of the assumptions.
With
g_{i}(x)=\rho_{i}x
for
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
Proposition

2.16
readily yields the subgradients of the linear combination of functionals:

Corollary 2.17

(Linear combination rule)
.

Consider the proper functionals
\mathcal{J}_{1},\mathcal{J}_{2}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
,
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\geq 0
, and
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then,

\hat{\partial}(\rho_{1}\mathcal{J}_{1}+\rho_{2}\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})\supseteq\rho_{1}\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})+\rho_{2}\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{2}(\bar{\mu}).

If additionally
\mathcal{J}_{2}
is differentiable at
\bar{\mu}
,

\hat{\partial}(\rho_{1}\mathcal{J}_{1}+\rho_{2}\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})=\rho_{1}\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})+\rho_{2}\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})
and, if
\mathcal{J}_{2}
is
differentiable on a Wasserstein neighborhood of
\bar{\mu}
with

\mu\mapsto\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\mu)
continuous at
\bar{\mu}
(convergence in Wasserstein in the argument and in the value),

\partial(\rho_{1}\mathcal{J}_{1}+\rho_{2}\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})=\rho_{1}\partial\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})+\rho_{2}\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu}).

Next, we deploy
Proposition

2.16
to study the variance via
Proposition

2.12
:

Corollary 2.18

(Variance)
.

Consider the functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
,

\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq\mathrm{Var}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]
, where
V\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is such that both
V
and
V^{2}
have uniformly bounded Hessians (i.e., there exists
M>0
so that
\left\|\nabla^{2}{V}(x)\right\|\leq M
and
\left\|\nabla^{2}{(V^{2})}(x)\right\|\leq M
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
).
Then,
\mathcal{J}
is real-valued and, at every
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, differentiable, with gradient

{\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})={(\mathrm{Id},2(V-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\bar{\mu}}\left[V(x)\right])\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}.}

2.5
Variational geometry

In this section, we study the geometry of the constraint set
\mathcal{C}
, which we will combine in
Section

3
with the subdifferentiability of the objective function to derive our necessary optimality conditions. Similarly to Euclidean settings, all the necessary information is encoded in tangent and normal cones (see
Figure

3
for an intuition in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
). We start with defining the tangent cone:

Definition 2.19

(Tangent cone)
.

The
tangent cone
of
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}
, denoted by
{\tangent_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})}
, is the set of all the
\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
such that for some
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
,
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
,
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
,
\xi_{n}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu_{n})}
and
\tau_{n}\searrow 0
, one has

W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\frac{1}{\tau_{n}}\xi_{n},\bar{\xi}\right)\to 0
.

We highlight the formal similarity with the tangent cone in Euclidean spaces, defined by all the
\bar{\upsilon}
for which there is
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{C}
,
x_{n}\to\bar{x}
, and
\tau_{n}\searrow 0
so that
\frac{1}{\tau_{n}}(x_{n}-\bar{x})\to\bar{\upsilon}
. In particular, if
\mu_{n}=\delta_{x_{n}}
and
\bar{\mu}=\delta_{\bar{x}}
, then
\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu_{n})=\{\delta_{(\bar{x},x_{n})}\}
and
\xi_{n}=\delta_{(\bar{x},x_{n}-\bar{x})}
. Namely, the definitions in the Wasserstein space and in the Euclidean space are consistent.
Intuitively, the tangent cone is a restriction of the tangent space, and it describes the variations admissible within the set
\mathcal{C}
.
Dual to tangent cones are the normal cones:

Definition 2.20

(Normal cone)
.

The
regular normal cone
of
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}
, denoted by
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
,
contains all the

\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
such that for all
\mu\in\mathcal{C}
, one has

\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq o\left(\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\right)

for
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}
.
The
normal cone
of
\mathcal{C}
at
\bar{\mu}
, denoted by
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
, is the limit of regular normals; i.e.,
\bar{\xi}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\bar{\mu}}
belongs to
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
if there exist

(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{C}
,

\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
,

\xi_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n})

so that

\xi_{n}\to\bar{\xi}
.

Figure 3:
Variational Geometry in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. On the left, the arrows depict some examples of regular normals (e.g., at the cusp). The interior points have a trivial normal cone, indicated by a single blue dot. At the same location
x\in\mathcal{C}
they may have different scales and directions. There also may not be any regular normal. In the middle, the
solid blue arrows
depict some examples of normals. They are the limit points of sequences of regular normals. The sequence is colored with increasing opacity as the regular normals approach the general normal. On the right, in
dotted red
there are sequences
(x_{n}-x)/\tau_{n}\to w\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{C}}(x)}
, with the tangent vector
w
being depicted in
solid red
. The inner product between tangent vectors and regular normals is nonpositive, whereas the same might not hold with general normals.

Similarly to the definition of subgradient (cf.
Definition

2.2
), for general normals we consider convergence in Wasserstein of regular normals. Thus, general normals may not be in the tangent space, and need not be. Analogously to the tangent cone, if we specify the definition of normal cone to Dirac deltas, we recover the notion of normal cone in Euclidean spaces; see also
Proposition

2.22
.

Another similarity to Euclidean spaces (cf.
Figure

3
) is the duality between normal and tangent cones:

Proposition 2.21

(Normal and tangent cones relationships)
.

At any
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the sets
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
and
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
are nonempty closed cones, with respect
to
