W_{\bar{\mu}}
and in Wasserstein, respectively. Additionally, if

\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})

then

\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq 0

for all

\xi\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})}.

As a first example, we study the normal cones of trivial constraint sets:

Proposition 2.22

(Trivial normal cones)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}
. Then,

1.

if
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})=\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})=\normal_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})=\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
;

2.

if
\bar{\mu}
is in the interior (with respect to the Wasserstein distance) of
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
\bar{\mu}\in\mathrm{int}_{W_{2}}{\mathcal{C}}
, then

\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})=\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})=\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
;

3.

for
\Xi\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, if
\mathcal{C}=\{\delta_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,|\,x\in\Xi\}
, then at any
\bar{\mu}=\delta_{\bar{x}}\in\mathcal{C}
we have

\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})=\{\delta_{\bar{x}}\times\nu\,|\,\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\nu}\left[x\right]\in\hat{\normal}_{\Xi}(\bar{x})\}
and

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})=\{\delta_{\bar{x}}\times\nu\,|\,\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\nu}\left[x\right]\in\normal_{\Xi}(\bar{x})\}
,
where
\hat{\normal}_{\Xi}(\bar{x})
and
\normal_{\Xi}(\bar{x})
are the regular and general normal cones in the Euclidean space, respectively.

In particular, analogously to the Euclidean setting, the normal cone at
\bar{\mu}
trivializes if the feasible set covers the whole space (
Proposition

2.22
1
) or if
\bar{\mu}
lies in its interior (
Proposition

2.22
2
); cf.
Figure

3
. Moreover, when we restrict ourselves to the space of Dirac deltas, the normal cone is consistent with its Euclidean counterpart (
Proposition

2.22
3
); namely, a “tangent vector”
\nu
lies in the normal cone if and only if its “average” tangent vector lies in the Euclidean normal cone.
We will study normal cones of level sets of functionals in
Section

2.5.3
, after having introduced horizon subgradients.

2.5.1
Technical concepts

We now introduce
strong
counterparts of the subgradient,
the normal cone, and the differential, together with a compactness property of
constraint sets. These technical concepts enter (i) the normal-cone characterizations
of
Section

2.5.3
, (ii) the extremal principle and the intersection
rule of
Appendix

D
, and (iii) the statement and proof of our
necessary optimality conditions in
Section

3
. The strong notions are
obtained by requiring the defining inequalities to hold against
all

transport plans, rather than only the optimal ones:

Definition 2.23

(Strong subgradient)
.

Consider a functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and a probability measure
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}
. We say that:

1.

\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
is a
strong regular subgradient of
\mathcal{J}
at
\bar{\mu}
, written
\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}^{s}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
, if for all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and all
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)}
, it holds that

\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\geq\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+o\left(\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\right).

2.

\bar{\xi}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\bar{\mu}}
is a
strong (general) subgradient of
\mathcal{J}
at
\bar{\mu}
, written
\bar{\xi}\in\partial^{s}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
, if there are sequences
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
with
\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})\to\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\partial}^{s}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
with
\xi_{n}\to\bar{\xi}
.

Definition

2.23
differs
from
Definition

2.2
only in that the
inequality is required for
all
transport plans

\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)
, and not merely the optimal ones

\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)
, with the error measured by

\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}
(which coincides with

W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)
on optimal transport plans).
Analogously, we strengthen the notion of normal cone:

Definition 2.24

(Strong normal cone)
.

The
strong regular normal cone
of
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}
, denoted by
\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
, contains all the
\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
such that for all
\mu\in\mathcal{C}
, one has
\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq o\left(\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\right)
for
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)}
.
The
strong normal cone
of
\mathcal{C}
at
\bar{\mu}
, denoted by
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})
, is the limit of strong regular normals; i.e.,
\bar{\xi}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\bar{\mu}}
belongs to
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})
if there exist
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
,
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
, and
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n})
so that
\xi_{n}\to\bar{\xi}
.

Since membership is now tested against more variations, the
strong notions are more demanding, and the resulting sets are smaller than
their regular counterparts:

Lemma 2.25

(Relationship between strong and regular subgradients and normal cones)
.

Let
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

1.

For every functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
with
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}
, it holds that
\hat{\partial}^{s}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and, consequently,
\partial^{s}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.

2.

For every set
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}
, it holds that
\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
and, consequently,
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
.

We strengthen differentiability accordingly:

Definition 2.26

(Strongly differentiable functional)
.

A functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
is
strongly differentiable
at
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if

-\hat{\partial}^{s}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})\cap\hat{\partial}^{s}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\neq\emptyset.

By
Lemma

2.25
1
,
applied to
\mathcal{J}
and to
-\mathcal{J}
, a strongly differentiable
functional is differentiable. Hence

Proposition

2.4
3

applies, and every
\xi\in-\hat{\partial}^{s}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})\cap\hat{\partial}^{s}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
satisfies

\xi=\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})
; we call
this element the
strong gradient

\nabla^{s}{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})

(so
\nabla^{s}{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})=\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})
).

A few comments are in order. First, the distinction between
the strong and the standard notions is specific to the Wasserstein space:
There is exactly one way to couple a Dirac delta
\delta_{\bar{x}}
with any
probability measure (namely, their product), so at Dirac deltas the strong
regular subgradient and the strong regular normal cone coincide with their
counterparts in

Definitions

2.2
and
2.20
.
Accordingly, no analogous distinction arises in Euclidean spaces. Second, the
interplay between the two families is dictated by the direction of our
results. Our optimality conditions are
necessary
: They confine the
candidate minimizers to sets built from subgradients and normal cones (cf.

Theorem

3.2
), and they remain valid
if any of these sets is replaced by a superset—the conditions merely “get
bigger”, yielding more candidates but never missing a minimizer. Thus,
whenever an exact characterization of a normal cone is out of reach, an
upper bound suffices for the purpose of necessary conditions, as in

Section

2.5.3
. Conversely, since smaller sets make a necessary
condition sharper, we state the conclusion of

Theorem

3.2
with the
strong

normal cone; the version with the normal cone follows from

Lemma

2.25
2
.
Assumptions, by contrast, become
weaker
when imposed on the
smaller, strong sets: A requirement on all strong regular normals is easier
to satisfy than the same requirement on all regular normals, and the
corresponding results apply more broadly. We exploit this asymmetry three
times: in the constraint qualifications of the intersection rule
(
Appendix

D
) and of our first-order optimality conditions
(
Theorem

3.2
), which feature the
strong normal cones, and in the compactness property below, which is imposed
only along strong regular normals.

Finally, to prove our first-order optimality conditions in
Theorem

3.2
, we will require a technical property for the constraint set:

Definition 2.27

(Sequential normal compactness)
.

A set
\mathcal{C}
is
sequentially normally compact (SNC)
at
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}
if for any sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
,
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
and
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n})
so that
\xi_{n}\rightharpoonup\xi{}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have
\xi_{n}\to\xi
.

Informally,
Definition

2.27
asks that, along
sequences in
\mathcal{C}
, narrow convergence of strong regular normals
upgrades to convergence in Wasserstein. In finite dimensions, where weak and
strong convergence coincide, every set enjoys the analogous property, and no
such condition appears in Euclidean variational
analysis
[
46
,
49
]
; in
infinite dimensions, it is a standard regularity
requirement
[
47
]
. The same is true in the
Wasserstein space, where narrow convergence does not imply convergence in
Wasserstein.
In line with the discussion above, imposing
this property only along the
strong
regular normals yields a weaker
assumption and, in turn, more widely applicable optimality conditions. We
verify the
SNC
property for closed Wasserstein balls in

Proposition

2.36
.

2.5.2
Variational geometry and epigraphs

In
Section

3
, we provide the first-order optimality conditions for general, constrained, optimization problems and present differentiable functionals as a special case.
From a technical standpoint, however, we
first
establish optimality conditions for differentiable functions, and
then
study the nondifferentiable case via an epigraphical argument. This will yield a “constraint qualification”, which is typical in Euclidean spaces and we find in the Wasserstein space as well.
More formally, recall that the epigraph of a proper functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
is the set

\widetilde{\mathrm{epi}}\mathcal{J}\coloneqq\left\{(\mu,\beta)\,|\,\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathcal{J}(\mu)\leq\beta\right\}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}
.
Then, we immediately observe that

\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{J}(\mu)=\inf_{(\mu,\beta)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{epi}}(\mathcal{J})\cap(\mathcal{C}\times\mathbb{R})}\beta=\inf_{(\mu,\beta)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{epi}}(\mathcal{J})\cap(\mathcal{C}\times\mathbb{R})}\tilde{l}(\mu,\beta)
,
with
\tilde{l}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
being the linear function
\tilde{l}(\mu,\beta)=\beta
. Being linear,
\tilde{l}
is in principle easy to optimize.

Unfortunately, this argument does not directly carry over to the Wasserstein space: While on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
the epigraph is a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
, on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it is a subset of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}
and not
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
.
Thus, one has to study the differential structure and variational geometry of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}
.
To avoid this burden, we take a different angle. Since each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be “embedded” in the space of probability measures as the Dirac delta
\delta_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
we define the epigraph as

\epi\mathcal{J}\coloneqq\left\{\mu\times\delta_{\beta}\,|\,\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathcal{J}(\mu)\leq\beta\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}).

In particular, contrary to
\widetilde{\mathrm{epi}}\mathcal{J}
,
\epi\mathcal{J}
is now a subset of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
.
Then, the optimization of
\mathcal{J}
over
\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to the optimization of
l:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})\to\mathbb{R}
,
l(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\mathbb{E}^{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\boldsymbol{\mu}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{x}\right\rangle\right]
, over
\epi\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{C}_{e}
, with

\mathcal{C}_{e}\coloneqq\{\mu\times\delta_{\beta}\,|\,\mu\in\mathcal{C},\beta\in\mathbb{R}\}.

Clearly, the same equivalence holds locally, which is what we will use to
derive optimality conditions.
Note that we use bold symbols when working in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
(e.g.,
\boldsymbol{x}=(x,\beta)
with
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
).
Given
\boldsymbol{\mu}
, we can “extract”
\mu
and
\delta_{\beta}
via
{(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\mu
(i.e., we forget the last component) and
{(\pi_{d+1})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\delta_{\beta}
(i.e., we keep only the last component).
Similarly, given a variation
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
such that
{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
, we can extract the variation corresponding to
\mu
via
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
, where

T\coloneqq(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d},\pi_{d+2},\ldots,\pi_{2d+1})

(2.9)

(i.e., we forget the last component of the first and the second marginal),
and the variation “corresponding” to
\delta_{\beta}
via
{(\pi_{d+1},\pi_{2d+2})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
(i.e., we only keep the last component of the first and the second marginal).
With this formalism, the gradient of
l
follows directly from
Proposition

2.12
and it reads
\boldsymbol{\mu}\times\boldsymbol{\delta_{(0,\ldots,0,1)}}
. Thus, the complexity of our main result moves to proving first-order optimality conditions in the differentiable case subject to epigraphical constraints, for which, in particular, we need to study the variational geometry of
\epi\mathcal{J}
, which we do next.
To start, observe that epigraphical normals “point downwards in expectation”:

Proposition 2.28

(Characterization of the epigraphical normals)
.

For
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
, consider
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at which
\mathcal{J}
is finite. For all

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
(i.e., at the boundary of
\epi\mathcal{J}
) we have
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]\leq 0
.

Proposition

2.28
is reminiscent of the well-known fact that, in Euclidean settings, epigraphical normals never point upwards.
We depict this idea below on the right.

For the (discontinuous) function
f
with graph depicted in black, the epigraph is the region in light green above the graph. The thick blue arrows represent the pairs
(\upsilon,-1)
, with
\upsilon
regular subgradient of
f
at the discontinuity point (black-filled point). These arrows are also related to the normals of the epigraph, which contain their
\lambda
-scaled versions as well. These are depicted in dashed blue, and they intersect the
-\lambda
plane.

We rigorously show that the relationship between the nonhorizontal normals (in expectation) to
\epi\mathcal{J}
and the subgradients of
\mathcal{J}
translates to the Wasserstein space:

Proposition 2.29

(Subgradients and epigraphical normals)
.

Let
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
, let
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at which
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}
, and let

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})

(resp.,
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
) with
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=-1
. Then,
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
(resp.,
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
) with
T
as in (
2.9
).

Note that
Proposition

2.29
characterizes
all
nonhorizontal normals.
Indeed, if
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
satisfies
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=-\lambda<0
, then, since the normal cone is a cone (cf.
Proposition

2.21
),
\frac{1}{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\xi}
also belongs to
\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
and it satisfies
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\frac{1}{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\frac{1}{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=-1
. Thus,

{T}_{\#}{\frac{1}{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.
In Euclidean spaces, horizontal normals are referred to as horizon subgradients (we depict one in red in the figure above).
Thus, we introduce the natural counterpart in the Wasserstein space:

Definition 2.30

(Wasserstein horizon subgradients)
.

For
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and any
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at which
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}
,
we say that
\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\bar{\mu}}
is a
horizon subgradient of
\mathcal{J}
at
\bar{\mu}
,
\xi\in\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
, if there exists
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
such that
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\xi
and
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=0
, with
T
as in (
2.9
).

The horizon subgradients play a role in the constraint qualifications and, as we shall see in
Section

3
, are required for a comprehensive theory of optimization. Similar ideas are necessary already in Euclidean spaces and, perhaps surprisingly, they naturally carry over to the Wasserstein space. Since they are defined starting from the general normals to the epigraph, they are not guaranteed to be in the tangent space.
Nonetheless, for sufficiently well-behaved functionals they are trivial:

Proposition 2.31

(Horizon subgradients for strictly continuous functions)
.

Let
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}
. If
\mathcal{J}
is
strictly continuous
at
\bar{\mu}
, i.e.,

\limsup_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\to\bar{\mu}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\objective(\varGeneric_1) - \objective(\varGeneric_2)}}{W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)}<\infty
,
then the horizon subgradient of
\mathcal{J}
at
\bar{\mu}
is trivial; i.e.,
\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}{(\bar{\mu})}=\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
.

For instance, all locally Lipschitz functionals are strictly continuous and therefore have trivial horizon subgradients.
This is also the case for many of the functionals discussed so far:

Proposition 2.32

(Some horizon subgradients)
.

Each of the functionals
\mathcal{J}\colon\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
below is Lipschitz on bounded subsets of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(in the Wasserstein distance) and, in particular, strictly continuous:

1.

the potential energy
\mathcal{J}(\mu)=\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]
, with
V
satisfying the assumptions of
Proposition

2.12
and
V(x)\leq C(1+\left\|x\right\|^{2})
for some
C>0
;

2.

the interaction energy
\mathcal{J}(\mu)=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{(x,y)\sim\mu\times\mu}\left[U(x-y)\right]
, with
U
as in
Proposition

2.14
;

3.

the squared Wasserstein distance
\mathcal{J}(\mu)=\tfrac{1}{2}W_{2}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)^{2}
to a fixed
\hat{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, as in
Proposition

2.8
;

4.

the variance
\mathcal{J}(\mu)=\mathrm{Var}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]
, with
V
as in
Corollary

2.18
.

In particular, all these functionals have trivial horizon subgradient,
\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})=\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
, at every
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

The strict-continuity assumption in
Proposition

2.31
cannot be weakened to differentiability: a functional may be differentiable at
\bar{\mu}
and still have a nontrivial horizon subgradient there.

Example 2.33

(A differentiable functional with a nontrivial horizon subgradient)
.

Let
d=1
and let
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be
f(0)\coloneqq 0
and
f(x)\coloneqq x^{2}\sin(x^{-2})
for
x\neq 0
. Then
f
is differentiable on all of
\mathbb{R}
, with
f^{\prime}(0)=0
and, for
x\neq 0
,
f^{\prime}(x)=2x\sin(x^{-2})-2x^{-1}\cos(x^{-2})
; in particular,
f^{\prime}
is unbounded near
0
and takes arbitrarily large values of both signs. Consider
\mathcal{J}\colon\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq f\bigl(\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[x\right]\bigr)
, and
\bar{\mu}\coloneqq\delta_{0}
, so that
\mathcal{J}(\delta_{0})=0
.

\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at
\delta_{0}
, with
\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\delta_{0})=\mathbf{0}_{\delta_{0}}
.
For
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\delta_{0},\mu)}
, the mean of
\mu
equals
\mathbb{E}^{(x,\upsilon)\sim\xi}\left[\upsilon\right]
, so
\absolutevalue{\expectedValue{x\sim\varGeneric}{x}}\leq\left\|\xi\right\|_{\delta_{0}}=W_{2}\left(\delta_{0},\mu\right)
. Since
f
is differentiable at
0
with
f^{\prime}(0)=0
, we get
\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\delta_{0})=f(\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[x\right])-f(0)=o\left(\absolutevalue{\expectedValue{x\sim\varGeneric}{x}}\right)=o\left(\left\|\xi\right\|_{\delta_{0}}\right)
. Hence
\mathbf{0}_{\delta_{0}}
is a regular subgradient of both
\mathcal{J}
and
-\mathcal{J}
at
\delta_{0}
, and
Definition

2.3
gives differentiability with
\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\delta_{0})=\mathbf{0}_{\delta_{0}}
.

\mathcal{J}
is not strictly continuous at
\delta_{0}
.
Indeed,
\mathcal{J}(\delta_{s})=f(s)
and
W_{2}\left(\delta_{s_{1}},\delta_{s_{2}}\right)=\absolutevalue{s_1-s_2}
, so
\frac{\absolutevalue{\objective(\delta_{s_1})-\objective(\delta_{s_2})}}{W_{2}\left(\delta_{s_{1}},\delta_{s_{2}}\right)}=\frac{\absolutevalue{f(s_1)-f(s_2)}}{\absolutevalue{s_1-s_2}}
, whose supremum as
s_{1},s_{2}\to 0
is infinite because
f^{\prime}
is unbounded near
0
.

The horizon subgradient of
\mathcal{J}
at
\delta_{0}
is nontrivial.
Fix
w\neq 0
and choose
x_{n}\to 0
with
f^{\prime}(x_{n})\to+\infty
if
w>0
(resp.,
f^{\prime}(x_{n})\to-\infty
if
w<0
), and set
\tau_{n}\coloneqq w/f^{\prime}(x_{n})\searrow 0
. Repeating the estimate above at
x_{n}
(against epigraph points
\mu\times\delta_{\beta}
with
\beta\geq\mathcal{J}(\mu)
) shows that the deterministic variation
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}\coloneqq\delta_{\left((x_{n},f(x_{n})),\,(f^{\prime}(x_{n}),-1)\right)}
belongs to
\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\delta_{x_{n}}\times\delta_{f(x_{n})})
, with last velocity component
-1
in expectation and
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}}=\delta_{(x_{n},f^{\prime}(x_{n}))}
(
T
as in (
2.9
)). Since the regular normal cone is a cone,
\tau_{n}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}=\delta_{\left((x_{n},f(x_{n})),\,(w,-\tau_{n})\right)}
is also a regular normal. As
n\to\infty
, the base points
\delta_{x_{n}}\times\delta_{f(x_{n})}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
converge to
\delta_{0}\times\delta_{0}
and
\tau_{n}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}\coloneqq\delta_{\left((0,0),(w,0)\right)}
, so
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\delta_{0}\times\delta_{0})
by
Definition

2.20
. Its last velocity component has zero expectation and
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\delta_{(0,w)}={(\mathrm{Id},w)}_{\#}{\delta_{0}}
, so
{(\mathrm{Id},w)}_{\#}{\delta_{0}}\in\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\delta_{0})
by
Definition

2.30
. As
w\neq 0
was arbitrary,
\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\delta_{0})\neq\{\mathbf{0}_{\delta_{0}}\}
.

Thus
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at
\delta_{0}
yet has a nontrivial horizon subgradient, so the hypothesis of
Proposition

2.31
is sharp. Consistently, when we treat the differentiable case of
Theorem

3.2
we do not rely on the horizon subgradient being trivial.

With horizon subgradients, we can finally provide a full characterization of epigraphical normals:

Proposition 2.34

(Subgradients and epigraphical normals - cont’d)
.

Let
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at which
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}
. With
T
as in (
2.9
), for

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
with

{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}

we have that
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
if and only if either

1.

\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=-\lambda,\lambda>0
and
\frac{1}{\lambda}{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
; or

2.

\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=0
and
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.

Discussion.

The definition of horizon subgradients introduced in
Definition

2.30
follows the geometric ideas used for Euclidean spaces in
[
49
, Definition 1.18]
. It departs from the alternative definition of the horizon subgradients as the scaled limit of regular subgradients
[
46
, Definition 8.3]
, according to which
\bar{\xi}\in\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
if there are sequences
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu},\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})\to\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
so that there exists
(\tau_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\tau_{n}\searrow 0
and
\tau_{n}\xi_{n}\to\bar{\xi}
.
Nonetheless, it is well known that, at least in Euclidean settings, the two definitions are identical, cf.
[
46
, Theorem 8.9]
.
Finally, we could have defined the subgradients also as the nonhorizontal epigraphical normals, as in
[
49
, Definition 1.18]
. Nonetheless, to ease the presentation, we opted for the more established
Definition

2.2
, as in
[
46
, Definition 8.3]
.

2.5.3
Example: Level sets

To conclude this section, we study the normal cone to sublevel sets of continuous functionals:

Proposition 2.35

(Level sets)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,|\,\mathcal{J}(\mu)\leq 0\right\}
for a proper, continuous functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then, at any
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}
so that
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})=0
, we have

\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})\supseteq\left\{\lambda\xi\,|\,\lambda\geq 0,\xi\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\right\}

and

\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})\supseteq\left\{\lambda\xi\,|\,\lambda\geq 0,\xi\in\hat{\partial}^{s}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\right\}
,
and, if
\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\not\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
, then

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\left\{\lambda\xi\,|\,\lambda\geq 0,\xi\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\right\}\cup\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.
If
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})<0
, then
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})=\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})=\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})=\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})=\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
.

Proposition

2.35
is closely related to Lagrange multipliers in the Wasserstein space: The normal cone to a sublevel set of a functional
\mathcal{J}{}
consists of any scaling (by a multiplier) of the subgradients of
\mathcal{J}{}
. In particular, it includes the case where the constraint is not active (i.e., the multiplier is zero and the normal cone trivializes) and the case where the constraint is active (i.e., the multiplier is nonzero). The condition
\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\not\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
resembles the same “constraint qualification” required in Euclidean settings (cf.
[
46
, Proposition 10.3]
), and in practice it is often not restrictive.
With this result, we now study the normal cone to (closed) Wasserstein balls:

Proposition 2.36

(Normal cone to closed Wasserstein balls)
.

For
\varepsilon>0
and
\hat{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, consider the closed Wasserstein ball
\mathcal{C}=\bar{\mathbb{B}}_{W_{2}}(\hat{\mu};\varepsilon)=\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,|\,W_{2}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)\leq\varepsilon\}
. Let
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}
. Then,

1.

if
\bar{\mu}
lies in the interior of
\mathcal{C}
(i.e.,
W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu}\right)<\varepsilon
), then
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})=\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})=\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
;

2.

if
\bar{\mu}
is at the boundary of
\mathcal{C}
(i.e.,
W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu}\right)=\varepsilon
), then

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\{\xi\,|\,\xi\in{(\pi_{1},2\lambda(\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}))}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})},\lambda\geq 0\}
.
In particular, if
\bar{\mu}
is absolutely continuous, then

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\{{(\mathrm{Id},2\lambda(\mathrm{Id}-T))}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}\,|\,\lambda\geq 0\}
, where
T
is the unique optimal transport map between
\bar{\mu}
and
\hat{\mu}
;
and

3.

the closed Wasserstein ball
\mathcal{C}
is
SNC
at
\bar{\mu}
(
Definition

2.27
).

As a special case, we study the feasible set of the optimization problem (
1.2
), presented in
Section

1
, namely the second-moment constraint:

Example 2.37

(Second-moment constraint)
.

For
\varepsilon>0
, consider

\mathcal{C}=\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,|\,\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[\left\|x\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\varepsilon^{2}\}.

Then,
\mathcal{C}=\bar{\mathbb{B}}_{W_{2}}(\delta_{0};\varepsilon)
and, thus, for any
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in the interior of
\mathcal{C}
the (strong) normal cone trivializes, whereas for all the ones with

\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\bar{\mu}}\left[\left\|x\right\|^{2}\right]=\varepsilon^{2}

we have

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\left\{{(\mathrm{Id},2\lambda\mathrm{Id})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}\,|\,\lambda\geq 0\right\}.

3
Optimality conditions

In this section, we present and discuss our main result. To start, we need a notion of local optimality:

Definition 3.1

(Local optimality in the Wasserstein space)
.

A functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
attains a local minimum over a constraint set
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at
\mu^{\ast}\in\mathcal{C}
if there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for all
\mu\in\mathbb{B}_{W_{2}}(\mu^{\ast};\varepsilon)\cap\mathcal{C}
we have

\mathcal{J}(\mu)\geq\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast}).

Next is the main result of this work, which encompasses the celebrated Karush–Kuhn–Tucker and Lagrange conditions: first-order necessary optimality conditions in the Wasserstein space.

Theorem 3.2

(First-order optimality conditions)
.

If a proper functional
\mathcal{J}
attains a local minimum at
\mu^{\ast}\in\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with
\mathcal{J}
locally lower semicontinuous around
\mu^{\ast}
and
\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathcal{C}

SNC
at
\mu^{\ast}
and locally closed around
\mu^{\ast}
,
and the constraint qualification

\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\cap\left(-\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})\right)=\{\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\}

holds, then

\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})+\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})\coloneqq\left\{\xi_{1}\,+_{\alpha}\,\xi_{2}\,|\,\xi_{1}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast}),\xi_{2}\in\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}),\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\right\}.

In particular, since

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})\subseteq\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu^{\ast})

(
Lemma

2.25
2
),
the constraint qualification is implied by

\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\cap\left(-\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu^{\ast})\right)=\{\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\}

and, thus, the statement holds, a fortiori, with the normal cone

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu^{\ast})
in place of the strong normal cone

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})
, both in the constraint
qualification and in the conclusion.
When
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable (resp. strongly differentiable), the constraint qualification is not needed and the optimality condition reads

-\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\in\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu^{\ast})

(resp.
-\nabla^{s}{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\in\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})
)
even if
\mathcal{C}
is not
SNC
at
\mu^{\ast}
.

Geometrically, the constraint qualification requires that the feasible set
\mathcal{C}
is not “tangent” to the epigraph at
\mu^{\ast}
.
Similarly to the Euclidean setting, at optimality at least one element of the subgradient of the cost functional (direction of improvement) must align and have the same magnitude (but opposite direction) with one element of the normal cone (the “blocked” directions). We refer to
Section

3.1
for further discussion.
In the unconstrained case, the normal cone trivializes (cf.
Proposition

2.22
) and we recover Fermat’s rule:

Theorem 3.3

(Fermat’s rule in the Wasserstein space)
.

If a proper functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
attains a local minimum at
\mu^{\ast}

with
\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\in\mathbb{R}
, then

\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\in\hat{\partial}^{s}\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\subseteq{}\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast}),

which implies
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\in\partial^{s}\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\subseteq{}\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})
. When
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable (resp. strongly differentiable), this condition reduces to

\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}=\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})

(resp.
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}=\nabla^{s}{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})
).

3.1
Discussion

Before showcasing our first-order necessary optimality conditions in various examples, we discuss several related aspects.

Relation to classical variational analysis.

The conditions in
Theorems

3.2
and
3.3
very much resemble their counterparts in Euclidean spaces and Asplund spaces: the negative subgradient of the objective function must lie in the normal cone to the feasible set, provided that a constraint qualification (i.e., the intersection of the horizon subgradient and the “negative” normal cone is trivial) and a regularity condition on the feasible set hold (i.e., the set
\mathcal{C}
is
SNC
); e.g., see
[
46
,
49
]
for the corresponding statement in Euclidean spaces (where the
SNC
condition always holds and thus does not appear) and
[
48
, Section 5.1]
for the infinite-dimensional version.
Note that these results in Euclidean spaces generalize, among others, the celebrated Karush–Kuhn–Tucker and Lagrange conditions.

Comparison with the literature.

General necessary optimality conditions in the Wasserstein space are studied in
[
1
]
, in the smooth (or nonsmooth but convex) setting where the constraint is the level set of a real-valued functional, and in
[
38
,
39
,
29
]
in the different setting of optimal control problems in the Wasserstein space. With this work, we provide a general theory of variational analysis in the Wasserstein space and significantly generalize existing necessary conditions for static optimization.

How to apply
Theorem

3.2
.

The application of
Theorem

3.2
requires characterizing the subgradients of
\mathcal{J}
and the normal cone to
\mathcal{C}
.
For many functionals of practical interest, these characterizations are provided in
Section

2.4.2
. Moreover,
Proposition

2.16
provides tools to derive the subgradients of additional functionals.
The characterization of subgradients for other functionals is left to future research. Importantly, this needs to be carried out only once for each functional and, as in Euclidean spaces, we envision a growing collection of reusable results.
Similarly,
Proposition

2.35
provides a formula for the normal cones to the important class of feasible sets defined as level sets of functionals. These formulas can then be combined using the product and intersection rule in
Appendix

D
. As with subgradients, the derivation of further normal-cone formulas is left to future research.
Finally, the only remaining assumptions are that (i) the constraint set is
SNC
(properness is typically immediate in meaningful optimization settings) and (ii) the constraint qualification
\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\cap\left(-\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})\right)=\{\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\}
, imposed only along the smaller strong normal cone (cf.
Section

2.5.1
).
The
SNC
assumption is not required when the objective function is differentiable or when the problem is unconstrained. If the objective is merely subdifferentiable, then one must instead study the regularity properties of the constraint set, as we did in
Proposition

2.36
for Wasserstein balls.
By contrast, the constraint qualification is automatically satisfied for many functionals of practical interest, including nonsmooth ones, because their horizon subdifferentials are trivial. When the horizon subdifferential is nontrivial, however, this condition must be verified explicitly. In the unconstrained case, the normal cone reduces to
\{\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\}
and the constraint qualification holds automatically.

Computational aspects.

Our optimality conditions transform the problem of minimizing a function over the infinite-dimensional space of probability measures into an inclusion problem at the level of transport plans which, in turn, amounts to a set of conditions over
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Depending on the application, they can be solved in (quasi) closed-form or addressed via (nonlinear) function approximators
[
58
,
59
]
, as we show in our examples. For this reason, we argue that our necessary conditions are significantly more tractable than the initial infinite-dimensional optimization problem.
Nonetheless, more rigorous statements of the computational aspects of necessary conditions for optimality depend on the specifics of the problem at hand, as is already the case in Euclidean settings.

Sufficient conditions.

As in Euclidean settings, our conditions are not sufficient for optimality.
We expect sufficient conditions for optimality to be intimately related to geodesic convexity
[
35
, Section 7]
and second-order calculus in the Wasserstein space
[
60
]
; see
[
1
]
for preliminary results.

3.2
Pedagogical examples

In the remainder of this section, we illustrate our necessary conditions across several examples. Despite its pedagogical approach, this section culminates with
Example

3.6
, which already generalizes existing insights in
DRO
.
We start by rigorously solving the example in
Section

1
:

Example 3.4

(Expected value subject to second-moment constraints)
.

For
\theta\neq 0
and
\varepsilon>0
, consider the problem

\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\;\mathcal{J}(\mu)
, where
\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle\right]
, subject to

\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[\left\|x\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\varepsilon^{2}
.
By
Example

2.13
,
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable with gradient
\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu)={(\mathrm{Id},\theta)}_{\#}{\mu}
and general subdifferential
\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu)=\{\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu)\}
; its horizon subgradient is trivial (
Proposition

2.32
), so the constraint qualification of
Theorem

3.2
holds.
The strong normal cone to the second-moment constraint was studied in
Example

2.37
,
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})=\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
if
\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\bar{\mu}}\left[\left\|x\right\|^{2}\right]<\varepsilon^{2}
and
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\{{(\mathrm{Id},2\lambda\mathrm{Id})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}\,|\,\lambda\geq 0\}
if
\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\bar{\mu}}\left[\left\|x\right\|^{2}\right]=\varepsilon^{2}
; the set
\mathcal{C}
is a closed Wasserstein ball, hence closed, and it is
SNC
by
Proposition

2.36
. Any candidate optimal solution
\mu^{\ast}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
must satisfy the optimality conditions in
Theorem

3.2
.
We study two cases. If
\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\|x\right\|^{2}\right]<\varepsilon^{2}
, it holds

\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}=\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})
or, equivalently,

0=\left\|\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\right\|_{\mu^{\ast}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu^{\ast}(x)>0
,
which is absurd. Therefore, the optimal solution (if it exists) lies at the boundary, and a
\lambda>0
(for
\lambda=0
, the condition reduces to the interior case, which is absurd) and a “sum coupling”
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast}),\lambda{(\mathrm{Id},2\mathrm{Id})}_{\#}{\mu^{\ast}})
must exist so that

\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}=\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\,+_{\alpha}\,\lambda{(\mathrm{Id},2\mathrm{Id})}_{\#}{\mu^{\ast}}
or, equivalently,

0=\left\|\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\,+_{\alpha}\,\lambda{(\mathrm{Id},2\mathrm{Id})}_{\#}{\mu^{\ast}}\right\|_{\mu^{\ast}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\theta+2\lambda x\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu^{\ast}(x)
.
The above is precisely a “Lagrange’s condition” in the Wasserstein space, which yields
\mu^{\ast}=\delta_{-\frac{\theta}{2\lambda}}
and
\lambda=\frac{\left\|\theta\right\|}{2\varepsilon}
, since
\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\|x\right\|^{2}\right]=\varepsilon^{2}
.

We now show that necessary conditions for optimality can be used to produce certificates that an optimal solution does not exist:

Example 3.5

(A certificate of nonexistence of a minimizer)
.

Consider the problem of finding the worst-case mean-variance risk over the full Wasserstein space. In this case, for
\rho>0
and
0\neq\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we seek the minimum of the functional

\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq-\left(\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle\right]+\frac{\rho}{2}\mathrm{Var}^{x\sim\mu}\left[\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle\right]\right).

By
Propositions

2.12
,
2.16
and
2.18
,
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable with gradient

\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu)=-{(\mathrm{Id},(1+\rho(\left\langle\theta,\mathrm{Id}\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle\right]))\theta)}_{\#}{\mu}
.
To find the unconstrained minimizer of
\mathcal{J}
, we can deploy
Theorem

3.3
:
at any optimal
\mu^{\ast}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
it holds
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}=\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})
or, equivalently,
0=\left\|\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\right\|^{2}_{\mu^{\ast}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|(1+\rho(\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,y\right\rangle\right]))\theta\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu^{\ast}(x)
.
Thus,

0=1+\rho(\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,y\right\rangle\right])\quad\mu^{\ast}\text{-a.e.},

(3.1)

but taking the expected value with respect to
\mu^{\ast}
of both sides in (
3.1
) yields (it holds
\mu^{\ast}\text{-a.e.}
)

0=\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[0\right]=\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[1+\rho(\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,y\right\rangle\right])\right]=1
,
a contradiction. Thus, the infimum of
\mathcal{J}
is not attained.

While not a novel result, the above example shows that with only simple algebraic manipulations we can use the optimality conditions in
Theorems

3.2
and
3.3
to prove nonexistence of a solution, in a way that mirrors arguments in Euclidean spaces.

We now consider an example in the setting of
DRO
, a ubiquitous framework for decision-making under uncertainty. We seek to evaluate the worst-case mean-variance of a linear portfolio with allocation
\theta
but now constrained to a closed Wasserstein ball of a given radius
\varepsilon
and center
\hat{\mu}
.
Formally, this example is similar to
Example

3.5
, but now the worst-case is taken over a Wasserstein ball instead of the entire Wasserstein space.
As we shall see below, our necessary conditions lead to a closed-form solution for the worst-case probability measure, generalizing
[
1
, Section 4]
to non-absolutely continuous measures (which, among others, allows us to study the data-driven setting where
\hat{\mu}
is empirical) and
[
61
, Appendix E]
, which does not provide a closed-form solution and assumes positive variance of
\hat{\mu}
.

Example 3.6

(A constrained problem: mean-variance
DRO
)
.

Consider the mean-variance functional
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
defined in
Example

3.5
and the constraint
\mu\in\bar{\mathbb{B}}_{W_{2}}(\hat{\mu};\varepsilon)
for some
\hat{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\varepsilon>0
.
We computed the gradient of
\mathcal{J}
in
Example

3.5
, and we recall from
Proposition

2.36
that the strong normal cone to
\bar{\mathbb{B}}_{W_{2}}(\hat{\mu};\varepsilon)
at any
\bar{\mu}\in\bar{\mathbb{B}}_{W_{2}}(\hat{\mu};\varepsilon)
satisfies

\normal_{\bar{\mathbb{B}}_{W_{2}}(\hat{\mu};\varepsilon)}^{s}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\{\xi\,|\,\xi\in{(\pi_{1},2\lambda(\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}))}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})},\lambda\geq 0\}
, and that the (closed) ball is
SNC
.
The remaining hypotheses of

Theorem

3.2
hold as well:
\mathcal{J}

is a linear combination of an expected value and a variance of the linear
function
x\mapsto\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle
(whose square has uniformly
bounded Hessian
2\theta{\theta}^{\top}
), so each term is Lipschitz on
bounded sets (
Proposition

2.32
),
\mathcal{J}
is
strictly continuous, and its horizon subgradient is trivial
(
Proposition

2.31
), whence the constraint
qualification; moreover,
\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})=\{\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\}
, since the expected-value part has
singleton subdifferential (
Proposition

2.12
),
the variance part is differentiable
(
Corollary

2.18
) with a gradient that is
continuous by its explicit formula (convergence in Wasserstein in the argument
and in the value), and the sum rule
(
Proposition

2.16
) applies.
Then, by
Theorem

3.2
, at any optimal
\mu^{\ast}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
an optimal transport plan
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu^{\ast},\hat{\mu})
, a Lagrange multiplier
\lambda\geq 0
, and a “sum coupling”
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast}),2\lambda{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma})

exist so that
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}=\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\,+_{\alpha}\,2\lambda{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma}
. Equivalently,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\min_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast}),2\lambda{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma})}\left\|\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\,+_{\alpha}\,2\lambda{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma}\right\|^{2}_{\mu^{\ast}}

\displaystyle=\min_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast}),2\lambda{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}(\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\,+_{\alpha}\,2\lambda{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma})(\bar{x},\upsilon)

\displaystyle=\min_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast}),2\lambda{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{1}+\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha(x,\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}).

Let now
\alpha
be the minimizer. Then,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|-(1+\rho(\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,z\right\rangle\right]))\theta+\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha(x,\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|-(1+\rho(\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,z\right\rangle\right]))\theta+\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}({\pi_{13}}_{\#}{\alpha})(x,\upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|-(1+\rho(\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,z\right\rangle\right]))\theta+2\lambda\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}({(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma})(x,\upsilon)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|-(1+\rho(\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,z\right\rangle\right]))\theta+2\lambda(x-y)\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y).

Thus, if
\mu^{\ast}\in\bar{\mathbb{B}}_{W_{2}}(\hat{\mu};\varepsilon)
is optimal, we must have

(1+\rho(\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,z\right\rangle\right]))\theta=2\lambda(x-y)\quad\gamma\text{-a.e.}

(3.2)

We now proceed in three steps:

Step 1.

We take the expected value with respect to
\gamma
of both sides of (
3.2
) to get

\theta=2\lambda(\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[x\right]-\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\hat{\mu}}\left[y\right]).

(3.3)

If
\lambda=0
, it must also be
\theta=0
, so that either there is no solution (if
\theta\neq 0
, consistently with
Example

3.5
) or any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is optimal, by direct inspection.
Suppose
\theta\neq 0
and, thus,
\lambda>0
, so that applying the linear map
\left\langle\theta,\cdot\right\rangle
to both sides of (
3.3
) we get:

\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle\right]=\frac{\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}}{2\lambda}+\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\hat{\mu}}\left[\left\langle\theta,y\right\rangle\right].

(3.4)

Step 2.

We square both sides of (
3.2
) and take the expected value with respect to
\gamma
. The right-hand side gives

\mathbb{E}^{(x,y)\sim\gamma}\left[\left\|2\lambda(x-y)\right\|^{2}\right]=4\lambda^{2}\mathbb{E}^{(x,y)\sim\gamma}\left[\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}\right]=4\lambda^{2}\varepsilon^{2}
,
where the last equality comes from
\lambda>0
and so
\mu^{\ast}\in\partial\mathbb{B}_{W_{2}}(\hat{\mu};\varepsilon)
.
The squared norm of the left-hand side, instead, reads in expectation as

\mathbb{E}^{(x,y)\sim\gamma}\left[\left\|(1+\rho(\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,z\right\rangle\right]))\theta\right\|^{2}\right]=\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}\left(1+\rho^{2}\mathbb{E}^{(x,y)\sim\gamma}\left[\left(\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}^{z\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,z\right\rangle\right]\right)^{2}\right]\right)=\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}\left(1+\rho^{2}\mathrm{Var}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle\right]\right)
.
Overall,

\mathrm{Var}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle\right]=\frac{4\lambda^{2}\varepsilon^{2}-\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}}{\rho^{2}\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}}.

(3.5)

Step 3.

We write (
3.2
) as

(\rho\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}-2\lambda)\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle+\text{const.}=-2\lambda\left\langle\theta,y\right\rangle

and evaluate the variance with respect to
\gamma
on both sides to get

(\rho\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}-2\lambda)^{2}\mathrm{Var}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle\right]=4\lambda^{2}\mathrm{Var}^{y\sim\hat{\mu}}\left[\left\langle\theta,y\right\rangle\right].

(3.6)

Thus, if
\mathrm{Var}^{y\sim\hat{\mu}}\left[\left\langle\theta,y\right\rangle\right]>0
, then
\lambda\neq\frac{\rho\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}}{2}
, and (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) yield
\lambda
from a polynomial equation (cf.
[
1
, Proposition 4.7]
).
In this case, the optimal cost is

\frac{\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}}{2\lambda}+\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\hat{\mu}}\left[\left\langle\theta,y\right\rangle\right]+\frac{2\rho\lambda^{2}}{(\rho\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}-2\lambda)^{2}}\mathrm{Var}^{y\sim\hat{\mu}}\left[\left\langle\theta,y\right\rangle\right]
.
If, instead,
\mathrm{Var}^{y\sim\hat{\mu}}\left[\left\langle\theta,y\right\rangle\right]=0
(and so
\hat{\mu}
is not absolutely continuous), then (
3.6
) yields two cases.
If
\lambda=\frac{\rho\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}}{2}
, then (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) yield the optimal cost

\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\hat{\mu}}\left[\left\langle\theta,y\right\rangle\right]+\frac{\rho\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{2\rho}
.
If
\mathrm{Var}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[\left\langle\theta,x\right\rangle\right]=0
, then (
3.5
) gives
\lambda=\frac{\left\|\theta\right\|}{2\varepsilon}
and (
3.4
) yields the optimal cost

\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\hat{\mu}}\left[\left\langle\theta,y\right\rangle\right]+\left\|\theta\right\|\varepsilon
.
With
\lambda
, the optimal solution follows from inverting the affine relation (cf. (
3.2
)):

y=T(x)={\left(I-\frac{\rho\theta{\theta}^{\top}}{2\lambda}\right)}x-{\frac{1}{2\lambda}\left({1-\rho\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\hat{\mu}}\left[\left\langle\theta,y\right\rangle\right]-\frac{\rho\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}}{2\lambda}}\right)\theta}.

Finally, since
{(\mathrm{Id},T)}_{\#}{\mu^{\ast}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu^{\ast},\hat{\mu})
,
T
is an optimal transport map. By
[
51
, Theorem 5.10]
, it is also a gradient of a convex function, which implies
\lambda\geq\frac{\rho\left\|\theta\right\|^{2}}{2}
. In particular, our results generalize
[
1
]
.

4
Applications

We demonstrate the practical appeal of our first-order optimality conditions by discussing several applications in machine learning and nonlinear
DRO
.
Importantly, our optimality conditions have already contributed to the development of several state-of-the-art algorithms, including learning diffusion processes from population data
[
23
]
and energy-based generative modeling
[
25
]
.

4.1
Machine learning

In
[
23
]
, we showcase how our first-order optimality conditions improve the computational efficiency of learning the dynamics of particles undergoing diffusion, bypassing cumbersome bi-level optimization procedures
[
62
,
23
]
. In this section, we study applications to drug discovery and distribution fitting.

4.1.1
Drug discovery

In Euclidean settings, the proximal operator appears as an often closed-form subroutine of various first-order optimization algorithms
[
63
]
. Recently, it has found applications in learning the dynamics of particles undergoing diffusion
[
62
,
23
]
,
single-cell perturbation analysis
[
22
]
, and
drug discovery
[
64
, Section 6]
. We use the first-order optimality conditions provided in this work to study the proximal operator, and we showcase how these results can be applied to molecular discovery. More precisely, following
[
64
, Section 6]
, the task of drug discovery and drug repurposing can be cast as an iterative process that aims to increase the
drug-likeness

\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]
of a distribution of molecules
\mu
while staying close to the original distribution
\mu_{t}
, which (up to replacing
V
with
-V
) gives the optimization problem

\mu_{t+1}=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]+\frac{1}{2}W_{2}\left(\mu,\mu_{t}\right)^{2}.

The iterative approach is preferred in practice over a one-shot solution due to the nonconvexity of the drug-likeness potential
V
.
We now study this optimization problem.
Unlike
[
64
, Section 6]
, we do not require any convex proxy for it:

Example 4.1

(Proximal operator in the Wasserstein space)
.

Let
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy the assumptions of
Proposition

2.12
3
.
Consider the proximal operator in the Wasserstein space, defined by

{\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathcal{J}(\mu),\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]+\frac{1}{2}W_{2}\left(\mu,\bar{\mu}\right)^{2}.}

(4.1)

We aim to characterize optimal solutions of (
4.1
).
Since
V
is differentiable, the sum rule (
Proposition

2.16
1
with
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}\left(\mu,\bar{\mu}\right)^{2}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu)=\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]
) gives

\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu)\subseteq\left\{{(\mathrm{Id},\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\mu}\,+_{\alpha}\,\xi\,|\,\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\bar{\mu})},\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}({(\mathrm{Id},\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\mu},\xi)\right\}
.
By
Theorem

3.3
, the subgradient of
\mathcal{J}{}
vanishes at an optimal solution
\mu^{\ast}
. Thus, at optimality there exist
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu^{\ast},\bar{\mu})
and
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}({(\mathrm{Id},\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\mu^{\ast}},{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma})

so that
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}={(\mathrm{Id},\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\mu^{\ast}}\,+_{\alpha}\,{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma}
.
After the (by now) usual steps, we conclude that this equivalently means that
for almost all
(x,y)\in\supp(\gamma)
, it necessarily holds

0=\nabla{V}(x)+(x-y)=\nabla{}\left(V(x)+\frac{\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}}{2}\right)
,
which recovers the standard optimality condition for the proximal operator in Euclidean spaces.
By direct inspection, we conclude that the cost-minimizing choice is
x\in\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(z)+\frac{\left\|z-y\right\|^{2}}{2}
for
\gamma\text{-a.e.}

y
,

\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})=\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\bar{\mu}}\left[\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x)+\frac{1}{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}\right]
.
In particular, the proximal operator (
4.1
) in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be expressed in terms of the proximal operator on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In the special case where the function
x\mapsto V(x)+\frac{\left\|x\right\|^{2}}{2}
is strictly convex, we conclude
\mu^{\ast}={(\mathrm{Id}+\nabla{V})^{-1}}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
.

Importantly,
Example

4.1
suggests a closed-form solution that can be incorporated into, e.g.,
[
64
]
to improve the efficiency of the numerical procedure. The numerical benefits of using reformulations inspired by our first-order optimality conditions have been explored in detail in, e.g.,
[
23
]
.

4.1.2
Learning Gaussians

Gaussian mixture models are among the most widespread techniques for distribution fitting
[
65
,
23
,
66
]
. It is well known (e.g., see
[
66
]
and references therein) that this problem can be formulated as an optimization problem in the space of probability measures:
Since a Gaussian mixture model can be written as
\mu\ast\phi
with
\phi(x)
being the Gaussian kernel
3
3

3

Here, we consider the mixture of Gaussians with unit variance, but the discussion in this section easily extends to general mixtures.

\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\exp(-\frac{\norm{x}^2}{2})
, the learning problem can be cast as the optimization problem

\min_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x-X_{i})\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)
,
where
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
is a given dataset.
Since the objective is differentiable,
Theorem

3.3
yields the necessary optimality condition

0=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\nabla{\phi}(x-X_{i})}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(y-X_{i})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\ast}(y)}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu^{\ast}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{(x-X_{i})\exp(-\frac{\norm{x-X_i}^2}{2})}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp(-\frac{\norm{y-X_i}^2}{2})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\ast}(y)}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu^{\ast}(x),

(4.2)

where we used the sum and chain rules (
Proposition

2.16
) and differentiability of
\mu\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x-X_{i})\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
(
Proposition

2.12
3
) to evaluate the subgradient.
At this point, one can parametrize
\mu
with finitely many samples
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
, with weights
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{m}
, and search for the
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
and
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{m}
which best “fit” the first-order optimality condition. We showcase the results of the method in
Figure

4
. Most importantly, our method also provides a simple and computationally efficient way to numerically assess the output of other distribution fitting methods: For approximately optimal solutions, evaluating (
4.2
) must result in negligible values.

Figure 4:
Data (red crosses) generated from mixtures with one, three, and five Gaussian components (left to right) are fitted using the method outlined in
Section

4.1.2
with three components.

4.2
Nonlinear mean-variance optimization

In this section, we extend the existing duality result for linear mean-variance distributionally robust optimization
[
10
,
11
,
13
,
67
,
12
]
to the nonlinear case.
Specifically, for
\hat{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\rho>0,\varepsilon>0
, and a differentiable function
V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying the assumptions of
Corollary

2.18
, consider the problem of evaluating the
worst-case
risk over a Wasserstein ball:

{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})=\inf_{\mu\in\bar{\mathbb{B}}_{W_{2}}(\hat{\mu};\varepsilon)}\mathcal{J}(\mu)\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq-\left(\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]+\rho\mathrm{Var}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]\right).}

(4.3)

We use our results to show that any candidate optimal solution
\mu^{\ast}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to (
4.3
) yields the worst-case cost

\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})=-\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\geq 0\\
\beta\in\mathbb{R}\end{subarray}}\;\lambda\varepsilon^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{V(y)+\rho(V(y)-\beta)^{2}-\lambda\left\|y-\hat{x}\right\|^{2}\right\}\mathrm{d}\hat{\mu}(\hat{x}).

(4.4)

Importantly, (
4.4
) reduces the infinite-dimensional problem in the space of probability measures (
4.3
) to a two-dimensional problem over the reals.
Moreover, with
\lambda^{\ast},\beta^{\ast}
being the minimizers above, there exists
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu^{\ast},\hat{\mu})
such that

x^{\ast}\in\mathop{\rm arg\,max}_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{V(y)+\rho(V(y)-\beta^{\ast})^{2}-\lambda^{\ast}\left\|y-\hat{x}\right\|^{2}\right\}\quad\forall(x^{\ast},\hat{x})\in\supp(\gamma).

(4.5)

Thus, the optimizers
\lambda^{\ast},\beta^{\ast}
can be used to construct an optimal solution of (
4.3
) by allocating the mass at
\hat{x}

\hat{\mu}\text{-a.e.}
to the corresponding set of maximizers in (
4.5
).

To prove (
4.4
), we establish the two inequalities separately.
For “
\leq
”,
Theorem

3.2
combined with
Propositions

2.12
,
2.16
,
2.18
,
2.32
and
2.36
(in particular, the constraint qualification holds since each term of
\mathcal{J}
is Lipschitz on bounded sets and the horizon subgradient is thus trivial, cf.
Propositions

2.32
and
2.31
, and the closed Wasserstein ball is
SNC
by
Proposition

2.36
) requires that, for any optimal solution
\mu^{\ast}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, there are
\gamma\in{\Gamma_{o}({\mu^{\ast}},\hat{\mu})}
and
\xi=2\lambda^{\ast}{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma}
with
\lambda^{\ast}>0
(in particular,
W_{2}\left(\mu^{\ast},\hat{\mu}\right)=\varepsilon
and
\xi\in\normal_{\bar{\mathbb{B}}_{W_{2}}(\hat{\mu};\varepsilon)}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})
; for
\lambda^{\ast}=0
, see
Example

3.5
) such that

\mathbf{0}_{{\mu^{\ast}}}\in{(\pi_{1},-\nabla{V}\circ\pi_{1}(1+2\rho(V\circ\pi_{1}-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[V(x)\right]))+\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi}

or, equivalently,

\mathbf{0}_{{\mu^{\ast}}}\in{(\pi_{1},-\nabla{V}\circ\pi_{1}(1+2\rho(V\circ\pi_{1}-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[V(x)\right]))+2\lambda^{\ast}(\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}))}_{\#}{\gamma}
.
This implies that for all
(x^{\ast},\hat{x})\in\supp(\gamma)
we have

0=-\nabla{V}(x^{\ast})(1+2\rho(V(x^{\ast})-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[V(x)\right]))+2\lambda^{\ast}(x^{\ast}-\hat{x})

, which, following
Example

4.1
, allows us to conclude

x^{\ast}\in\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{-V(y)-\rho(V(y)-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[V(x)\right])^{2}+\lambda^{\ast}\left\|y-\hat{x}\right\|^{2}\right\}
.
Overall, we have

x^{\ast}\in\mathop{\rm arg\,max}_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{V(y)+\rho(V(y)-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[V(x)\right])^{2}-\lambda^{\ast}\left\|y-\hat{x}\right\|^{2}\right\}\quad\forall(x^{\ast},\hat{x})\in\supp(\gamma).

(4.6)

Thus, with
\mathrm{Var}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]=\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[(V(x)-\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\mu}\left[V(y)\right])^{2}\right]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}^{x^{\ast}\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[V(x^{\ast})+\rho\left(V(x^{\ast})-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[V(x)\right]\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle{=}\begin{aligned} -\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x^{\ast})+&\rho\left(V(x^{\ast})-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[V(x)\right]\right)^{2}-\lambda^{\ast}\left\|x^{\ast}-\hat{x}\right\|^{2}+\lambda^{\ast}\left\|x^{\ast}-\hat{x}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(x^{\ast},\hat{x})\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{\ast}\varepsilon^{2}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{V(y)+\rho\left(V(y)-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu^{\ast}}\left[V(x)\right]\right)^{2}-\lambda^{\ast}\left\|y-\hat{x}\right\|^{2}\right\}\mathrm{d}\hat{\mu}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\geq 0\\
\beta\in\mathbb{R}\end{subarray}}-\lambda\varepsilon^{2}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{V(y)+\rho\left(V(y)-\beta\right)^{2}-\lambda\left\|y-\hat{x}\right\|^{2}\right\}\mathrm{d}\hat{\mu}(\hat{x}),

which establishes the first inequality.
For “
\geq
”, we rewrite
\mathrm{Var}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]
as the minimization
\inf_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[(V(x)-\beta)^{2}\right]
and use a standard minimax argument:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]-\rho\inf_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[(V(x)-\beta)^{2}\right]+\lambda(W_{2}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)^{2}-\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\geq 0\\
\beta\in\mathbb{R}\end{subarray}}-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)+\rho(V(x)-\beta)^{2}\right]+\lambda(W_{2}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)^{2}-\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\geq 0\\
\beta\in\mathbb{R}\end{subarray}}\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)+\rho(V(x)-\beta)^{2}\right]+\lambda(W_{2}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)^{2}-\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\geq 0\\
\beta\in\mathbb{R}\end{subarray}}-\lambda\varepsilon^{2}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{V(y)+\rho\left(V(y)-\beta\right)^{2}-\lambda\left\|y-\hat{x}\right\|^{2}\right\}\mathrm{d}\hat{\mu}(\hat{x}),

where the last equality follows from the proximal operator in
Example

4.1
. This establishes the second inequality and concludes the proof of (
4.4
).

Appendix A
Auxiliary technical results

A.1
Basics of calculus and optimal transport

This section gathers auxiliary technical results of limited novelty used throughout the paper.
We start with some properties of functionals on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

Proposition A.1

(Subgradients of
\lambda
-convex functions)
.

For any proper, lower semicontinuous,
\lambda
-convex functional
V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and any point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
V(x)\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\hat{\partial}V(x)=\left\{\upsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,|\,\forall y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},V(y)\geq V(x)+\left\langle\upsilon,y-x\right\rangle+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\|y-x\right\|^{2}\right\}=\partial V(x)
and

\partial^{\infty}V(x)\subseteq\left\{\upsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,|\,\forall y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},V(y)\in\mathbb{R},0\geq\left\langle\upsilon,y-x\right\rangle\right\}
.
In particular, if
V(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subseteq\mathbb{R}
,
\partial^{\infty}V(x)=\{0\}
.

Proof A.2
.

Proof.
The characterization for
\hat{\partial}V(x)
follows by definition of
\lambda
-convexity (cf.
[
35
, Equation 10.4.10]
). Then, for any
\upsilon\in\partial V(x)
consider any sequences
x_{n}\to x
,
\upsilon_{n}\to\upsilon
,
\upsilon_{n}\in\hat{\partial}V(x_{n})
. Since
V
is lower semicontinuous, we have
\liminf_{n\to\infty}V(x_{n})\geq V(x)
and, since it is proper and
\upsilon_{n}\in\hat{\partial}V(x_{n})
, for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

V(y)\geq V(x_{n})+\left\langle\upsilon_{n},y-x_{n}\right\rangle+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\|y-x_{n}\right\|^{2}
, which implies by continuity of the inner product and of the norm that

V(y)\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\left(V(x_{n})+\left\langle\upsilon_{n},y-x_{n}\right\rangle+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\|y-x_{n}\right\|^{2}\right)\geq V(x)+\left\langle\upsilon,y-x\right\rangle+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\|y-x\right\|^{2}
.
In particular,
\partial V(x)\subseteq\hat{\partial}V(x)
and, since the other inclusion is immediate,
\partial V(x)=\hat{\partial}V(x)
.
Let now
\upsilon\in\partial^{\infty}V(x)
and consider the corresponding sequences
\tau_{n}\searrow 0
,
x_{n}\to x
,
V(x_{n})\to V(x)
and
\tau_{n}\upsilon_{n}\to\upsilon
,
\upsilon_{n}\in\hat{\partial}V(x_{n})
. For all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\upsilon_{n}\in\hat{\partial}V(x_{n})
implies
V(y)\geq V(x_{n})+\left\langle\upsilon_{n},y-x_{n}\right\rangle+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\|y-x_{n}\right\|^{2}
and, thus,

\tau_{n}V(y)\geq\tau_{n}V(x_{n})+\left\langle\tau_{n}\upsilon_{n},y-x_{n}\right\rangle+\tau_{n}\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\|y-x_{n}\right\|^{2}
.
For every
y
with
V(y)\in\mathbb{R}
, letting
n\to\infty
(using
\tau_{n}\searrow 0
,
V(x_{n})\to V(x)\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\tau_{n}\upsilon_{n}\to\upsilon
) yields
0\geq\left\langle\upsilon,y-x\right\rangle
, which is the desired inclusion.
Since
V-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2}
is proper, lower semicontinuous, and convex,
with the same domain as
V
,
[
46
, Proposition 8.12]

together with
[
46
, Exercise 8.8(c)]
(horizon
subgradients are unaffected by smooth additions) gives
\partial^{\infty}V(x)=\normal_{\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,|\,V(y)\in\mathbb{R}\}}(x),

so that if
V(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, we conclude
\partial^{\infty}V(x)=\{0\}
.

The second result extends
[
27
, Lemma 6.1]
to the case where one marginal is common. In particular, the proof is analogous, and the same proof allows us to state the result “pointwise”, rather than just for the infimum. The extension to multiple marginals, or to several common marginals, is straightforward, so we omit the proof.

Lemma A.3

(Pushforward and optimal transport)
.

Assume that, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i^{\prime}}
are separable completely metrizable topological spaces,

c:\mathcal{X}_{1^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{X}_{2^{\prime}}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
is a transportation cost,

l_{i}:\mathcal{X}_{i}\to\mathcal{X}_{i^{\prime}}
are maps, and

\xi_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}_{i})
are probability measures
such that
{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\xi_{1}}={\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\xi_{2}}
.
Then, for every

\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
,
there exists

\alpha^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{1}({(\pi_{1},l_{1}\circ\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi_{1}},{(\pi_{1},l_{2}\circ\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi_{2}})

such that:

\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}}c(l_{1}(x_{1}),l_{2}(x_{2}))\mathrm{d}\alpha(x,x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}_{1^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{X}_{2^{\prime}}}c(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\alpha^{\prime}(x,x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}).

In particular, it holds:

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}}c(l_{1}(x_{1}),l_{2}(x_{2}))\mathrm{d}\alpha(x,x_{1},x_{2})}

\displaystyle={\inf_{\alpha^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{1}({(\pi_{1},l_{1}\circ\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi_{1}},{(\pi_{1},l_{2}\circ\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi_{2}})}\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}_{1^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{X}_{2^{\prime}}}c(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\alpha^{\prime}(x,x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}).}

Remark A.4

(Extension to countably many spaces)
.

The proof of
[
27
, Lemma 6.1]
extends to countably many spaces, provided that the limit distribution arising from the iterated application of
[
51
, Gluing Lemma]
is well defined. For this, it suffices to invoke the Kolmogorov extension theorem
[
68
, Section 2.4]
.

The next result concerns the stability of the set of
transport plans:

Lemma A.5
.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 2
, and
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m-1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and consider the
set-valued map

\mu\mapsto G(\mu)=\Gamma(\mu,\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m-1})\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m})
,
defined on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Then,
G
is lower hemicontinuous in Wasserstein.

Proof A.6
.

Proof.
We prove the case with two marginals (
m=2
), the general case being analogous.
For lower hemicontinuity, let
\mu_{n}\to\mu
and
\gamma\in G(\mu)=\Gamma(\mu,\nu_{1})
. We need to show that there is a sequence
\gamma_{n}\in G(\mu_{n})=\Gamma(\mu_{n},\nu_{1})
so that
\gamma_{n}\to\gamma
.
To start, since
\mu_{n}\to\mu
, there is a sequence
\bar{\gamma}_{n}\in\Gamma(\mu,\mu_{n})
so that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x-x_{n}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\gamma}_{n}(x,x_{n})\to 0
.
Via
[
51
, Gluing Lemma]
, define
\alpha_{n}\in\Gamma^{1}(\bar{\gamma}_{n},\gamma)
by “gluing”
\bar{\gamma}_{n}
and
\gamma
; in particular,
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\alpha_{n}}=\bar{\gamma}_{n}
and
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha_{n}}=\gamma
.
Define
\gamma_{n}={(\pi_{2},\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha_{n}}
. By construction,
\gamma_{n}\in G(\mu_{n})=\Gamma(\mu_{n},\nu_{1})
. We now claim
\gamma_{n}\to\gamma
. With the candidate transport plan
\beta_{n}={(\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{1},\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha_{n}}\in\Gamma(\gamma_{n},\gamma)
, we have

\displaystyle W_{2}\left(\gamma_{n},\gamma\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{n}-x\right\|^{2}+\left\|y_{n}-y\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\beta_{n}(x_{n},y_{n},x,y)

\displaystyle={\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{n}-x\right\|^{2}+\left\|y-y\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{n}(x,x_{n},y)}

\displaystyle={\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{n}-x\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{n}(x,x_{n},y)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{n}-x\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\gamma}_{n}(x,x_{n})\to 0.

Thus,
\gamma_{n}\to\gamma
, which concludes the proof.

A.2
Geometry of the Wasserstein space

We recall from
[
69
]
several properties of the operations in
Definition

2.1
:

Proposition A.7

(Sum and scale)
.

Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
. Then,

1.

For all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
,
\tau\xi_{1}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
.

2.

For
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
,

\xi_{1}\,+_{\alpha}\,\xi_{2}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}+\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
.

Proof A.8
.

Proof of
Proposition

A.7
.
See
[
69
, Proposition 4.25]
for (i) and
[
69
, Proposition 4.29]
and the preceding discussion for (ii).

Next, we study the geometry of the Wasserstein space:

Proposition A.9

(Properties of the Wasserstein geometry)
.

Let
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\xi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\bar{\mu}}
.
The following statements hold:

1.

W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right)^{2}=\left\|\xi_{1}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}-2\left\langle\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+\left\|\xi_{2}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}
.

2.

The map
W_{\bar{\mu}}
is continuous in the topology induced by
W_{\bar{\mu}}
.

3.

The map
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}
is continuous in the topology induced by
W_{\bar{\mu}}
.

4.

\left\langle\lambda_{1}\xi_{1},\lambda_{2}\xi_{2}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\left\langle\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}

for any
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\geq 0
.

5.

\left\langle\xi_{1}\,+_{\alpha}\,\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq\left\langle\xi_{1},\xi_{3}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+\left\langle\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}

for any
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
.

6.

\lambda\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
and

\left\langle\lambda\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\left\langle\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=0

for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

7.

\xi_{1}\,+_{\alpha}\,\xi_{2}=\xi_{2}\,+_{\alpha^{\prime}}\,\xi_{1}

for any
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
,
\alpha^{\prime}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{3},\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\alpha}
.

8.

\xi\,+_{\alpha}\,\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}=\xi
for any
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}})
.

9.

(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})\xi=\lambda_{1}\xi\,+_{\alpha}\,\lambda_{2}\xi
for any
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha={(\pi_{1},\lambda_{1}\pi_{2},\lambda_{2}\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi}
.

10.

\left\langle\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq\left\|\xi_{1}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\left\|\xi_{2}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}
and
\left\langle\xi,\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}
.

11.

\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=0
if and only if
\xi=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
.

12.

If
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right)<\varepsilon
, there exist
\xi_{12}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\bar{\mu}}
with
\left\|\xi_{12}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\leq\varepsilon
and
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{12})
so that
\xi_{1}\,+_{\alpha}\,\xi_{12}=\xi_{2}
.

Proof A.10
.

Proof.
We prove the statements separately.

1.

See
[
35
, Proposition 12.4.2]
.

2.

Every distance is continuous in the topology it induces; see
[
70
, Theorem 21.1]
and
[
35
, Proposition 12.4.2]
.

3.

See
[
69
, Proposition 4.21]
.

4.

See
[
69
, Proposition 4.27]
.

5.

See
[
69
, Proposition 4.27]
.

6.

By definition of
\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
and of scaling,
\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}=\lambda\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\langle\lambda\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\lambda\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}},\xi)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle=\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\lambda\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}},\xi)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\lambda 0,\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle=0=\left\langle\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}.

7.

Commutativity follows directly from commutativity of every operation involved; it suffices to consider the pushforward via the map
(\pi_{1},\pi_{3},\pi_{2})
of the couplings.

8.

See
[
69
, Proposition 4.22]
.

9.

See
[
69
, Proposition 4.22]
.

10.

We have

\displaystyle\left\langle\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle{\leq}\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{1}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})}
