\displaystyle\qquad\times\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{1}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1})}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}(\bar{x},\upsilon_{2})}

\displaystyle=\left\|\xi_{1}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\left\|\xi_{2}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}},

where in
\heartsuit
we used Hölder’s inequality. In the case
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=\xi
, we additionally have

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi}\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi)
and

\left\langle\xi,\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}({(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi})(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(\bar{x},\upsilon)=\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}
.
Thus,
\left\langle\xi,\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}
.

11.

By
10
and
6
with
\xi=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
and
\lambda=1
,
\left\|\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=0
. Suppose now
\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=0
. Then,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(\bar{x},\upsilon)=0
and, thus,
{\pi_{2}}_{\#}{\xi}=\delta_{0}
. Since
{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\xi}=\bar{\mu}
,
\xi=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
.

12.

Let
\alpha^{\ast}\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
be the optimal coupling in
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right)
.
With this, define

\alpha\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},-\pi_{2}+\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha^{\ast}}
and

\xi_{12}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha}
. Then,
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{12})
and
\xi_{1}\,+_{\alpha}\,\xi_{12}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}+\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}+\pi_{3})}_{\#}{{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},-\pi_{2}+\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha^{\ast}}}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}+(-\pi_{2}+\pi_{3}))}_{\#}{\alpha^{\ast}}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha^{\ast}}=\xi_{2}
.
To conclude,
\left\|\xi_{12}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi_{12}(\bar{x},\upsilon)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\left\|-\upsilon_{1}+\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha^{\ast}(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})}=W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right)^{2}
. Thus,
\left\|\xi_{12}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}<\varepsilon
.

Finally, we collect how a
deterministic coordinate
—a
Dirac marginal in the last coordinate of the base measure, the situation
arising systematically for epigraphs (cf.

Section

2.5.2
)—interacts with the
Wasserstein geometry:

Lemma A.11

(Splitting off a deterministic coordinate)
.

Let
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
b\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
T
be as in
(
2.9
), so that
{T}_{\#}{\cdot}

maps transport plans (resp., variations) on
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
to transport plans
(resp., variations) on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; we write
\boldsymbol{x}=(x,\beta)
with

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Then:

1.

Optimal transport.
For every

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
, with

\mu\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
every

\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b},\boldsymbol{\mu})

satisfies

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)

and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{x}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x^{\prime}-x\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})(x,x^{\prime})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\absolutevalue{\beta'-b}^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}),

where the last term does not depend on
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. In
particular,

W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b},\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)^{2}=W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\absolutevalue{\beta'-b}^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
;

\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b},\boldsymbol{\mu})

if and only if

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)
;
and every
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)
is of
the form
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
for some

\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b},\boldsymbol{\mu})
.
If, additionally,
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mu\times\delta_{\beta}

for some
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, then

\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b},\mu\times\delta_{\beta})=\left\{{(\pi_{1},b,\pi_{2},\beta)}_{\#}{\gamma}\,|\,\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)\right\},

and every variation in

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b},\mu\times\delta_{\beta})}

has last velocity component almost everywhere equal to
\beta-b
.

2.

Tangency.
A variation
\boldsymbol{\xi}
at

\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}
satisfies

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b})}

if and only if

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
.
Moreover,
{T}_{\#}{\cdot}
is
1
-Lipschitz: for all variations

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}
at

\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}
,

W_{\bar{\mu}}\left({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}}\right)\leq W_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}\right)

and, for all

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
,

W_{2}\left({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}}\right)\leq W_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}\right)
.

3.

Norm and inner product.
Let
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
be a
variation at
\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}
whose last velocity component is
almost everywhere equal to some
c\in\mathbb{R}
. Then,

\left\|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}}^{2}=\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}+c^{2}

and, for every variation
\boldsymbol{\xi}
at

\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}
,

\left\langle\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}}=\left\langle{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+c\,\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right].

(A.1)

Proof A.12
.

Proof.
Throughout, we use that the last coordinate of the first marginal of any
transport plan out of, and of any variation at,
\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}
is
almost everywhere equal to
b
. We prove the three statements separately.

Proof of
1
.
The marginals
of
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
are

{(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})}_{\#}{(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b})}=\bar{\mu}

and
\mu
, so

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)
.
Since
\beta=b
for
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
-almost every

((x,\beta),(x^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}))
, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{x}\right\|^{2}=\left\|x^{\prime}-x\right\|^{2}+\absolutevalue{\beta'-b}^{2}

\boldsymbol{\gamma}\text{-a.e.}
, and integrating yields the
displayed identity, where the last term equals

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\absolutevalue{\beta'-b}^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
by
the marginal constraint and, in particular, does not depend on

\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. Hence, the cost of every

\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b},\boldsymbol{\mu})

is at least

W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\absolutevalue{\beta'-b}^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
.
The bound is attained: let

\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{x^{\prime}}\}_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
be
the disintegration of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
with respect to
\mu

(cf.
[
35
, Theorem 5.3.1]
) and, for

\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)
, let

\boldsymbol{\gamma}
be the law of
((x,b),(x^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}))
under

\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{x^{\prime}}(\beta^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,x^{\prime})
. Then,

\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b},\boldsymbol{\mu})

with
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\gamma
, and its
cost attains the lower bound. This proves the expression for

W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b},\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)^{2}
,
and, with it, both the optimality equivalence (the cost of

\boldsymbol{\gamma}
is minimal if and only if the cost of

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is) and the attainment of
every
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)
as a
projection. If
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mu\times\delta_{\beta}
, the
only transport plan of
\delta_{b}
and
\delta_{\beta}
is
\delta_{(b,\beta)}
, so
every

\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b},\mu\times\delta_{\beta})

equals

{(\pi_{1},b,\pi_{2},\beta)}_{\#}{({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}
;
combined with the optimality equivalence, this yields the characterization of

\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b},\mu\times\delta_{\beta})
.
Finally, the variation induced by such a

\boldsymbol{\gamma}
has last velocity component
\beta-b
almost
surely, since the last coordinates of the two marginals are almost everywhere
b

and
\beta
, respectively.

Proof of
2
.
We start with
the
1
-Lipschitz bounds. For the first, let

\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Gamma^{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2})
:
dropping the last component of the anchor and of the two velocities maps

\boldsymbol{\alpha}
to an element of

\Gamma^{1}({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}})

whose cost is no larger, since

\left\|\upsilon_{1}-\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{2}\right\|^{2}
;
minimizing over
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
yields the bound. The
second bound is analogous, using that
T
is a coordinate projection and,
thus,
1
-Lipschitz, so any coupling of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}
and

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}
pushes forward, via
T
applied to both
components, to a coupling of the images with no larger cost.

Next, observe that, since
T
drops the last component of positions and
velocities alike, for every variation
\boldsymbol{\xi}
at

\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}
and every
\varepsilon\geq 0
, dropping the last
coordinate of both marginals of

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}

yields

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}})}
.
By the optimality equivalence in

1
,

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}

is optimal if and only if

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}})}

is. Hence,
\boldsymbol{\xi}
belongs to the set in
(
2.6
) (with base point
\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}
)
if and only if
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
belongs to the set
in (
2.6
) (with base point
\bar{\mu}
).

For the “only if” direction of the tangency claim, let

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b})}

and let
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in the set in
(
2.6
) with

W_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k},\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)\to 0
.
Then,
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}}
belongs to the set in
(
2.6
) by the previous paragraph, and

W_{\bar{\mu}}\left({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right)\to 0

by the first
1
-Lipschitz bound, so

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
.

For the “if” direction, let

\xi\coloneqq{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}

and let

(\xi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})

with
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi_{k},\xi\right)\to 0
; such a
sequence exists because the tangent space is the

W_{\bar{\mu}}
-closure of the set in
(
2.6
). Since the last position component of

\boldsymbol{\xi}
is deterministic,
\boldsymbol{\xi}
is
characterized by the law of
((x,\upsilon),u)
, where

(x,\upsilon)\sim\xi
and
u\in\mathbb{R}
denotes the last
velocity component; let

\{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{(x,\upsilon)}\}_{(x,\upsilon)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

be the disintegration of this law with respect to
(x,\upsilon)
(cf.

[
35
, Theorem 5.3.1]
). For each
k
, let

\alpha_{k}\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi_{k})

attain
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi,\xi_{k}\right)
, define

\hat{\alpha}_{k}
via

\mathrm{d}\hat{\alpha}_{k}(x,\upsilon,\upsilon_{k},u)\coloneqq\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{(x,\upsilon)}(u)\mathrm{d}\alpha_{k}(x,\upsilon,\upsilon_{k})
,
and let
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}
be the law of

((x,b),(\upsilon_{k},u))
under
\hat{\alpha}_{k}
. Then,

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}
is a variation at
\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}
with

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}}=\xi_{k}
in the set in
(
2.6
) and, thus,
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}

belongs to the set in (
2.6
) by the equivalence
above.
Moreover, let
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}
be the law
of
((x,b),(\upsilon_{k},u),(\upsilon,u))
under

\hat{\alpha}_{k}
, i.e., the pushforward of

\hat{\alpha}_{k}
under the map

(x,\upsilon,\upsilon_{k},u)\mapsto((x,b),(\upsilon_{k},u),(\upsilon,u))

from
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
to

\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
. Writing

\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{k}\coloneqq(\upsilon_{k},u)
and

\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\coloneqq(\upsilon,u)
for the two
velocity slots, which share the last coordinate
u
, the

((x,b),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{k})
-marginal of

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}
is
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}
by the
definition of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k}
, and its

((x,b),\boldsymbol{\upsilon})
-marginal is

\boldsymbol{\xi}
, since the
(x,\upsilon)
-marginal of

\alpha_{k}
is
\xi
and the kernel

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{(x,\upsilon)}
does not depend on

\upsilon_{k}
. Hence,

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\in\Gamma^{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k},\boldsymbol{\xi})

and

\displaystyle W_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{k},\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\left\|\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{k},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}}\left\|\upsilon_{k}-\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\alpha}_{k}(x,\upsilon,\upsilon_{k},u)=W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi_{k},\xi\right)^{2}\to 0,

where the first equality is the change of variables defining

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}
together with

\left\|\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\|^{2}=\left\|\upsilon_{k}-\upsilon\right\|^{2}

(the shared
u
cancels), and the second holds because the

(x,\upsilon,\upsilon_{k})
-marginal of

\hat{\alpha}_{k}
is
\alpha_{k}
, which attains

W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi,\xi_{k}\right)
.
Since the tangent space is

W_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}}
-closed, we conclude

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b})}
.

Proof of
3
.
Since the last
position and velocity components of
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
are
deterministic,
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
is the law of
((x,b),(\upsilon,c))
for

(x,\upsilon)\sim{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}
; in
particular,

\left\|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{b}}^{2}=\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}+c^{2}
.
For the inner product, let

\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Gamma^{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\varsigma})

and write the two velocities as
(\upsilon_{1},u_{1})
and

(\upsilon_{2},u_{2})
. Then,
u_{2}=c
almost everywhere, so

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{1},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{1},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{1},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{2})+c\,\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right],

where the second term does not depend on

\boldsymbol{\alpha}
. Dropping the last component of the anchor
and of the two velocities maps
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
to an
element of

\Gamma^{1}({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}})

with the same value of the first term; conversely, every

\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}})

lifts to an admissible
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
with the same value
of the first term: with

\{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{(x,\upsilon_{1})}\}_{(x,\upsilon_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

the disintegration, with respect to
(x,\upsilon_{1})
, of the law of

((x,\upsilon_{1}),u_{1})
under
\boldsymbol{\xi}
, take the law
of
((x,b),(\upsilon_{1},u_{1}),(\upsilon_{2},c))
under

\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{(x,\upsilon_{1})}(u_{1})\mathrm{d}\alpha(x,\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})
.
Taking the maximum over the couplings yields
(
A.1
).

In words, a deterministic coordinate is “transparent” to the
Wasserstein geometry: it contributes an additive, transport-plan-independent term to
transport costs, it neither creates nor destroys tangency, and it enters
norms and inner products separately from the other coordinates, through the
deterministic velocity
c
and the expectation of the last velocity component.

Appendix B
Proofs for
Section

2

Proof B.1
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.4
.
We prove the statements separately:

1.

Let
\xi_{l}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and
-\xi_{u}\in\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})
. We prove that
\xi_{l}=\xi_{u}
; i.e., if both
\mathcal{J}
and
-\mathcal{J}
admit a regular subgradient, then these coincide (up to the direction of the arrows) and
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at
\bar{\mu}
.
We prove the claim in three
steps: we show that

\left\langle\xi_{l},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+\left\langle-\xi_{u},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq 0\qquad\text{for all }\xi\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})},

(B.1)

first for
\xi\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})

(Step 1) and then, by density, on the whole tangent space (Step 2); we
conclude by evaluating
(
B.1
) at

\xi=\xi_{l}
and
\xi=-\xi_{u}
(Step 3).

Step 1
((
B.1
) in
\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})
).

Let
\xi\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})
.
If
\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=0
, both inner products in (
B.1
) are nonpositive by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality (
Proposition

A.9
10
) and there is nothing to prove. Let then
\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}>0
.
By (
2.6
), there is
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
so that,
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon})
,

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu_{\varepsilon})
for

\mu_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi}
.
In particular,
\varepsilon\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu_{\varepsilon})}
,

W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{\varepsilon}\right)=\varepsilon\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}
, and

\mu_{\varepsilon}\to\bar{\mu}
as

\varepsilon\searrow 0
. Thus, both regular subgradient inequalities (for

\xi_{l}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and

-\xi_{u}\in\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})
) apply
along
(\mu_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon\xi)
. In particular, we
may assume without loss of generality
\mathcal{J}(\mu_{\varepsilon})\in\mathbb{R}
for all

\varepsilon
sufficiently small; otherwise, were
\mathcal{J}(\mu_{\varepsilon})=+\infty
(resp.,
-\infty
) along a
subsequence
\varepsilon\searrow 0
, the difference quotient in the regular subgradient inequality for

-\xi_{u}\in\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})

(resp.,
\xi_{l}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
) would
tend to
-\infty
, contradicting that inequality.
Thus, with
Proposition

A.9
4
, we get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})-\left\langle\xi_{l},\varepsilon\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{\varepsilon}\right)}

\displaystyle\hphantom{{}\leq{}}+\liminf_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\frac{-\mathcal{J}(\mu_{\varepsilon})+\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})-\left\langle-\xi_{u},\varepsilon\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{\varepsilon}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\left(-\frac{\varepsilon\left\langle\xi_{l},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+\varepsilon\left\langle-\xi_{u},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{\varepsilon\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\left\langle\xi_{l},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+\left\langle-\xi_{u},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}};

i.e., (
B.1
) holds on
\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})
.

Step 2 (extension to the tangent space).

Both
\xi\mapsto\left\langle\xi_{l},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}

and

\xi\mapsto\left\langle-\xi_{u},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}

are continuous with respect to
W_{\bar{\mu}}

(
Proposition

A.9
3
).
Since
{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
is the closure with respect
to
W_{\bar{\mu}}
of the set in
(
2.6
), Step 1 extends by continuity and
(
B.1
) holds on all of

{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
.

Step 3 (conclusion).

As regular subgradients, both
\xi_{l}
and
-\xi_{u}
belong to

{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
(cf.

Definition

2.2
1
),
so (
B.1
) applies to both.
Using

\left\langle\xi,\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}

(
Proposition

A.9
10
)
and
\left\|-\xi_{u}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=\left\|\xi_{u}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}
, we obtain

\left\|\xi_{l}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}+\left\langle-\xi_{u},\xi_{l}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq 0

and

\left\langle\xi_{l},-\xi_{u}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+\left\|\xi_{u}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}\leq 0
, and so, by the symmetry of the inner product,

\left\|\xi_{l}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}+\left\|\xi_{u}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}+2\left\langle-\xi_{u},\xi_{l}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq 0.

By
Lemma

A.3
,

\left\langle-\xi_{u},\xi_{l}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=-\min_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{u},\xi_{l})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{u},\upsilon_{l}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{u},\upsilon_{l})\geq-\left\langle\xi_{u},\xi_{l}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}
, the minimum
never exceeding the maximum in (
2.4
) over
the same set of couplings, and so

0\geq\left\|\xi_{l}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}+\left\|\xi_{u}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}-2\left\langle\xi_{u},\xi_{l}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi_{u},\xi_{l}\right)^{2},

where the last equality follows from
Proposition

A.9
1
.
Therefore,
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi_{u},\xi_{l}\right)=0

and, thus,
\xi_{u}=\xi_{l}
; see

[
35
, Proposition 12.4.6]
.

2.

For any
\xi\in\hat{\partial}G(\bar{\mu})
and any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\geq G(\mu)-G(\bar{\mu})\geq\sup_{\varsigma\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}}\left\langle\xi,\varsigma\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)

and so
\xi\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.

3.

This statement follows from
1
.

4.

We prove the two directions separately.

We start by showing that if
\xi\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
satisfies (
2.8
), then
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable with
\xi=\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})
. Indeed, if
\xi
satisfies (
2.8
),
\xi\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.
Since
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at
\bar{\mu}
by assumption,
3
gives
\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})=\{\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})\}
, and therefore
\xi=\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})
.

On the other hand, let
\xi=\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})
.
Then,
\xi\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and, for all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\xi^{\prime}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}
, we have

\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\geq{\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime})+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)
.
Moreover, since also
-\xi\in\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})
, using
Lemma

A.3
we get

\displaystyle-\mathcal{J}(\mu)-(-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}))

\displaystyle\geq{\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(-\xi,\xi^{\prime})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime})+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)

\displaystyle={\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle-\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime})+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)

\displaystyle=-\inf_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime})+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime})+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right).

As a result, we have

{\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime})\leq\inf_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime})+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)
, which is equivalent to

{\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime})=\inf_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime})+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)
.
To conclude, we show that the infimum is attained. The set
\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
is narrowly compact, since it is a set of couplings with fixed marginals
[
35
, Remark 5.2.3]
. Moreover, the map

\alpha\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime})

is continuous on this set
[
35
, Lemma 5.2.4]
. Thus, the infimum is attained and the statement follows.

5.

Abbreviate

\bar{\xi}\coloneqq\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})
. We first prove
the map structure, in four steps, and then (1)–(3).

Step 1 (a max–min identity along tangent directions).

For every variation
\xi
at
\bar{\mu}
,

Lemma

A.3
yields

\left\langle-\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=-\min_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\bar{\xi},\xi)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}).

(B.2)

In particular, since the minimum in
(
B.2
) never exceeds the maximum in
(
2.4
) over the same set of couplings,

\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+\left\langle-\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\geq 0
for every
variation
\xi
at
\bar{\mu}
.
We claim that, conversely,

\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+\left\langle-\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=0\qquad\text{for all }\xi\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}.

(B.3)

Indeed, since
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at

\bar{\mu}
,
Definition

2.3
gives

\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and

-\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})
, so
(
B.1
), established in the
proof of
1
, applies with

\xi_{l}=\xi_{u}=\bar{\xi}
and gives

\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+\left\langle-\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq 0
for all

\xi\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
. This proves
(
B.3
).

Step 2 (the minimum over the self-couplings of the gradient
equals its squared norm).
We claim that

\min_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})=\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}.

(B.4)

As a regular subgradient,
\bar{\xi}
belongs to

{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
(cf.

Definition

2.2
1
),
so evaluating (
B.3
) at

\xi=\bar{\xi}
gives

\left\langle\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+\left\langle-\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=0
. Since

\left\langle\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}

(
Proposition

A.9
10
),
this reads

\left\langle-\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=-\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}
, which is
(
B.4
) in view of
(
B.2
) with

\xi=\bar{\xi}
.

Step 3 (every self-coupling of the gradient is concentrated
on the diagonal).
Let
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi})
be arbitrary.
By (
B.4
), we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})\geq\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}
. For the reverse bound, we integrate
with respect to
\alpha
the pointwise Young inequality

\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\upsilon_{1}\right\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}

(which amounts to

0\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\upsilon_{1}-\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}
).
Since

{\pi_{12}}_{\#}{\alpha}={\pi_{13}}_{\#}{\alpha}=\bar{\xi}
, both

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{1}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})
and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})
equal

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\xi}(\bar{x},\upsilon)=\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}
(cf. (
2.5
)), so
that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})\leq\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}
. Hence, equality holds, and,
expanding the square,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{1}-\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})=2\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}-2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})=0;

i.e.,
\upsilon_{1}=\upsilon_{2}
holds

\alpha\text{-a.e.}
.

Step 4 (the gradient is induced by a map).
Disintegrate

\bar{\xi}
with respect to its first marginal
\bar{\mu}
(cf.

[
35
, Theorem 5.3.1]
): there is a Borel family

\{(\bar{\xi})_{\bar{x}}\}_{\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}(\bar{\xi})_{\bar{x}}(\upsilon)<\infty
for
\bar{\mu}
-almost
every
\bar{x}
, so that
\bar{\xi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta_{\bar{x}}\times(\bar{\xi})_{\bar{x}}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})
. Consider

\alpha^{\ast}\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta_{\bar{x}}\times(\bar{\xi})_{\bar{x}}\times(\bar{\xi})_{\bar{x}}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(\bar{x}),

i.e., the coupling of two copies of
\bar{\xi}
under which the two arrows
are drawn independently from
(\bar{\xi})_{\bar{x}}
, conditionally on the
base point
\bar{x}
. Since

{\pi_{12}}_{\#}{\alpha^{\ast}}={\pi_{13}}_{\#}{\alpha^{\ast}}=\bar{\xi}
, we have
\alpha^{\ast}\in\Gamma^{1}(\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi})
, and Step 3 gives

1=\alpha^{\ast}\left(\left\{\upsilon_{1}=\upsilon_{2}\right\}\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left((\bar{\xi})_{\bar{x}}\times(\bar{\xi})_{\bar{x}}\right)\left(\left\{(\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})\,|\,\upsilon_{1}=\upsilon_{2}\right\}\right)\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(\bar{x}),

so that
(\bar{\xi})_{\bar{x}}\times(\bar{\xi})_{\bar{x}}
is
concentrated on the diagonal for
\bar{\mu}
-almost every
\bar{x}
. For a
probability measure
\theta\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the Fubini–Tonelli theorem
gives

(\theta\times\theta)\left(\left\{\upsilon_{1}=\upsilon_{2}\right\}\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\theta(\{\upsilon\})\mathrm{d}\theta(\upsilon)
, which equals
1
if and
only if
\theta(\{\upsilon\})=1
for
\theta
-almost every

\upsilon
, i.e., if and only if
\theta
is a Dirac measure. Therefore,
for
\bar{\mu}
-almost every
\bar{x}
,

(\bar{\xi})_{\bar{x}}=\delta_{\phi(\bar{x})}
, where

\phi(\bar{x})\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\upsilon\mathrm{d}(\bar{\xi})_{\bar{x}}(\upsilon)
is Borel (by measurability of
the disintegration) and belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\bar{\mu})
, since

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\phi(\bar{x})\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\xi}(\bar{x},\upsilon)=\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}<\infty
. Hence,

\bar{\xi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta_{\bar{x}}\times\delta_{\phi(\bar{x})}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})={(\mathrm{Id},\phi)}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
, as claimed.

Step 5 (consequences).
For (1), let
\xi
be a
variation at
\bar{\mu}
and
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\bar{\xi})
. Its marginal

{\pi_{13}}_{\#}{\alpha}=\bar{\xi}
is
concentrated on the graph
\{(\bar{x},\phi(\bar{x}))\,|\,\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
,
so
\upsilon_{2}=\phi(\bar{x})
holds
\alpha\text{-a.e.}
and, consequently,
\alpha
is the pushforward of

\xi
under
(\bar{x},\upsilon)\mapsto(\bar{x},\upsilon,\phi(\bar{x}))
.
Conversely, this pushforward always
belongs to
\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\bar{\xi})
, which is
therefore a singleton. For (2), observe that, for
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
,

\tau\bar{\xi}={(\mathrm{Id},\tau\phi)}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
(cf.

Definition

2.1
2
) is again
induced by a map, so, by the argument of (1), the unique element of

\Gamma^{1}(\tau\bar{\xi},\xi)
is the pushforward
of
\xi
under
(\bar{x},\upsilon)\mapsto(\bar{x},\tau\phi(\bar{x}),\upsilon)
, whence

\left\langle\tau\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\tau\phi(\bar{x}),\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(\bar{x},\upsilon)=\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\phi(\bar{x}),\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(\bar{x},\upsilon)=\tau\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}},

where the last equality is the case
\tau=1
of the first two equalities.
For (3), let

\mu_{k}\to\bar{\mu}
(without loss of generality,

\mu_{k}\neq\bar{\mu}
for all
k
) and pick any

\xi_{k}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu_{k})}
, so that

\left\|\xi_{k}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{k}\right)
. Since

{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\xi_{k}}=\bar{\mu}
, the Cauchy–Schwarz
inequality in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d};\xi_{k})
gives

\absolutevalue{\int_{\reals^d\times\reals^d} \innerProduct{\phi(\bar x)}{\varTangentReals}\d\varTangent_k(\bar x, \varTangentReals)}\leq(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\phi(\bar{x})\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi_{k}(\bar{x},\upsilon))^{1/2}(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi_{k}(\bar{x},\upsilon))^{1/2}=\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{k}\right)
and, thus, by (2) with

\tau=\pm 1
,

\absolutevalue{\innerProduct{\pm\varTangentFixed}{\varTangent_k}_{\varFixed}}\leq\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{k}\right)
. The subgradient inequalities
for
\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and

-\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})
along

(\mu_{k},\xi_{k})
then give

-\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{k})-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{k}\right)}\leq\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{k})-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{k}\right)}\leq\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}};

in particular,
\mathcal{J}(\mu_{k})\in\mathbb{R}
for
k
sufficiently large
and
\mathcal{J}(\mu_{k})\to\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
. Since the sequence was
arbitrary,
\mathcal{J}
is continuous in Wasserstein at
\bar{\mu}
.

Proof B.2
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.5
.
We prove the statements separately:

1.

See
[
35
, Theorem 10.2.2]
.

2.

See
[
35
, Theorem 10.2.6]
.

3.

Let
\xi\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.
By

1

and

Proposition

2.4
1
,
\hat{\xi}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})}
exists such that
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi,\hat{\xi}\right)=0
. In particular, this implies
\xi=\hat{\xi}
; see
[
35
, Proposition 12.4.6]
.
Since the map

(x,\upsilon)\mapsto(x,x-\upsilon)
is an involution,

\xi={(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma}
for some

\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
yields

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi}=\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
.

4.

Without loss of generality, suppose
W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu}\right)>0
; otherwise
\bar{\mu}=\hat{\mu}
and the optimal transport plan is trivially unique.
We prove the statement via contraposition, i.e., by showing that if
\mathcal{J}
is regularly subdifferentiable at
\bar{\mu}
, then the set of optimal transport plans is a singleton. Suppose
\mathcal{J}
is regularly subdifferentiable at
\bar{\mu}
. Then, there exists
\xi\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.
Now let
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
be arbitrary. By
1
,

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma}

is a regular subgradient of
-\mathcal{J}
at
\bar{\mu}
.
Thus, by
Proposition

2.4
1
and
1
(applied to
\xi\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\tilde{\gamma}}\in-\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})
),
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi,{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\tilde{\gamma}}\right)=0
for all
\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
.
Therefore, if
\gamma,\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
, then

W_{2}\left({(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma},{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\tilde{\gamma}}\right)\leq W_{\bar{\mu}}\left({(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma},{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\tilde{\gamma}}\right)=0.

Since the map
(x,y)\mapsto(x,x-y)
is injective, this implies
\gamma=\tilde{\gamma}
.
Thus,
\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
is a singleton,
which proves the contrapositive and, thus, the statement.

Proof B.3
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.8
.
By
Proposition

2.5
, for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have either
\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu)=\emptyset
or
\emptyset\neq\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu)\subseteq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\hat{\mu})}
.
Consider now any (possibly trivial) sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
converging to
\bar{\mu}
such that
\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})\neq\emptyset
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Such a sequence exists because
\mathcal{P}_{2,\mathrm{abs}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is dense in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(cf.
[
42
, Theorem 2.2.7]
),
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2,\mathrm{abs}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(by
Proposition

2.5
2
), and
\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
converges to
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
(clearly, the squared Wasserstein distance is continuous in Wasserstein).
Specifically, let
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
.
By
Proposition

2.5
3
,

\varsigma_{n}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi_{n}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{n},\hat{\mu})
.
We now claim that, up to a subsequence (not relabeled),
\varsigma_{n}\to\varsigma
for some
\varsigma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
.
By
[
51
, Theorem 5.20]
(see also
[
35
, Proposition 7.1.3]
),

\varsigma_{n}
converges narrowly (up to subsequences) to some
\varsigma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x\right\|^{2}+\left\|y\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\varsigma_{n}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x\right\|^{2}+\left\|y\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\varsigma(x,y),

which proves convergence of the second moment. Thus,
\varsigma_{n}\to\varsigma
.
Since the pushforward via Lipschitz continuous maps is Lipschitz continuous with respect to
W_{2}
, we have
\xi_{n}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\varsigma_{n}}\to{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\varsigma}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})}
.
Moreover, if
\bar{\xi}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
, let
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and

(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be as in the definition of the general
subgradient, so that
\xi_{n}\to\bar{\xi}
;
the argument above provides a subsequence of
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

converging in Wasserstein to an element of

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})}
, and uniqueness
of the Wasserstein limit yields
\bar{\xi}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})}
.
Thus,
\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
is nonempty and contained in
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})}
.

Proof B.4
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.10
.
In virtue of
[
51
, Theorem 4.1]
,
\mathcal{J}
is well defined. We now prove the statements separately.

1.

From the nonnegativity of
c
it directly follows that
\mathcal{J}
is nonnegative.
Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
To show that
\mathcal{J}(\mu)<+\infty
, consider the Taylor expansion of
c
at
(x,y)=(0,0)
:

c(x,y)-c(0,0)\leq\left\langle\nabla_{x}{c}(0,0),x\right\rangle+\left\langle\nabla_{y}{c}(0,0),y\right\rangle+\frac{M}{2}(\left\|x\right\|^{2}+\left\|y\right\|^{2}),

(B.5)

where
M<+\infty
since the Hessian of
c
is bounded. Thus, using Cauchy–Schwarz’s and Young’s inequalities, there exists
C>0
so that

c(x,y)\leq C(1+\left\|x\right\|^{2}+\left\|y\right\|^{2})
. Thus, for any
\gamma\in\Gamma(\mu,\hat{\mu})
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}c(x,y)\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}1+\left\|x\right\|^{2}+\left\|y\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)=C\left(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|y\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\mu}(y)\right)<+\infty

since both
\mu
and
\hat{\mu}
have finite second moment. Thus,
W_{c}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)<+\infty
for all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\mathcal{J}
is proper.
To show continuity, first observe that optimal transport discrepancies are narrowly lower semicontinuous
[
51
, Remark 6.12]
. Since convergence in Wasserstein implies narrow convergence, they are also lower semicontinuous in Wasserstein.
Thus, it suffices to establish upper semicontinuity.
For this, consider the function
{\gamma\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}c(x,y)\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)}
. Since
c
is continuous with bounded Hessian (and thus at most quadratic growth), this function is continuous in Wasserstein.
Moreover, by
Lemma

A.5
, the set-valued map
\mu\mapsto\Gamma(\mu,\hat{\mu})
is nonempty and lower hemicontinuous.
Thus,
\mu\mapsto\mathcal{J}(\mu)=\min_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\mu,\hat{\mu})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}c(x,y)\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)
is upper semicontinuous
[
71
, Lemma 17.29]
. Overall,
\mathcal{J}
is therefore continuous.

2.

We divide the proof into six steps.

Step 1 (
\xi^{+}
and
\xi^{-}
belong to
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
).

Fix
\gamma\in\Gamma_{c}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
and define the variations

\xi^{+}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\gamma}

and

\xi^{-}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},-\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\gamma}
.
Boundedness of the Hessian implies that
\nabla_{x}{c}
is Lipschitz (with some constant
L
). Thus,

\displaystyle\left\|\xi^{+}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\nabla_{x}{c}(\bar{x},\hat{x})\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},\hat{x})

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|\nabla_{x}{c}(0,0)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\nabla_{x}{c}(\bar{x},\hat{x})-\nabla_{x}{c}(0,0)\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},\hat{x})

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|\nabla_{x}{c}(0,0)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+2L^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\bar{x}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\hat{x}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\mu}(\hat{x})\right)<+\infty,

since
\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
; the same bound holds for
\xi^{-}
. Thus, both
\xi^{+}
and
\xi^{-}
have finite second moment and, having first marginal
\bar{\mu}
, belong to
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Step 2 (
\xi^{+}
and
\xi^{-}
belong to the tangent space).

We show that
\xi^{-}\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})
, which is what
Step 3
requires.
Let
M
bound the Hessian of
c
as in (
B.5
) and fix
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{M})
. We claim that the transport plan

\gamma_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\varepsilon\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\gamma}

is optimal for the quadratic cost, i.e.,
\gamma_{\varepsilon}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},{(\pi_{1}-\varepsilon\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\gamma})
. In virtue of
[
51
, Theorem 5.10]
, it suffices to show that the support of
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
is cyclically monotone (its transport cost is finite by the moment bound above). Since
\gamma
is
c
-optimal,
\supp{\gamma}
is
c
-cyclically monotone
[
51
, Theorem 5.10]
. Thus, for every finite family
(\bar{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{1}),\dots,(\bar{x}_{n},\hat{x}_{n})\in\supp{\gamma}
(indices modulo
n
),

\sum_{i=1}^{n}c(\bar{x}_{i},\hat{x}_{i})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}c(\bar{x}_{i+1},\hat{x}_{i}).

(B.6)

With
p_{i}\coloneqq\bar{x}_{i}-\varepsilon\nabla_{x}{c}(\bar{x}_{i},\hat{x}_{i})
and
Q\coloneqq\sum_{i}\left\|\bar{x}_{i+1}-\bar{x}_{i}\right\|^{2}
, the identity
\sum_{i}\left\langle\bar{x}_{i},\bar{x}_{i+1}-\bar{x}_{i}\right\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}Q
gives

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle p_{i},\bar{x}_{i+1}-\bar{x}_{i}\right\rangle=-\tfrac{1}{2}Q-\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle\nabla_{x}{c}(\bar{x}_{i},\hat{x}_{i}),\bar{x}_{i+1}-\bar{x}_{i}\right\rangle.

The Taylor expansion of
c(\cdot,\hat{x}_{i})
(with
M
-bounded Hessian) yields
c(\bar{x}_{i+1},\hat{x}_{i})-c(\bar{x}_{i},\hat{x}_{i})\leq\left\langle\nabla_{x}{c}(\bar{x}_{i},\hat{x}_{i}),\bar{x}_{i+1}-\bar{x}_{i}\right\rangle+\frac{M}{2}\left\|\bar{x}_{i+1}-\bar{x}_{i}\right\|^{2}
; summing and using (
B.6
),

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle\nabla_{x}{c}(\bar{x}_{i},\hat{x}_{i}),\bar{x}_{i+1}-\bar{x}_{i}\right\rangle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(c(\bar{x}_{i+1},\hat{x}_{i})-c(\bar{x}_{i},\hat{x}_{i})\right)-\tfrac{M}{2}Q\geq-\tfrac{M}{2}Q.

Therefore
\sum_{i}\left\langle p_{i},\bar{x}_{i+1}-\bar{x}_{i}\right\rangle\leq-\frac{1}{2}Q+\varepsilon\frac{M}{2}Q=-(1-\varepsilon M)\frac{Q}{2}\leq 0
for
\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{M}
. As cyclical monotonicity is a closed condition and the map
(\bar{x},\hat{x})\mapsto(\bar{x},\bar{x}-\varepsilon\nabla_{x}{c}(\bar{x},\hat{x}))
is continuous, it passes to
\supp{\gamma_{\varepsilon}}
, proving the claim.

For every
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{M})
,
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi^{-}}=\gamma_{\varepsilon}
is quadratically optimal; hence, by (
2.6
) (with
\bar{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{M}
),
\xi^{-}\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
.
Since the tangent space is closed under negation (
Proposition

A.7
with
\tau=-1
), also
\xi^{+}=-\xi^{-}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
.

Step 3 (Superdifferentiability).

We generalize
[
1
, Proposition 2.17(vi)]
to any measure.
Via Taylor’s expansion we have, for any
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

c(x_{3},x_{2})-c(x_{1},x_{2})\leq\left\langle\nabla_{x}{c}(x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3}-x_{1}\right\rangle+\frac{M}{2}\left\|x_{3}-x_{1}\right\|^{2}.

Fix
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\xi\in\Gamma_{c}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
, an optimal transport plan
\xi^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)
, and
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}c(x,\hat{x})-c(\bar{x},\hat{x})\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\hat{x},x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\nabla_{x}{c}(\bar{x},\hat{x}),x-\bar{x}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\hat{x},x)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{M}{2}W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)^{2}.

The integrand pairs the arrow of
{(\pi_{1},\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\xi}
with the arrow of the displacement variation
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\xi^{\prime}}
.
We can minimize over couplings to obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\mu)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}({(\pi_{1},\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\xi},{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\xi^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}),

where we used
\frac{M}{2}W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)^{2}=o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)
and
Lemma

A.3
(applied with
l_{1}=\nabla_{x}{c}
,
l_{2}(\bar{x},x)=x-\bar{x}
, and cost
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
to the variations augmented with their base point).
Thus,

\displaystyle-\mathcal{J}(\mu)

\displaystyle-(-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}))+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq-\min_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}({(\pi_{1},\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\xi},{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\xi^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle=\left\langle{(\pi_{1},-\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\xi},{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\xi^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}},

where the last equality follows from
-\min_{\alpha}f=\max_{\alpha}(-f)
and
Lemma

A.3
(with the map
\upsilon\mapsto-\upsilon
), together with the definition (
2.4
) of the inner product. Since
{(\pi_{1},-\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
by
Step 2
and
\mu
,
\xi^{\prime}
were arbitrary, this shows
{(\pi_{1},-\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})
and, thus,
{(\pi_{1},\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\xi}\in-\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})
.

Step 4 (Subdifferentiability when
\Gamma_{c}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
is a singleton and induced by an optimal transport map
T_{\bar{\mu}}^{\hat{\mu}}
).

The proof is identical to the proof of
[
1
, Proposition 2.17(vi)]
. Indeed, let

\bar{\xi}={(\mathrm{Id},\nabla_{x}{c}\circ(\mathrm{Id},T_{\bar{\mu}}^{\hat{\mu}}))}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}

be the candidate subgradient. For
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, let

\xi={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma}
with
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)
.
Then, since the second coordinate of
\bar{\xi}
is a deterministic function of the first,
\alpha\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\nabla_{x}{c}\circ(\pi_{1},T_{\bar{\mu}}^{\hat{\mu}}\circ\pi_{1}),\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma}

is the unique element in

\Gamma^{1}(\bar{\xi},\xi)
.
This trivializes the inner product to
\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\nabla_{x}{}c(\bar{x},T_{\bar{\mu}}^{\hat{\mu}}(\bar{x})),\hat{x}-\bar{x}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},\hat{x})
, so that we can directly use the argument in
[
1
, Proposition 2.17(vi)]
(in particular, no modifications are needed to deal with the inner product).
Finally,
\bar{\xi}
is the variation
\xi^{+}
of
Step 2
for the
c
-optimal transport plan
{(\mathrm{Id},T_{\bar{\mu}}^{\hat{\mu}})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
, so
\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
by
Step 2
.
Hence,
\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.

Step 5 (Differentiability at absolutely continuous measures).

When
c
has bounded Hessian and
y\mapsto\nabla_{x}{c}(x,y)
is injective, absolute continuity is sufficient for existence and uniqueness of an optimal transport map between
\bar{\mu}
and
\hat{\mu}

[
51
, Theorem 10.28]
. Thus, at all absolutely continuous measures, both the regular sub- and superdifferentials are nonempty. Thus,
Proposition

2.4
implies that
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at all absolutely continuous probability measures.

Step 6 (Generalized subgradient).

Let
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a sequence such that
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
and
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at every
\mu_{n}
, so that
\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})\neq\emptyset
(cf.
Proposition

2.4
). Such a sequence always exists since
\mathcal{P}_{2,\mathrm{abs}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is dense in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Since
\mathcal{J}
is continuous, we also have that
\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})\to\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.
We can then proceed as in the proof of
Proposition

2.8
(together with Lipschitz continuity of
\nabla_{x}{c}
), to conclude that
\partial\mathcal{J}
is nonempty and contained (as a result of the same arguments as in
Proposition

2.5
3
) in
{(\pi_{1},\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{c}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})}
.

3.

By
Step 3
,
{(\pi_{1},\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\gamma}\in-\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})
, and by
Step 4
,
{(\pi_{1},\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\gamma}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
; hence
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at
\bar{\mu}
with
\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})={(\pi_{1},\nabla_{x}{c})}_{\#}{\gamma}
by
Proposition

2.4
.

Proof B.5
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.12
.
We prove the statements separately.

1.

See
[
35
, Proposition 10.4.2]
.

2.

The proposed subgradients are in our definition of tangent space in virtue of
\lambda
-convexity of
V
by an argument analogous to
[
1
, Lemma 2.6]
. Then the result follows from approximation (cf.
[
35
, Proposition 5.1.8]
) and by definition of the general subgradient (cf.
Definition

2.2
), in view of
Proposition

A.1
.

3.

Set
\bar{\xi}\coloneqq{(\mathrm{Id},\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
.
We show that
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at every
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

with
\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})=\bar{\xi}
; when

V\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
has uniformly bounded Hessian, this is

[
1
, Proposition 2.20]
.

Step 1 (linear growth of
\nabla{V}
).

Since
V
is differentiable, it is real-valued, and

W\coloneqq V-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2}
is a finite, differentiable, convex
function with
\nabla{W}=\nabla{V}-\lambda\mathrm{Id}
. The hypothesis
V(x)\leq C(1+\left\|x\right\|^{2})
is one half of a two-sided quadratic
bound; the other half is an explicit quadratic minorant of the
\lambda
-convex

V
. With
g\coloneqq\nabla{W}(0)
, the subgradient inequality for the convex

W
at
0
, namely
W(x)\geq W(0)+\left\langle g,x\right\rangle
, reads

V(x)\geq V(0)+\left\langle g,x\right\rangle+\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\left\|x\right\|^{2}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Since
\left\langle g,x\right\rangle\geq-\tfrac{1}{2}\left\|g\right\|^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\left\|x\right\|^{2}
and

\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\left\|x\right\|^{2}\geq-\tfrac{\absolutevalue{\lambda}}{2}\left\|x\right\|^{2}
, this yields

V(x)\geq-C_{0}(1+\left\|x\right\|^{2})
for some
C_{0}>0
; combined with the upper bound
(enlarging
C_{0}
so that
C_{0}\geq C
),
\absolutevalue{V(x)}\leq C_{0}(1+\left\|x\right\|^{2})
, and hence

\absolutevalue{W(x)}\leq C_{0}^{\prime}(1+\left\|x\right\|^{2})
for some
C_{0}^{\prime}>0
. In particular,

\mathcal{J}(\mu)=\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]\in\mathbb{R}
for all

\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Convexity of
W
together with this bound forces
the linear growth
\left\|\nabla{V}(x)\right\|\leq C_{1}(1+\left\|x\right\|)
for some
C_{1}>0
.
Indeed, the gradient inequality for the convex function
W
gives

\left\langle\nabla{W}(x),h\right\rangle\leq W(x+h)-W(x)
for all
h
. Fix
x
with

\nabla{W}(x)\neq 0
(otherwise the bound below is trivial) and evaluate this
at
h=(1+\left\|x\right\|)\nabla{W}(x)/\left\|\nabla{W}(x)\right\|
, so that

\left\|h\right\|=1+\left\|x\right\|
and hence
\left\|x+h\right\|\leq 1+2\left\|x\right\|
; then

\displaystyle(1+\left\|x\right\|)\left\|\nabla{W}(x)\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla{W}(x),h\right\rangle\leq W(x+h)-W(x)

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}^{\prime}(1+\left\|x+h\right\|^{2})+C_{0}^{\prime}(1+\left\|x\right\|^{2})

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}^{\prime}\big(2+(1+2\left\|x\right\|)^{2}+\left\|x\right\|^{2}\big)\leq 5C_{0}^{\prime}(1+\left\|x\right\|)^{2},

where we used
\absolutevalue{W}\leq C_{0}^{\prime}(1+\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2})
in the second line and

\left\|x+h\right\|\leq 1+2\left\|x\right\|
in the third. Dividing by
1+\left\|x\right\|>0
yields

\left\|\nabla{W}(x)\right\|\leq 5C_{0}^{\prime}(1+\left\|x\right\|)
, which also holds trivially when

\nabla{W}(x)=0
. Since
\nabla{V}=\nabla{W}+\lambda\mathrm{Id}
, we
conclude
\left\|\nabla{V}(x)\right\|\leq 5C_{0}^{\prime}(1+\left\|x\right\|)+\absolutevalue{\lambda}\left\|x\right\|\leq C_{1}(1+\left\|x\right\|)
with
C_{1}\coloneqq 5C_{0}^{\prime}+\absolutevalue{\lambda}
. Consequently
\nabla{V}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\bar{\mu})
, and

\bar{\xi}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\bar{\mu}}
.

Step 2 (first-order estimate).

Fix
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, an optimal transport plan

\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)
, and the induced variation

\xi\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma}
,
so that
{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\xi}=\bar{\mu}
and

\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)
. Writing

R(x,\upsilon)\coloneqq V(x+\upsilon)-V(x)-\left\langle\nabla{V}(x),\upsilon\right\rangle

and using
{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\xi}=\bar{\mu}
and

\mu={(\pi_{1}+\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi}
,

\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\nabla{V}(x),\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}R(x,\upsilon)\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon).

(B.7)

Since
\bar{\xi}
is induced by the map
\nabla{V}
, every

\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\bar{\xi},\xi)
has
first variation
\nabla{V}(\bar{x})
for
\bar{\mu}
-almost every
\bar{x}
, so

\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\nabla{V}(x),\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)

(cf.
Lemma

A.3
). We claim that the
right-hand side of (
B.7
) is

o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)
as

\mu\to\bar{\mu}
, uniformly over the choice of optimal transport plan. For
s>0
, let

\omega(x,s)\coloneqq\sup_{0<\left\|\upsilon\right\|\leq s}\frac{\absolutevalue{R(x,\varTangentReals)}}{\left\|\upsilon\right\|}.

Differentiability of
V
at
x
gives
\omega(x,s)\searrow 0
as
s\searrow 0
.
Moreover, since
V=W+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2}
with
W
convex, the map

t\mapsto V(x+t\upsilon)
is absolutely continuous with derivative

\left\langle\nabla{V}(x+t\upsilon),\upsilon\right\rangle
, so the
fundamental theorem of calculus gives

V(x+\upsilon)-V(x)=\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle\nabla{V}(x+t\upsilon),\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}t
;
subtracting the constant
\left\langle\nabla{V}(x),\upsilon\right\rangle
yields

R(x,\upsilon)=\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle\nabla{V}(x+t\upsilon)-\nabla{V}(x),\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}t
.
Together with the linear growth of
\nabla{V}
, this gives

\omega(x,s)\leq C_{2}(1+\left\|x\right\|+s)
. By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and

\absolutevalue{R(x,\varTangentReals)}\leq\omega(x,\left\|\upsilon\right\|)\left\|\upsilon\right\|
,

\frac{\absolutevalue{\int_{\reals^d\times\reals^d}R(x,\varTangentReals)\d\varTangent(x,\varTangentReals)}}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\omega(x,\left\|\upsilon\right\|)^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)\right)^{1/2}.

Fix
\delta>0
and split the last integral along
\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|\leq\delta\}

and
\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta\}
. On the first set, monotonicity of
\omega
in

s
and
{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\xi}=\bar{\mu}
give

\int_{\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|\leq\delta\}}\omega(x,\left\|\upsilon\right\|)^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\omega(x,\delta)^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(x)
, which does not depend on

\mu
. On the second set,
\omega(x,s)\leq C_{2}(1+\left\|x\right\|+s)
and
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}

give
\omega(x,\left\|\upsilon\right\|)^{2}\leq C_{3}\big((1+\left\|x\right\|)^{2}+\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\big)

with
C_{3}\coloneqq 2C_{2}^{2}
, whence

\displaystyle\int_{\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta\}}\omega(x,\left\|\upsilon\right\|)^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{3}\int_{\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta\}}(1+\left\|x\right\|)^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+C_{3}\int_{\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta\}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon).

The last integral is at most
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)=W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)^{2}
,
which vanishes as
\mu\to\bar{\mu}
. For the first, fix
R>0
and split

\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta\}
according to whether
\left\|x\right\|\leq R
or
\left\|x\right\|>R
. We first bound the
\xi
-measure of the set

\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta\}
. At every point
(x,\upsilon)
,

\mathbf{1}_{\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta\}}(x,\upsilon)\leq\frac{\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}}{\delta^{2}},

since the left-hand side is
1
when
\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta
, and then

\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}/\delta^{2}>1
, and
0
otherwise, while the right-hand side is
nonnegative. Integrating this pointwise inequality against
\xi
and recalling

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)=W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)^{2}
,

\displaystyle\xi(\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta\})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta\}}(x,\upsilon)\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)=\frac{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)^{2}}{\delta^{2}}.

Using this and
{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\xi}=\bar{\mu}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta\}}(1+\left\|x\right\|)^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)

\displaystyle\leq(1+R)^{2}\,\xi(\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta\})+\int_{\{\left\|x\right\|>R\}}(1+\left\|x\right\|)^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1+R)^{2}}{\delta^{2}}W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)^{2}+\int_{\{\left\|x\right\|>R\}}(1+\left\|x\right\|)^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(x).

For each fixed
R
the first term vanishes as
\mu\to\bar{\mu}
, so

\limsup_{\mu\to\bar{\mu}}\int_{\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta\}}(1+\left\|x\right\|)^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)\leq\int_{\{\left\|x\right\|>R\}}(1+\left\|x\right\|)^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(x).

Since
(1+\left\|x\right\|)^{2}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\bar{\mu})
(as
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
),
letting
R\to\infty
sends the right-hand side to
0
; consequently

\int_{\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>\delta\}}\omega(x,\left\|\upsilon\right\|)^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)\to 0

as
\mu\to\bar{\mu}
. Hence, for every
\delta>0
,

\limsup_{\mu\to\bar{\mu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\omega(x,\left\|\upsilon\right\|)^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\omega(x,\delta)^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(x),

and letting
\delta\searrow 0
(dominated convergence, with dominating function

C_{2}^{2}(2+\left\|x\right\|)^{2}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\bar{\mu})
) shows this
\limsup
is
0
.
With (
B.7
), we conclude

\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})=\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right).

(B.8)

Step 3 (tangency of
\bar{\xi}
and
-\bar{\xi}
).

For
\varepsilon>0
with
1+\varepsilon\lambda>0
, the function

\frac{1}{2}\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2}+\varepsilon V=\varepsilon W+\frac{1+\varepsilon\lambda}{2}\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2}
is convex, so its gradient

\mathrm{Id}+\varepsilon\nabla{V}
is an optimal transport map

[
52
, Theorem 1.48]
; that is,

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\bar{\xi}}={(\mathrm{Id},\mathrm{Id}+\varepsilon\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
is optimal
for all such
\varepsilon
, and (
2.6
) yields

\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
. For
-\bar{\xi}
we
argue by approximation, since
\frac{1}{2}\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2}-\varepsilon V
need not be
convex when
\nabla{V}
is not Lipschitz. For
\tau>0
, let

W_{\tau}\coloneqq e_{\tau}W
be the Moreau envelope of the convex function
W

and set
V_{\tau}\coloneqq W_{\tau}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2}
. Then
W_{\tau}
is convex
and continuously differentiable, with
\tfrac{1}{\tau}
-Lipschitz gradient

\nabla{W_{\tau}}=\tfrac{1}{\tau}(\mathrm{Id}-P_{\tau}W)

[
63
, Proposition 12.30]
, so
V_{\tau}
is differentiable with

L_{\tau}
-Lipschitz gradient
\nabla{V_{\tau}}=\nabla{W_{\tau}}+\lambda\mathrm{Id}
,
where
L_{\tau}\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{\tau}+\absolutevalue{\lambda}
. As above,

\frac{1}{2}\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2}-\varepsilon V_{\tau}
is convex for
\varepsilon\leq 1/L_{\tau}
, so

\mathrm{Id}-\varepsilon\nabla{V_{\tau}}
is an optimal transport map and

{(\mathrm{Id},-\nabla{V_{\tau}})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
.
Finally, as
\tau\searrow 0
,
\nabla{W_{\tau}}(x)\to\nabla{W}(x)
for every

x
, with
\left\|\nabla{W_{\tau}}(x)\right\|\leq\left\|\nabla{W}(x)\right\|

[
63
, Proposition 12.30]
; hence

\nabla{V_{\tau}}\to\nabla{V}
pointwise and, by dominated convergence (using
the linear growth of
\nabla{V}
and
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
), in

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\bar{\mu})
. Since

W_{\bar{\mu}}\left({(\mathrm{Id},-\nabla{V_{\tau}})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}},-\bar{\xi}\right)\leq\left\|\nabla{V_{\tau}}-\nabla{V}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\bar{\mu})}\to 0
and the
tangent space is closed with respect to
W_{\bar{\mu}}
, we obtain

-\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
.

Conclusion.

By (
B.8
) and

\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
, for all

\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and all

\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}

we have
\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\geq\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)
,
i.e.,
\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
. Since

\bar{\xi}
is induced by a map,

\left\langle-\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=-\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}
,
so negating (
B.8
) and using

-\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
gives

-\mathcal{J}(\mu)-(-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}))\geq\left\langle-\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)
,
i.e.,
-\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})
. Therefore

\bar{\xi}\in-\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})\cap\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
,
and by
Definition

2.3
the functional
\mathcal{J}
is
differentiable at
\bar{\mu}
with

\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})=\bar{\xi}={(\mathrm{Id},\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
.

Proof B.6
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.14
.
We prove the statements separately.

1.

See
[
1
, Proposition 2.21(i)-(ii)]
.

2.

The proof is analogous to
[
1
, Proposition 2.21(v)]
. However, the last step there uses that
\nabla{U}
is odd, which requires
U
to be even. We therefore repeat the same argument for completeness and keep the antisymmetrized expression.
Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and let
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)
.
Define
\xi\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}U(x-y)\mathrm{d}(\mu\times\mu)(x,y)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}U(\bar{x}-\bar{y})\mathrm{d}(\bar{\mu}\times\bar{\mu})(\bar{x},\bar{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(U(x-y)-U(\bar{x}-\bar{y})\right)\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},x)\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{y},y).

Since
U\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with bounded Hessian, there exists
M>0
such that

\left\|\nabla^{2}U(z)\right\|\leq M
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Thus, by Taylor expansion,

\displaystyle U(x-y)-U(\bar{x}-\bar{y})

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla{U}(\bar{x}-\bar{y}),(x-y)-(\bar{x}-\bar{y})\right\rangle+R(x,y,\bar{x},\bar{y}),

with

|R(x,y,\bar{x},\bar{y})|\leq\frac{M}{2}\left\|(\bar{x}-x)-(\bar{y}-y)\right\|^{2}
.
Since

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|(\bar{x}-x)-(\bar{y}-y)\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},x)\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{y},y)

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\left\|\bar{x}-x\right\|^{2}+\left\|\bar{y}-y\right\|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},x)\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{y},y)

\displaystyle=4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\bar{x}-x\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},x)=4W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)^{2},

we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\nabla{U}(\bar{x}-\bar{y}),(x-\bar{x})-(y-\bar{y})\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},x)\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{y},y)

\displaystyle+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right).

Next, we split the first-order term to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\nabla{U}(\bar{x}-\bar{y}),(x-\bar{x})-(y-\bar{y})\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},x)\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{y},y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla{U}(\bar{x}-\bar{y})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(\bar{y}),x-\bar{x}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},x)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla{U}(\bar{x}-\bar{y})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(\bar{x}),y-\bar{y}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{y},y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla{U}(\bar{x}-\bar{y})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(\bar{y}),x-\bar{x}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},x)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla{U}(\bar{y}-\bar{x})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(\bar{y}),x-\bar{x}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle T_{\bar{\mu}}(\bar{x}),x-\bar{x}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},x),

where
T_{\bar{\mu}}(\bar{x})\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\nabla{U}(\bar{x}-\bar{y})-\nabla{U}(\bar{y}-\bar{x})\right)\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(\bar{y})
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle T_{\bar{\mu}}(\bar{x}),x-\bar{x}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\gamma(\bar{x},x)+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle T_{\bar{\mu}}(\bar{x}),\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(\bar{x},\upsilon)+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right).

Moreover, since
U
has bounded Hessian,
\nabla{U}
has at most linear growth, and therefore there exists
C>0
such that

\left\|T_{\bar{\mu}}(x)\right\|\leq C\left(1+\left\|x\right\|+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|y\right\|\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(y)\right),

which implies
T_{\bar{\mu}}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\bar{\mu})
.
Moreover,
T_{\bar{\mu}}=\nabla{W}
with

W(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(U(x-y)+U(y-x)\right)\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(y)
,
where differentiation under the integral sign is justified by the linear growth of

\nabla{U}
;
W
is twice continuously differentiable with

\left\|\nabla^{2}W(x)\right\|\leq M
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, so for
0<\varepsilon<1/M
the
map
\mathrm{Id}+\varepsilon T_{\bar{\mu}}
is the gradient of a convex function and
hence an optimal transport map
[
52
, Theorem 1.48]
. Thus,

{(\mathrm{Id},T_{\bar{\mu}})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
belongs to the set in
(
2.6
) and, in particular, to

{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
; the same argument applied to

-T_{\bar{\mu}}
gives

{(\mathrm{Id},-T_{\bar{\mu}})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
.
For any

\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}({(\mathrm{Id},T_{\bar{\mu}})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}},\xi)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon^{\prime},\upsilon\right\rangle

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon^{\prime},\upsilon)+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle T_{\bar{\mu}}(\bar{x}),\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(\bar{x},\upsilon)+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}),

where the first equality uses that

{\pi_{12}}_{\#}{\alpha}
is induced by the map

T_{\bar{\mu}}
, so that
\upsilon^{\prime}=T_{\bar{\mu}}(\bar{x})
for

\alpha
-almost every
(\bar{x},\upsilon^{\prime},\upsilon)
.
Since the above holds for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and every

\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)
, we conclude that

{(\mathrm{Id},T_{\bar{\mu}})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\cap-\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})

and, thus,
\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})={(\mathrm{Id},T_{\bar{\mu}})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
.

3.

If
U
is even, then
\nabla U
is odd. Then,
\nabla U(y-x)=-\nabla U(x-y)
and, thus,

T_{\bar{\mu}}(\bar{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla U(\bar{x}-\bar{y})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(\bar{y})=(\nabla U\ast\bar{\mu})(\bar{x}).

It follows that
\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})={(\mathrm{Id},(\nabla U\ast\bar{\mu}))}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
.

Proof B.7
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.16
.
We prove the statements separately.

1.

Let
\xi_{1}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}),\xi_{2}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{2}(\bar{\mu})
. Then, for all
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
, and all
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}\Bigl(\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)+\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu)\right)-\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})+\mathcal{J}_{2}(\bar{\mu})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\left\langle\xi_{1}\,+_{\alpha}\,\xi_{2},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\frac{1}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}\Bigl(\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)+\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu)\right)-\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})+\mathcal{J}_{2}(\bar{\mu})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\left\langle\xi_{1},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}-\left\langle\xi_{2},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\Bigr),

where the inequality follows from
Proposition

A.9
5
. Then,

\displaystyle\liminf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\to\bar{\mu}\\
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}\Bigl(\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)+\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu)\right)-\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})+\mathcal{J}_{2}(\bar{\mu})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\left\langle\xi_{1}\,+_{\alpha}\,\xi_{2},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\to\bar{\mu}\\
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})-\left\langle\xi_{1},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\liminf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\to\bar{\mu}\\
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}_{2}(\bar{\mu})-\left\langle\xi_{2},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}\geq 0.

This shows that
\xi_{1}\,+_{\alpha}\,\xi_{2}\in\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})
, proving the desired inclusion.
When
\mathcal{J}_{2}
is differentiable at
\bar{\mu}
, we can show the other direction:
Since
\mathcal{J}_{2}
is differentiable at
\bar{\mu}
, it is finite on a
Wasserstein neighborhood of
\bar{\mu}
; because regular subgradients at
\bar{\mu}

depend only on the values of the functional near
\bar{\mu}
, we may assume without
loss of generality that
\mathcal{J}_{2}
is real-valued, so that
-\mathcal{J}_{2}
is
proper and
\mathcal{J}_{1}=\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2}-\mathcal{J}_{2}
.
In view of the above inclusion, we have

\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})=\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2}-\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})\supseteq\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})+\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})=\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})+\{-\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})\}

and, thus,

\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})+\{\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})\}\supseteq\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})+\{-\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})\}+\{\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})\}.

For every

\xi\in\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})

and every

\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,-\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu}))
,
the coupling

\alpha^{\prime}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}+\pi_{3},-\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha}

belongs to

\Gamma^{1}(\xi\,+_{\alpha}\,(-\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})),\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu}))

and satisfies

(\xi\,+_{\alpha}\,(-\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})))\,+_{\alpha^{\prime}}\,\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\alpha}=\xi
,
so that
