\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})+\{-\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})\}+\{\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})\}\supseteq\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})
, which gives the desired inclusion.
It remains to establish the equality for the general
subgradients, i.e.,

\partial(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})=\partial\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})+\{\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})\}
. We first record three facts.
(i) continuity at
\bar{\mu}
(convergence in Wasserstein in the argument and in the value) of the map

\mu\mapsto\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\mu)
presupposes
that this map is defined on a Wasserstein neighborhood of
\bar{\mu}
,
i.e., that
\mathcal{J}_{2}
is differentiable there. In particular, the
equality for the regular subgradients established above holds at every

\nu
in this neighborhood with
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\nu)\in\mathbb{R}
:
its proof uses only that

\mathcal{J}_{1}(\nu),\mathcal{J}_{2}(\nu)\in\mathbb{R}
and that

\mathcal{J}_{2}
is differentiable at
\nu
.
(ii) By

Proposition

2.4
5
,

\bar{\xi}\coloneqq\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})
is
induced by a map, for every variation
\xi
at
\bar{\mu}
the
set
\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\bar{\xi})
is a
singleton (so the sums in
\partial\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})+\{\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})\}
do not depend on the choice of
the coupling, and we write
\xi\,+\,\bar{\xi}
),
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
is continuous in Wasserstein at
\bar{\mu}
.
(iii) Let
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
, let

\xi_{n},\varsigma_{n}
be variations at
\mu_{n}
with

\xi_{n}\to\xi
and

\varsigma_{n}\to\varsigma
, and suppose
that
\varsigma
is induced by a map. Then, for any choice of

\alpha_{n}\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{n},\varsigma_{n})
, the
sequence
(\alpha_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in
Wasserstein to the unique element
\alpha
of

\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\varsigma)
, and

\xi_{n}\,+_{\alpha_{n}}\,\varsigma_{n}\to\xi\,+_{\alpha}\,\varsigma
. Indeed,

(\alpha_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight, because its

(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
- and

(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})
-marginals converge in
Wasserstein and are therefore tight; every narrow limit point has marginals

\xi
and
\varsigma
(pushforwards through projections
commute with narrow limits) and thus equals
\alpha
, which
is unique because
\varsigma
is induced by a map, say by
\psi
:
every element of

\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\varsigma)
is
concentrated on
\{\upsilon_{2}=\psi(\bar{x})\}
and is therefore
the pushforward of
\xi
under
(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1})\mapsto(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\psi(\bar{x}))
(cf. the proof of

Proposition

2.4
5
).
Hence,

\alpha_{n}\rightharpoonup\alpha
, and the
second moments converge, since they split along the marginals:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{x}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\upsilon_{1}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{n}(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\bar{x}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\upsilon_{1}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi_{n}(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\varsigma_{n}(\bar{x},\upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\bar{x}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\upsilon_{1}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\varsigma(\bar{x},\upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\bar{x}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\upsilon_{1}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}),

so
\alpha_{n}\to\alpha

(cf.
[
35
, Proposition 7.1.5]
); the sums converge
because the map
(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})\mapsto(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1}+\upsilon_{2})
is Lipschitz.

We now prove the inclusion
\supseteq
. Let

\xi\in\partial\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})
and take sequences

\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
with

\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n})\to\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})
and

\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n})\ni\xi_{n}\to\xi
(cf.

Definition

2.2
2
).
For
n
sufficiently large,
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{R}
and

\mathcal{J}_{2}
is differentiable at
\mu_{n}
with

\bar{\xi}_{n}\coloneqq\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\mu_{n})\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu_{n})
(cf.

Proposition

2.4
3
);
moreover,
\bar{\xi}_{n}\to\bar{\xi}
by
hypothesis. Fix any
\alpha_{n}\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{n},\bar{\xi}_{n})
(nonempty,
e.g., by the gluing lemma
[
51
, Gluing Lemma]
). By the
sum rule proved above, applied at
\mu_{n}
,

\xi_{n}\,+_{\alpha_{n}}\,\bar{\xi}_{n}\in\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\mu_{n})
, and, by
(iii),
\xi_{n}\,+_{\alpha_{n}}\,\bar{\xi}_{n}\to\xi\,+\,\bar{\xi}
. Since,
by (ii),
(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\mu_{n})\to(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})
, we conclude

\xi\,+\,\bar{\xi}\in\partial(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})
, which proves

\supseteq
(recall from (ii) that the sum does not depend on the
coupling).

For the inclusion
\subseteq
, let
\varsigma\in\partial(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})
and take sequences

\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
with

(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\mu_{n})\to(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\bar{\mu})
and

\hat{\partial}(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\mu_{n})\ni\varsigma_{n}\to\varsigma
. For
n

sufficiently large,
\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{R}
(by
differentiability) and
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n})=(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\mu_{n})-\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{R}
, so, by (i), there are

\xi_{n}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n})
and

\alpha_{n}\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{n},\bar{\xi}_{n})
, with

\bar{\xi}_{n}\coloneqq\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\mu_{n})
,
so that

\varsigma_{n}=\xi_{n}\,+_{\alpha_{n}}\,\bar{\xi}_{n}
. The couplings

\tilde{\alpha}_{n}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}+\pi_{3},\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha_{n}}\in\Gamma^{1}(\varsigma_{n},\bar{\xi}_{n})
satisfy
the assumptions of (iii), since

\varsigma_{n}\to\varsigma
and

\bar{\xi}_{n}\to\bar{\xi}
by
hypothesis; hence,

\tilde{\alpha}_{n}\to\tilde{\alpha}
, the unique element of

\Gamma^{1}(\varsigma,\bar{\xi})
. Since the
map
(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})\mapsto(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1}-\upsilon_{2})
is Lipschitz,

\xi_{n}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}\to\xi\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\tilde{\alpha}}
. Moreover, by (ii),

\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n})=(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})(\mu_{n})-\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu_{n})\to\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})
, so

\xi\in\partial\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})
. Finally,

\alpha\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{3},\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\bar{\xi})
and

\xi\,+_{\alpha}\,\bar{\xi}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\tilde{\alpha}}=\varsigma
, so

\varsigma\in\partial\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})+\{\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\bar{\mu})\}
. This establishes the desired
equality.

2.

We first prove the statement for the regular subgradient.
To ease notation, let
\alpha\coloneqq\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})<+\infty
and
c\coloneqq g^{\prime}(\alpha)>0
.

To start, we show that

c\,\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\hat{\partial}(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu}).

To this end, let
\xi\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})
and define, for
\mu
near
\bar{\mu}
,

q(\mu)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\dfrac{g(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu))-g(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}))}{\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})},&\text{if }\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)\neq\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}),\\[5.16663pt]
c,&\text{if }\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)=\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}).\end{cases}

Since
\mathcal{J}_{1}
is continuous in Wasserstein at
\bar{\mu}
and
g
is differentiable at
\alpha
, we have

q(\mu)\to c

as

\mu\to\bar{\mu}.

Now let

\varsigma\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}.

Then,

\displaystyle\frac{g(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu))-g(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}))-\left\langle c\xi,\varsigma\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}

\displaystyle=q(\mu)\,\frac{\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})-\left\langle\xi,\varsigma\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}+\bigl(q(\mu)-c\bigr)\frac{\left\langle\xi,\varsigma\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}.

By Cauchy–Schwarz,

\absolutevalue{\innerProduct{\varTangent}{\varTangentOther}_{\varFixed}}\leq\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\,\left\|\varsigma\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\,W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right),

so that

\bigl(q(\mu)-c\bigr)\frac{\left\langle\xi,\varsigma\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}\to 0\qquad\text{as }\mu\to\bar{\mu}.

Since
q(\mu)\to c>0
and
\xi\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\liminf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\to\bar{\mu}\\
\varsigma\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}\Bigl(g(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu))-g(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}))

\displaystyle\qquad-\left\langle c\xi,\varsigma\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\Bigr)\geq 0.

Hence,

c\,\xi\in\hat{\partial}(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu}),

which proves the desired inclusion.

We now prove the reverse inclusion. Since
g^{\prime}(\alpha)>0
and
g
is continuously differentiable, there exists an open interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
containing
\alpha
such that
g
is strictly increasing on
I
. Therefore,
g_{|I}
admits an inverse

h:g(I)\to I,

which is continuously differentiable at
g(\alpha)
and satisfies

h^{\prime}(g(\alpha))=\frac{1}{g^{\prime}(\alpha)}=\frac{1}{c}.

We extend
h
to a map
\bar{\mathbb{R}}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
that is
continuously differentiable near
g(\alpha)
, strictly increasing, and satisfies

h(+\infty)=+\infty
; since
h\circ g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1}
then coincides
with
\mathcal{J}_{1}
on a Wasserstein neighborhood of
\bar{\mu}
, and regular
subgradients at
\bar{\mu}
depend only on the values of the functional near

\bar{\mu}
, the equality below holds.
Because
\mathcal{J}_{1}
is continuous at
\bar{\mu}
, after possibly restricting to a Wasserstein neighborhood of
\bar{\mu}
we may assume that
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)\in I
, so that

\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)=h(g(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)))

for all
\mu\text{ close enough to }\bar{\mu}
.
Applying the inclusion already proved to the functional
g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1}
and the map
h
, we obtain

\frac{1}{c}\,\hat{\partial}(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\hat{\partial}(h\circ g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})=\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}).

Therefore,

\hat{\partial}(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})\subseteq c\,\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}).

This proves the reverse inclusion and thus

\hat{\partial}(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})=g^{\prime}(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}))\,\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}).

We now establish the statement for the general subgradient, under
the additional hypothesis that
\mathcal{J}_{1}
is continuous in Wasserstein on a
neighborhood of
\bar{\mu}
(cf. the statement).
To start, we collect the ingredients. Since
\mathcal{J}_{1}
is continuous at

\bar{\mu}
, for every sequence
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
we
have
\alpha_{n}\coloneqq\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n})\to\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
; after
possibly shrinking the interval
I
introduced above so that
g^{\prime}>0
on
I

(possible, since
g^{\prime}
is continuous at
\alpha
with
g^{\prime}(\alpha)=c>0
), for
n

sufficiently large we have
\alpha_{n}\in I
,

c_{n}\coloneqq g^{\prime}(\alpha_{n})>0
with
c_{n}\to c
, and

g(\alpha_{n})\to g(\alpha)\in\mathbb{R}
. Since, moreover,
\mathcal{J}_{1}
is
continuous at
\mu_{n}
for
n
sufficiently large, the argument used above for the regular subgradients applies verbatim
at
\mu_{n}
(with the same interval
I
and the same extension of

h=(g_{|I})^{-1}
, which is continuously differentiable near
g(\alpha_{n})
for

n
sufficiently large) and yields

\hat{\partial}(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\mu_{n})=c_{n}\,\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n}).

We also record that scaled variations converge: if
\lambda_{n}\to\lambda
in

\mathbb{R}
and
\xi_{n}\to\xi
, then

\lambda_{n}\xi_{n}\to\lambda\xi
, since

W_{2}\left(\lambda_{n}\xi_{n},\lambda_{n}\xi\right)\leq\max(1,\absolutevalue{\lambda_n})W_{2}\left(\xi_{n},\xi\right)
(the
map
(\bar{x},\upsilon)\mapsto(\bar{x},\lambda_{n}\upsilon)
is

\max(1,\absolutevalue{\lambda_n})
-Lipschitz) and

W_{2}\left(\lambda_{n}\xi,\lambda\xi\right)^{2}\leq(\lambda_{n}-\lambda)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(\bar{x},\upsilon)
.

Let now
\varsigma\in\partial(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})
and take sequences

\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
with

g(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n}))\to g(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}))
and

\hat{\partial}(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\mu_{n})\ni\varsigma_{n}\to\varsigma
(cf.

Definition

2.2
2
). For
n

sufficiently large,
\xi_{n}\coloneqq c_{n}^{-1}\varsigma_{n}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n})
by the display above, and

\xi_{n}\to c^{-1}\varsigma
since

c_{n}^{-1}\to c^{-1}>0
. As
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n})\to\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})
by continuity of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
at
\bar{\mu}
, we
conclude
c^{-1}\varsigma\in\partial\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})
, i.e.,

\varsigma=c\left(c^{-1}\varsigma\right)\in g^{\prime}(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}))\,\partial\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})
.
Conversely, let
\xi\in\partial\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})
and take
sequences
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
with

\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n})\to\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})
and

\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n})\ni\xi_{n}\to\xi
. For
n
sufficiently large,

c_{n}\xi_{n}\in\hat{\partial}(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\mu_{n})
by the display above,

c_{n}\xi_{n}\to c\xi
, and

g(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{n}))\to g(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}))
by continuity of

g
on
I
, so
c\xi\in\partial(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})
. The two inclusions prove the
claim.

3.

Let
\alpha\coloneqq\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}
,

c\coloneqq g^{\prime}(\alpha)\in\mathbb{R}
(possibly nonpositive), and

\bar{\xi}\coloneqq\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{1}}(\bar{\mu})
, which is induced
by a map by

Proposition

2.4
5
;
in particular,

\left\langle\tau\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\tau\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}
for all

\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and all variations
\xi
at
\bar{\mu}
. Define
q
as in
the proof of
2
; the argument there shows that

q(\mu)\to c
as
\mu\to\bar{\mu}
(it uses
only continuity of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
at
\bar{\mu}
and differentiability of
g
at

\alpha
, not the sign of
c
), so
q
is bounded on a Wasserstein neighborhood of

\bar{\mu}
. By

Proposition

2.4
4
,
for all
\mu
in a Wasserstein neighborhood of
\bar{\mu}
on which

\mathcal{J}_{1}
is real-valued and
q
is defined (which suffices, as the

o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)
statements below only
constrain the behavior as
\mu\to\bar{\mu}
) and all

\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}
,

\displaystyle(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\mu)-(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})

\displaystyle=q(\mu)\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})\right)

\displaystyle=q(\mu)\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)

\displaystyle=c\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+\left(q(\mu)-c\right)\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}

\displaystyle\qquad+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left\langle c\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}+o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right),

where the second equality holds because
q
is bounded near
\bar{\mu}
, and the
last one because

\left(q(\mu)-c\right)\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=o\left(W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)\right)
(by

q(\mu)\to c
together with

\absolutevalue{\innerProduct{\varTangentFixed}{\varTangent}_{\varFixed}}\leq\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)
,
where the two-sided bound holds since
\bar{\xi}
is induced by a map, so
the inner product is a single integral to which the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
applies)
and because
c\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\left\langle c\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}
for any sign of
c
, as

\bar{\xi}
is induced by a map. Next, both
c\bar{\xi}
and

-c\bar{\xi}
belong to
{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
: by

Definition

2.3
and

Definition

2.2
1
, both

\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu})
and

-\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})
lie in the
tangent space, and
\pm c\bar{\xi}\in\{\absolutevalue{c}\bar{\xi},\absolutevalue{c}(-\bar{\xi})\}
, so

Proposition

A.7
applies. Therefore, the display
above shows
c\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})(\bar{\mu})
and, after negating it
and using
\left\langle-c\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=-\left\langle c\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}
,

-c\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\partial}(-(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1}))(\bar{\mu})
. By

Definition

2.3
and

Proposition

2.4
3
,

g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1}
is differentiable at
\bar{\mu}
with

\nabla{(g\circ\mathcal{J}_{1})}(\bar{\mu})=c\bar{\xi}=g^{\prime}(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bar{\mu}))\,\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{1}}(\bar{\mu})
.

Proof B.8
.

Proof of
Corollary

2.18
.

Step 1 (growth and convexity estimates).

Since
\left\|\nabla^{2}{V}(x)\right\|\leq M
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a
second-order Taylor expansion around
0
gives
\absolutevalue{V(x)}\leq A+B\left\|x\right\|^{2}
for some
A,B>0
, the gradient bound

\left\|\nabla{V}(x)\right\|\leq\left\|\nabla{V}(0)\right\|+M\left\|x\right\|
, and

\lambda
-convexity of
V
with
\lambda=-M
; since
\nabla^{2}{(V^{2})}

is uniformly bounded as well, the same three properties hold for
V^{2}
.
In particular,
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)^{2}\right]
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]
are finite for every

\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}_{1}-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{2}
is real-valued and, since
\absolutevalue{V}
and
V^{2}
are
continuous with at most quadratic growth,
\mathcal{J}_{1}
and

\mathcal{J}_{2}
are continuous in Wasserstein (cf.

Section

2.1
).

Step 2 (differentiability of the two terms).

By
Proposition

2.12
, applied to
V
and
to
V^{2}
(both proper and continuous, hence lower semicontinuous, and, by
Step 1,
\lambda
-convex, differentiable, and bounded above by
A+B\left\|x\right\|^{2}
), the functionals
\mathcal{J}_{2}
and
\mathcal{J}_{1}

are differentiable at every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with
gradients
\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}}(\mu)={(\mathrm{Id},\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\mu}
and

\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{1}}(\mu)={(\mathrm{Id},2V\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\mu}
. With
g(t)=t^{2}
(so that
g(+\infty)=+\infty
and
g
is continuously
differentiable) and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
continuous in Wasserstein by Step 1, the
chain rule for the differentiable case
(
Proposition

2.16
3
)
shows that
\mathcal{J}_{2}^{2}=g\circ\mathcal{J}_{2}
is differentiable at
every
\mu
, with gradient

2\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]{(\mathrm{Id},\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\mu}={(\mathrm{Id},2\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\mu}
; by

Definition

2.3
,
-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{2}
is then
differentiable as well, with gradient
{(\mathrm{Id},-2\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[V(x)\right]\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\mu}
.

Step 3 (sum rule).

By the sum rule
(
Proposition

2.16
1
), applied to

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}_{1}+(-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{2})
(with
\mathcal{J}_{1}

continuous in Wasserstein by Step 1),

\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\ni\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{1}}(\bar{\mu})\,+_{\alpha}\,\nabla{(-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{2})}(\bar{\mu})
; applied to
-\mathcal{J}=(-\mathcal{J}_{1})+\mathcal{J}_{2}^{2}
, it likewise yields

\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\bar{\mu})\ni\nabla{(-\mathcal{J}_{1})}(\bar{\mu})\,+_{\alpha}\,\nabla{\mathcal{J}_{2}^{2}}(\bar{\mu})
, which, by Step 4 below, is the
negation of the former element. Hence,
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at
every
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(
Definition

2.3
).

Step 4 (the formula).

All the variations above are induced by maps anchored at
\bar{\mu}
, so
in each sum the coupling
\alpha
is unique and the sum is
induced by the pointwise sum of the maps. Therefore,

\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu})={(\mathrm{Id},2V\nabla{V}-2\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\bar{\mu}}\left[V(x)\right]\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}={(\mathrm{Id},2(V-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\bar{\mu}}\left[V(x)\right])\nabla{V})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}.

Proof B.9
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.21
.
First, we notice that
\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu}),\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
: For all
\varsigma\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}},\varsigma\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}},\varsigma)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle=\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}},\varsigma)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle 0,\upsilon_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})=0\leq o\left(\left\|\varsigma\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\right).

In particular,
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu}),\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
are nonempty.
By
Proposition

A.9
4
and
Proposition

A.7
, which guarantees
\lambda\xi\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
, we conclude that
\xi\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
implies
\lambda\xi\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
for all
\lambda\geq 0
, i.e.,
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
is a cone.
The following approximation argument proves the analogous result for
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
. Given any sequences
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
,
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
,
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
so that
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n})
and
\xi_{n}\to\xi
, we have, for all
\phi\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
with at most quadratic growth,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x,y)\mathrm{d}(\lambda\xi_{n})(x,y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x,\lambda y)\mathrm{d}\xi_{n}(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x,\lambda y)\mathrm{d}\xi(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x,y)\mathrm{d}(\lambda\xi)(x,y)
.
Thus,
\lambda\xi_{n}\to\lambda\xi

[
51
, Definition 6.8]
and so
\lambda\xi\in\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
.

To prove that
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
is closed, let
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
with
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi_{n},\xi\right)\to 0
. Since
{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
is closed by definition with respect to
W_{\bar{\mu}}
,
\xi\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
. We show that
\xi\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
.
Let
(\mu_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
with
\mu_{m}\to\bar{\mu}
and

\varsigma_{m}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu_{m})}
. Since
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
, for every fixed
n
we have

\limsup_{m\to\infty}\frac{\left\langle\xi_{n},\varsigma_{m}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{\left\|\varsigma_{m}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}\leq 0.

Let
\alpha_{n}\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi,\xi_{n})
attain
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi,\xi_{n}\right)
and define
\alpha_{n}^{\prime}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},-\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha_{n}}
,
\alpha_{n}^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{3},\pi_{2}-\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha_{n}}
.
Then,
\alpha_{n}^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{n},\xi\,+_{\alpha_{n}^{\prime}}\,(-\xi_{n}))
and

\xi_{n}\,+_{\alpha_{n}^{\prime\prime}}\,\bigl(\xi\,+_{\alpha_{n}^{\prime}}\,(-\xi_{n})\bigr)={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}+\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha_{n}^{\prime\prime}}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{3}+(\pi_{2}-\pi_{3}))}_{\#}{\alpha_{n}}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\alpha_{n}}=\xi.

Thus, by
Proposition

A.9
5
and
10
,

\displaystyle\frac{\left\langle\xi,\varsigma_{m}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{\left\|\varsigma_{m}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}

\displaystyle={\frac{\left\langle\xi_{n}\,+_{\alpha_{n}^{\prime\prime}}\,(\xi\,+_{\alpha_{n}^{\prime}}\,(-\xi_{n})),\varsigma_{m}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{\left\|\varsigma_{m}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\langle\xi_{n},\varsigma_{m}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{\left\|\varsigma_{m}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}+\frac{\left\langle\xi\,+_{\alpha_{n}^{\prime}}\,(-\xi_{n}),\varsigma_{m}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{\left\|\varsigma_{m}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\langle\xi_{n},\varsigma_{m}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{\left\|\varsigma_{m}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}+\left\|\xi\,+_{\alpha_{n}^{\prime}}\,(-\xi_{n})\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}.

Taking the limit superior as
m\to\infty
yields

\limsup_{m\to\infty}\frac{\left\langle\xi,\varsigma_{m}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{\left\|\varsigma_{m}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}\leq\left\|\xi\,+_{\alpha_{n}^{\prime}}\,(-\xi_{n})\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi,\xi_{n}\right).

Finally, letting
n\to\infty
and using
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\xi,\xi_{n}\right)\to 0
, we obtain

\limsup_{m\to\infty}\frac{\left\langle\xi,\varsigma_{m}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{\left\|\varsigma_{m}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}\leq 0.

Since the sequence
(\mu_{m},\varsigma_{m})
is arbitrary, this proves

\xi\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
.
Therefore,
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
is closed.
For the general normal cone, we consider a diagonal argument. Consider
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})\ni\xi_{n}\to\xi
. Then, there are
\mu_{n}^{m}\to\bar{\mu}
and
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n}^{m})\ni\xi_{n}^{m}\to\xi_{n}
. Then,
W_{2}\left(\xi_{n}^{m},\xi\right)\leq W_{2}\left(\xi_{n}^{m},\xi_{n}\right)+W_{2}\left(\xi_{n},\xi\right)
hence, for every
n
we may pick
m(n)
with
W_{2}\left(\xi_{n}^{m(n)},\xi_{n}\right)\leq\frac{1}{n}
and
W_{2}\left(\mu_{n}^{m(n)},\bar{\mu}\right)\leq\frac{1}{n}
, so that
\mu_{n}^{m(n)}\to\bar{\mu}
,
\xi_{n}^{m(n)}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n}^{m(n)})
, and
\xi_{n}^{m(n)}\to\xi
. Therefore,
\xi\in\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
.

Finally, we prove the relationship between
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
and
{\tangent_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})}
.
Let
\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})

and
\xi\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})}
, as in the statement.
By definition of tangent cone (
Definition

2.19
), there exist
sequences
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
with

\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
,

(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}

with
\xi_{n}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu_{n})}
,
and
(\tau_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with
\tau_{n}\searrow 0
,
such that
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\frac{1}{\tau_{n}}\xi_{n},\xi\right)\to 0
.
Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Since
\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})

and
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
, there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}

such that

\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi_{n}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq\varepsilon\left\|\xi_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}

for all
n\geq N
.
Dividing by
\tau_{n}>0
and using
Proposition

A.9
4
,

\frac{\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi_{n}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{\tau_{n}}=\left\langle\bar{\xi},\tfrac{\xi_{n}}{\tau_{n}}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq\varepsilon\left\|\tfrac{\xi_{n}}{\tau_{n}}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\quad\text{for all }n\geq N.

Passing to the limit, using continuity of the inner product
(
Proposition

A.9
3
),
and
\left\|\frac{\xi_{n}}{\tau_{n}}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\to\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}
,

\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\langle\bar{\xi},\tfrac{\xi_{n}}{\tau_{n}}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\varepsilon\left\|\tfrac{\xi_{n}}{\tau_{n}}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=\varepsilon\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}.

Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary,
\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq 0
.

Proof B.10
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.22
.
We prove the statements separately.

1.

It suffices to show that for any generic
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})=\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
, since the limit is then trivially the same.
As already observed in the proof of
Proposition

2.21
,
\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})
.
Thus,
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})\supseteq\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
.
For the other inclusion, let
\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})
. By definition of regular normal cone,
\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
.
Indeed, every element of the tangent space belongs to the tangent
cone of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at
\bar{\mu}
: pick
\bar{\xi}^{k}
in the set of
(
2.6
) with

W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\bar{\xi}^{k},\bar{\xi}\right)\to 0
and

\tau_{k}\searrow 0
below the corresponding
\bar{\varepsilon}
, and set

\mu_{k}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1}+\tau_{k}\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\bar{\xi}^{k}}
; then
\tau_{k}\bar{\xi}^{k}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu_{k})}
,

W_{2}\left(\mu_{k},\bar{\mu}\right)\leq\tau_{k}\left\|\bar{\xi}^{k}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\to 0
, and

W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\tfrac{1}{\tau_{k}}(\tau_{k}\bar{\xi}^{k}),\bar{\xi}\right)\to 0
, as required by
Definition

2.19
.
By
Proposition

2.21
,
\left\langle\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq 0
. Thus, with
Proposition

A.9
10
-
11
,
\left\|\bar{\xi}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}\leq 0
and
\bar{\xi}=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
. This proves
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
, which concludes the proof.

2.

As
\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
(again by the proof of
Proposition

2.21
), it suffices to prove
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
.
Let
\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
.
Pick
\bar{\xi}^{k}
in the set of
(
2.6
) with

W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\bar{\xi}^{k},\bar{\xi}\right)\to 0
and

\tau_{k}\searrow 0
below the corresponding
\bar{\varepsilon}
; then

\mu_{k}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1}+\tau_{k}\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\bar{\xi}^{k}}

satisfies

W_{2}\left(\mu_{k},\bar{\mu}\right)\leq\tau_{k}\left\|\bar{\xi}^{k}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\to 0
,
so
\mu_{k}\in\mathcal{C}
for
k
large because
\bar{\mu}\in\mathrm{int}_{W_{2}}{\mathcal{C}}
; since moreover

\tau_{k}\bar{\xi}^{k}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu_{k})}

and

W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\tfrac{1}{\tau_{k}}(\tau_{k}\bar{\xi}^{k}),\bar{\xi}\right)\to 0
,

Definition

2.19
gives

\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})}
.
Then, the statement follows analogously to
1
.
For
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
, note that any

\mu_{n}\in\mathcal{C}
with

\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
eventually lies in

\mathrm{int}_{W_{2}}{\mathcal{C}}
, so

\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n})=\{\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}\}

for
n
large and the only possible limit is
\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
.

3.

We have that
\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
if and only if
\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
and for all
\mu_{n}=\delta_{x_{n}}\in\mathcal{C}
,
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}=\delta_{\bar{x}}
,
\xi_{n}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu_{n})}=\{\delta_{\bar{x}}\times\delta_{(x_{n}-\bar{x})}\}
we have
\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi_{n}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq o\left(\left\|\xi_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\right)
.
The
requirement
\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
is automatically satisfied: since

\Gamma(\delta_{\bar{x}},\mu)
is a singleton, hence its element is
optimal, for every
\mu
, any
\bar{\xi}
with

{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\bar{\xi}}=\delta_{\bar{x}}
belongs to
(
2.6
).
We now check the regular normal cone inequality (cf.
Definition

2.20
):

\displaystyle 0\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi_{n}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{\left\|\xi_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\left\|x_{n}-\bar{x}\right\|}\max_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\bar{\xi},\xi_{n})}

\displaystyle\hphantom{{}={}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,\upsilon_{n}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x},\upsilon,\upsilon_{n})

\displaystyle=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\left\|x_{n}-\bar{x}\right\|}

\displaystyle\hphantom{{}={}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,x_{n}-\bar{x}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\bar{\xi}(\bar{x},\upsilon).

We restrict attention to sequences with
x_{n}\neq\bar{x}
;
for
x_{n}=\bar{x}
the defining inequality holds trivially, since

\xi_{n}=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
.
Since by
Definition

2.20
it also holds
{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\bar{\xi}}=\delta_{\bar{x}}
and thus
\bar{\xi}=\delta_{\bar{x}}\times\xi
for some
\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the condition reads

0\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\left\|x_{n}-\bar{x}\right\|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,x_{n}-\bar{x}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(\upsilon)=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\left\langle\mathbb{E}^{\upsilon\sim\xi}\left[\upsilon\right],x_{n}-\bar{x}\right\rangle}{\left\|x_{n}-\bar{x}\right\|}
,
which is true if and only if
{\mathbb{E}^{\upsilon\sim\xi}\left[\upsilon\right]}\in\hat{\normal}_{\Xi}(\bar{x})
(cf.
[
46
, Definition 6.3]
).
The statement for the general normal cone follows taking the limit and by continuity of the inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and of the expected value in Wasserstein.
Conversely, if

v\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\nu}\left[x\right]\in\normal_{\Xi}(\bar{x})
, take

x_{n}\to\bar{x}
in
\Xi
and
v_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\Xi}(x_{n})
with
v_{n}\to v
, and set
\nu_{n}\coloneqq{(\mathrm{Id}+v_{n}-v)}_{\#}{\nu}
;
then
\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\nu_{n}}\left[x\right]=v_{n}
, so
\delta_{x_{n}}\times\nu_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\delta_{x_{n}})
by the first part, and

\delta_{x_{n}}\times\nu_{n}\to\delta_{\bar{x}}\times\nu
since

W_{2}\left(\delta_{x_{n}}\times\nu_{n},\delta_{\bar{x}}\times\nu\right)^{2}\leq\left\|x_{n}-\bar{x}\right\|^{2}+\left\|v_{n}-v\right\|^{2}
, whence
\delta_{\bar{x}}\times\nu\in\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
.

Proof B.11
.

Proof of
Lemma

2.25
.

Subgradients.
Since
\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)\subseteq\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)
for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the defining inequality of
\hat{\partial}^{s}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
(
Definition

2.23
), required for all
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)}
, holds in particular for all
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}
. On such optimal transport plans
\left\|\xi\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)
, so this is exactly the inequality defining
\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
(
Definition

2.2
); hence
\hat{\partial}^{s}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
. The inclusion for the general subgradients follows: if
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
with
\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})\to\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\partial}^{s}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
with
\xi_{n}\to\bar{\xi}
, then
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
by the above, and
Definition

2.2
gives
\bar{\xi}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.

Normal cones.
Since
\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)\subseteq\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)
for every
\mu\in\mathcal{C}
, the defining inequality of
\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
(
Definition

2.24
), required for all
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)}
, holds in particular for all
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}
, which is the defining inequality of
\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
(
Definition

2.20
). The inclusion for the (limiting) normal cones then follows directly: if
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
in
\mathcal{C}
and
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n})
with
\xi_{n}\to\bar{\xi}
as in
Definition

2.24
, then
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n})
by the above, and
Definition

2.20
gives
\bar{\xi}\in\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
.

Proof B.12
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.28
.
We prove the result for the regular normal cone, and then argue by continuity for the general normal cone.
Let
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
and suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that

\beta\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]>0
.
In this case,
(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\times(\delta_{0}\times\delta_{\beta})
belongs to the tangent cone
{\tangent_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})}
.
Indeed, note first that

(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\times(\delta_{0}\times\delta_{\beta})\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})}
:
with
T
as in (
2.9
),

{T}_{\#}{(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\times(\delta_{0}\times\delta_{\beta})}=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}

is tangent at
\bar{\mu}
, so the claim follows from

Lemma

A.11
2
. Consider the sequences
\mu_{n}=\bar{\mu}\to\bar{\mu}
and
\lambda_{n}\coloneqq\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})+\beta\tau_{n}\geq\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
for some
\tau_{n}\searrow 0
.
Then,

\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\lambda_{n}}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
for all
n
and

\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\lambda_{n}}\to\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}
.
Moreover, by

Lemma

A.11
1

(product-target case, with
b=\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
,
\mu=\bar{\mu}
, and

\beta=\lambda_{n}
) and since
\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\bar{\mu})
is the identity
transport plan,

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}\coloneqq(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\times(\delta_{0}\times\delta_{\lambda_{n}-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})

is the variation induced by the unique optimal transport plan between

\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}
and
\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\lambda_{n}}
,
with last velocity component almost everywhere
\lambda_{n}-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.
Then,
\frac{1}{\tau_{n}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}=(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\times(\delta_{0}\times\delta_{\frac{\lambda_{n}-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}{\tau_{n}}})=(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\times(\delta_{0}\times\delta_{\beta})
.
In particular,
\frac{1}{\tau_{n}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}\to(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\times(\delta_{0}\times\delta_{\beta})
, which therefore belongs to
{\tangent_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})}
.
We can now show that the inner product between
\boldsymbol{\xi}
and this tangent vector is strictly positive, which contradicts
Proposition

2.21
.
Specifically,
by

Lemma

A.11
3

(with
c=\beta
and the variation

(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\times(\delta_{0}\times\delta_{\beta})
,
whose last velocity component is almost everywhere
\beta
) and

{T}_{\#}{(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\times(\delta_{0}\times\delta_{\beta})}=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
,

\left\langle\boldsymbol{\xi},(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\times(\delta_{0}\times\delta_{\beta})\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}}=\beta\,\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=\beta^{2}>0
,
where the last equality is the definition of
\beta
.
This contradicts
Proposition

2.21
, and concludes the proof for the regular normal cone.
The argument above applies verbatim at any

\mu\times\delta_{b}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
, replacing

\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
by
b
and setting
\lambda_{n}\coloneqq b+\beta\tau_{n}\geq b\geq\mathcal{J}(\mu)
, so every regular normal at every point of

\epi\mathcal{J}
satisfies the claimed bound. Since any

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})

is the Wasserstein limit of such regular normals and the map

\boldsymbol{\xi}\mapsto\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]

is continuous in Wasserstein, the bound passes to the
limit.

Proof B.13
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.29
.
To prove this statement, we proceed in two steps.
We first show the statement for the regular normal cone, namely that for all
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
with
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=-1
it holds that
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
. Then, we prove the statement for the general normal cone.

Step 1 (regular normal cone).

For this step, we adapt the proof of
[
47
, Theorem 1.86]
.
First, observe that

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
:
indeed,

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})}

by
Definition

2.20
, and

Lemma

A.11
2

then yields the claim.
For the sake of contradiction, assume that there exists
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
with
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=-1
for which
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\not\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
. Then, it follows directly from the definition of subgradient (cf.
Definition

2.2
) that there exist
\lambda>0
and
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
,
\varsigma_{n}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu_{n})}
such that

\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})-\left\langle{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\varsigma_{n}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}<-\lambda W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{n}\right).

(B.9)

We now claim that
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\varsigma_{n},\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\right)\to 0
or equivalently
\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\to 0
. Indeed, since
\varsigma_{n}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma_{n}}
for some
\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu_{n})
, we have

\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\varsigma_{n}(\bar{x},\upsilon)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{n}-\bar{x}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma_{n}(\bar{x},x_{n})=W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{n}\right)^{2}\to 0
.
Define

\beta_{n}\coloneqq\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})+\left\langle{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\varsigma_{n}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}-\lambda W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{n}\right).

By continuity of the inner product with respect to the convergence induced by
W_{\bar{\mu}}\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)
(cf.
Proposition

A.9
3
),
\left\langle{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=0
(cf.
Proposition

A.9
6
).
By continuity of the Wasserstein distance in Wasserstein, we conclude
\beta_{n}\to\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.
Furthermore, (
B.9
) implies
\beta_{n}>\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
so that
\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
.
Since
\varsigma_{n}\times\delta_{(\beta_{n}-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}))}
is a variation at

\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}
whose last velocity component is
almost everywhere equal to
\beta_{n}-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
,
(
A.1
) (cf.

Lemma

A.11
3
),
together with

\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=-1
,
yields

\left\langle\boldsymbol{\xi},\varsigma_{n}\times\delta_{(\beta_{n}-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}))}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}}=\left\langle{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\varsigma_{n}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}-(\beta_{n}-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})).

Using
\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{n}\right)
, this yields

\displaystyle\frac{\left\langle\boldsymbol{\xi},\varsigma_{n}\times\delta_{(\beta_{n}-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}))}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}}}{W_{2}\left(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}},\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\langle{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\varsigma_{n}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}-(\beta_{n}-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}))}{\sqrt{\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}+\absolutevalue{\beta_n - \objective(\varFixed)}^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}{\sqrt{\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}^{2}+\absolutevalue{\innerProduct{\pushforward{T}{\boldsymbol{\varTangent}}}{\varTangentOther_n}_{\varFixed} - \lambda\norm{\varTangentOther_n}_{\varFixed}}^{2}}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}{\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}+\absolutevalue{\innerProduct{\pushforward{T}{\boldsymbol{\varTangent}}}{\varTangentOther_n}_{\varFixed} - \lambda\norm{\varTangentOther_n}_{\varFixed}}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}{\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}+\absolutevalue{\innerProduct{\pushforward{T}{\boldsymbol{\varTangent}}}{\varTangentOther_n}_{\varFixed}}+\lambda\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}{\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}+\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}+\lambda\left\|\varsigma_{n}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}}=\frac{\lambda}{1+\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}+\lambda}>0,

where the inequalities follow from subadditivity of the square root, triangle inequality, and
Proposition

A.9
10
.
By

Lemma

A.11
1
,
the lifted transport plan

{(\pi_{1},\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}),\pi_{2},\beta_{n})}_{\#}{\gamma_{n}}

belongs to

\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})},\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}})
,
it induces the variation
\varsigma_{n}\times\delta_{(\beta_{n}-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}))}
, and it yields

W_{2}\left(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}},\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}\right)^{2}=W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu_{n}\right)^{2}+\absolutevalue{\beta_n-\objective(\varFixed)}^{2}
,
as used above.
However, this inequality contradicts the assumption that
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
by the definition of the regular normal cone (cf.
Definition

2.20
), since
\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
converges to
\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}
while the left-hand side remains bounded away from zero.

Step 2 (general normal cone).

Consider now
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
with
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=-1
. Then, by
Definition

2.20
, there must exist
\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}})
for some
\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
with
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
and
\beta_{n}\to\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
,
\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}_{n}}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}
,
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}_{n}}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=-\lambda_{n}
, and
\lambda_{n}\to 1
, where we used the continuity of the expected value in Wasserstein.
Moreover, for
n
large enough,
\lambda_{n}>0
forces

\beta_{n}=\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
. Indeed, if
\beta_{n}>\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
,
then for all sufficiently small
t>0
we have

\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}-t}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
, and the
variation
\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}\times\delta_{-t}
induced by the optimal
transport plan pairing the Dirac measures has last velocity component
almost everywhere equal to
-t

(cf.
Lemma

A.11
1
); by
(
A.1
), together with

\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=-\lambda_{n}
,
this yields

\left\|\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}\times\delta_{-t}\right\|_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}}=t

and

\left\langle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n},\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}\times\delta_{-t}\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}}=t\lambda_{n}
,
so
Definition

2.20
requires
t\lambda_{n}\leq o\left(t\right)
as

t\to 0
, i.e.,
\lambda_{n}\leq 0
, a contradiction. In particular

\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})=\beta_{n}\to\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and

\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{R}
.
Since the regular normal cone is a cone (cf.
Proposition

2.21
), we have that
\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}\coloneqq\frac{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}_{n}}}{\lambda_{n}}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})})
and
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{{\xi_{n}}}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=-1
. Note that
\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}
is well defined since
\lambda_{n}>0
for
n
sufficiently large.
By
Step 1
, we thus have
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
. We need to show that
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\to{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
. Since
T
is Lipschitz continuous (cf.
Lemma

A.11
2
), it suffices to show that
\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}
. With
\boldsymbol{\tilde{\gamma}_{n}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}_{n}})
, define

\boldsymbol{\gamma_{n}}\coloneqq{(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}},\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}},\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}},\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\gamma}_{n}}}.

Then,
\boldsymbol{\gamma_{n}}\in\Gamma(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}})
and

\displaystyle W_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})\times(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}\left\|\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}-\boldsymbol{x}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{\upsilon}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon^{\prime}}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma_{n}}((\boldsymbol{\bar{x}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}),(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})\times(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}\left\|\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}-\boldsymbol{x}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{\upsilon}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\upsilon}}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\gamma}_{n}}((\boldsymbol{\bar{x}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}),(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\upsilon}}))

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})\times(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}\left\|\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}-\boldsymbol{x}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left(\absolutevalue{1 - \frac{1}{\lambda_n}}\left\|\boldsymbol{\tilde{\upsilon}}\right\|+\left\|\boldsymbol{\tilde{\upsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\|\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\gamma}_{n}}((\boldsymbol{\bar{x}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}),(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\upsilon}}))

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})\times(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}2\left\|\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}-\boldsymbol{x}\right\|^{2}+2\left\|\boldsymbol{\upsilon}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{\upsilon}}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\gamma}_{n}}((\boldsymbol{\bar{x}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}),(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\upsilon}}))

\displaystyle\qquad+2\absolutevalue{1 - \frac{1}{\lambda_n}}^{2}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})\times(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}\left\|\boldsymbol{\tilde{\upsilon}}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\gamma}_{n}}((\boldsymbol{\bar{x}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}),(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\upsilon}}))

\displaystyle\leq 2W_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}_{n}}\right)^{2}+2\absolutevalue{1 - \frac{1}{\lambda_n}}^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}_{n}}\right\|^{2}_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}}.

Since
W_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}_{n}}\right)\to 0
,
\left\|\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}_{n}}\right\|^{2}_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}}\to\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\right\|^{2}_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}}
(since convergence in Wasserstein implies convergence of the second moment), and
\lambda_{n}\to 1
, we conclude that
W_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}\right)\to 0
.

Proof B.14
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.31
.
Let
{\xi}\in\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
. Then, by
Definition

2.30
, there is
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
such that
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\xi
and
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=0
.
Therefore, by the definition of normal cone, there are
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
,
\beta_{n}\geq\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
,
\beta_{n}\to\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
,
\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}})
with
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=-\lambda_{n}
and
\lambda_{n}\to 0
, and
\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}
.

To start, we show that if
\beta_{n}>\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
then
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}=\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}
, which directly implies
\xi=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
.
If
\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}=0
, then
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}=\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}
and there is nothing to prove, so assume
\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}>0
.
By continuity of
\mathcal{J}
, for all
n
sufficiently large, there is
\bar{\varepsilon}_{n}>0
such that for all
\mu\in\mathbb{B}_{W_{2}}(\mu_{n};\bar{\varepsilon}_{n})
it holds
\beta_{n}\geq\mathcal{J}(\mu)
. Thus, for all
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}_{n}}{\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}}\right)
we can define
\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}}\in\mathbb{B}_{W_{2}}(\mu_{n};\bar{\varepsilon}_{n})
.
In particular,
\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
.
Suppose initially that
\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}})
. Then,

\varepsilon({(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{2d+1})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\times\delta_{0})\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}},\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}})}

for all
\varepsilon
sufficiently small.
Indeed, since
\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}})
,

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}

is an optimal transport plan for all
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, so by

Lemma

A.11
1

its image under
{T}_{\#}{\cdot}
, namely

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}}
,
lies in
\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{n},\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon})
. In turn,

Lemma

A.11
1

lifts this transport plan to an optimal transport plan between
\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}
and
the Dirac-marginal target
\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}
, which
induces the displayed variation.
Thus, by definition of regular normal cone (cf.
Definition

2.20
),

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pto\left(\left\|\varepsilon({(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{2d+1})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\times\delta_{0})\right\|_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left\langle\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}},\varepsilon({(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{2d+1})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\times\delta_{0})\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\left\langle\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}},{(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{2d+1})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\times\delta_{0}\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}^{2},

where the last equality is
(
A.1
) applied with
c=0
to the
variation

{(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{2d+1})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\times\delta_{0}
,
whose last velocity component vanishes and whose image under

{T}_{\#}{\cdot}
equals
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}
.
Thus,
\varepsilon\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}^{2}\leq o\left(\left\|\varepsilon({(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{2d+1})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\times\delta_{0})\right\|_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}}\right)
.
Dividing by
\varepsilon
and letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, we conclude
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}=\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}
.
If
\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}\notin\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}})
,
fix

\eta>0
and define
\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}^{\eta}}\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}})
so that

W_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}^{\eta}},\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}\right)<\eta
.
We can proceed analogously, with the displacements

\varepsilon({(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{2d+1})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}^{\eta}}}\times\delta_{0})

in place of

\varepsilon({(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{2d+1})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\times\delta_{0})
,
to get, for all
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small (depending on
\eta
),

\varepsilon\left\langle{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi^{\eta}_{n}}}\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}}\leq o\left(\left\|\varepsilon({(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{2d+1})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}^{\eta}}}\times\delta_{0})\right\|_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}}\right)
.
Dividing by
\varepsilon
and letting
\varepsilon\to 0
at fixed
\eta

yields

\left\langle{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi^{\eta}_{n}}}\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}}\leq 0
. Since

W_{\mu_{n}}\left({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}^{\eta}}},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right)\leq W_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}^{\eta}},\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}\right)<\eta
(cf.
Lemma

A.11
2
),
letting
\eta\to 0
and using the continuity of the inner product (cf.

Proposition

A.9
3
)
we conclude

\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}^{2}\leq 0

and, thus,
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}=\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}
.

If
\beta_{n}>\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
for infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the previous step gives
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}=\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}
along that subsequence, and passing to the limit yields
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
. We may therefore assume
\beta_{n}=\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})})\subseteq\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})})
,
Proposition

2.28
gives
\lambda_{n}\geq 0
.
We again prove that
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}=\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}
, which directly implies
\xi=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
.

We distinguish two cases, a subsequence of
\lambda_{n}=0
and of
\lambda_{n}>0
, which we treat separately:

\lambda_{n}>0
.

By
Step 1
of the proof of
Proposition

2.29
, there exists
{\xi}_{n}\coloneqq\frac{{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}}{\lambda_{n}}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
. For
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, define
\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi_{n}}
, so that
W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon}\right)=\varepsilon\left\|\xi_{n}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}
and
\varepsilon\xi_{n}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{n},\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon})}
, where, as above, we initially assume that
\xi_{n}\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\mu_{n})
.
Since
\mathcal{J}
is strictly continuous at
\bar{\mu}
, there exists
M>0
so that

\frac{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}{W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon}\right)}<M

for all
n
sufficiently large and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.
Using
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

0\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})-\left\langle\xi_{n},\varepsilon\xi_{n}\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}}}{W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon}\right)}\leq M-\left\|\xi_{n}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}=M-\frac{\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}}{\lambda_{n}}.

However, as
n\to\infty
this value is negative because
M
is constant,
\lambda_{n}\searrow 0
, and
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\to{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
. This yields a contradiction, unless
\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}\to 0
and, thus,
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
.
If instead
\xi_{n}\notin\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\mu_{n})
,
we
may assume
\left\|\xi_{n}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}>0
(otherwise

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}=\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}
and
there is nothing to prove), fix

\eta\in(0,\left\|\xi_{n}\right\|_{\mu_{n}})
, and define

\xi_{n}^{\eta}\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\mu_{n})
so that

W_{\mu_{n}}\left(\xi_{n}^{\eta},\xi_{n}\right)<\eta
.
With
\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon,\eta}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi_{n}^{\eta}}
, so that

\varepsilon\xi_{n}^{\eta}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{n},\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon,\eta})}

and

W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon,\eta}\right)=\varepsilon\left\|\xi_{n}^{\eta}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}

for all
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small (depending on
\eta
), this gives

0\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon,\eta})-\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})-\left\langle\xi_{n},\varepsilon\xi_{n}^{\eta}\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}}}{W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon,\eta}\right)}\leq M-\frac{\left\langle\xi_{n},\xi_{n}^{\eta}\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}}}{\left\|\xi_{n}^{\eta}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}}.

Letting
\eta\to 0
, the continuity of the inner product and of the norm (cf.

Proposition

A.9
3
)
yields
0\leq M-\left\|\xi_{n}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}=M-\frac{\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}}{\lambda_{n}}
,
and we can conclude as above.

\lambda_{n}=0
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, define
\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1}+\varepsilon\pi_{2})}_{\#}{{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}}
.
Suppose initially that
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\mu_{n})
.
Since
\mathcal{J}
is strictly continuous, the sequence of reals
\beta_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\frac{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}{\varepsilon}
is bounded and thus admits a subsequence converging to some
\beta_{n}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Thus, we now argue that
{(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{2d+1})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}^{0}}\in{\tangent_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})})}
.

Since
\mu_{n}^{\varepsilon}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})+\varepsilon\beta_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\in\epi\mathcal{J}

and
\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}
belongs to the tangent space at
\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}
, the limit as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
yields the result (cf.
Definition

2.19
).

However,

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}},{(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{2d+1})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}^{0}}\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\beta_{n}^{0}\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}^{2}\geq 0,

where the first equality is

Lemma

A.11
3

with
c=\beta_{n}^{0}
, and the second term vanishes as
\lambda_{n}=0
.
If
\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}>0
, we contradict
Proposition

2.21
.
Thus,
\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}=0
and so
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}=\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}
.
Note that

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\mu_{n})}

by

Lemma

A.11
2
,
since
\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}
belongs to the tangent space at

\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}
by

Definition

2.20
. If instead
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\notin\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\mu_{n})
,
let
\eta>0
and define
\xi_{n}^{\eta}
that does, so that
W_{\mu_{n}}\left(\xi_{n}^{\eta},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right)<\eta
.
Then, as above, we have that
{(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d},\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n}),\pi_{d+1},\ldots,\pi_{2d})}_{\#}{\xi_{n}^{\eta}}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}^{0}}\in{\tangent_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})})}
(note that
\xi_{n}^{\eta}
belongs to the tangent space at
\mu_{n}
, and not
\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}
, so we need to “add” the marginal
\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}
).
With
Proposition

2.21
, this gives

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\geq\left\langle\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}},{(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d},\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n}),\pi_{d+1},\ldots,\pi_{2d})}_{\#}{\xi_{n}^{\eta}}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}^{0}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}},\xi_{n}^{\eta}\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\beta_{n}^{0}\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\left\langle{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}},\xi_{n}^{\eta}\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}^{2}-W_{\mu_{n}}\left(\xi_{n}^{\eta},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right)^{2}+\left\|\xi_{n}^{\eta}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}^{2}-\eta^{2}\right),

where the first equality is

Lemma

A.11
3

with
c=\beta_{n}^{0}
(the second term vanishing as
\lambda_{n}=0
), and the last
equality uses
Proposition

A.9
1
.
Thus,
\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}\leq\eta
and, as
\eta
is arbitrary, we conclude
\left\|{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}\right\|_{\mu_{n}}=0
and, with it,
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{n}}}=\mathbf{0}_{{\mu_{n}}}
.

Conversely,
\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\subseteq\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
, with
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=0
and
{T}_{\#}{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}}}=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
, so
\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\in\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.

Proof B.15
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.32
.
We prove that each of the four functionals is Lipschitz on bounded subsets of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Strict continuity at any
\bar{\mu}
is then immediate, as the closed ball
\bar{\mathbb{B}}_{W_{2}}(\bar{\mu};r)
is a bounded neighborhood of
\bar{\mu}
, and
Proposition

2.31
yields the trivial horizon subgradient. Throughout,
m_{2}(\mu)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x\right\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
denotes the second moment, which is bounded on bounded sets.
For
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we fix an optimal transport plan
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
, so that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)=W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)^{2}
, with
x
- and
y
-marginals
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
.

Step 1 (a transport estimate).

We first record an estimate used repeatedly: if
\phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\absolutevalue{\phi(x)-\phi(y)}\leq c\,(1+\left\|x\right\|+\left\|y\right\|)\left\|x-y\right\|+c\,\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and some
c>0
, then
\mu\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
is finite on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and Lipschitz on bounded sets. Indeed, letting
x\to y
shows that
\phi
is continuous, and
y=0
gives
\absolutevalue{\phi(x)}\leq c^{\prime}(1+\left\|x\right\|^{2})
, so that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\absolutevalue{\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq c^{\prime}(1+m_{2}(\mu))<\infty
. Integrating the growth condition against
\gamma
and using Cauchy–Schwarz,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\int_{\reals^d}\phi(x)\,\d\varGeneric_1(x)-\int_{\reals^d}\phi(x)\,\d\varGeneric_2(x)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad=\absolutevalue{\int_{\reals^d\times\reals^d}\big(\phi(x)-\phi(y)\big)\,\d\varPlanGeneric(x, y)}

\displaystyle\leq c\,\left\|1+\left\|x\right\|+\left\|y\right\|\right\|_{L^{2}(\gamma)}W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)+c\,W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\big(1+m_{2}(\mu_{1})^{1/2}+m_{2}(\mu_{2})^{1/2}\big)W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)+c\,W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)^{2},

using
\left\|x-y\right\|_{L^{2}(\gamma)}=W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)
. On a bounded set,
m_{2}
and
W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)
are bounded, so the right-hand side is at most a constant multiple of
W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)
.

Step 2 (potential energy).

Since
V
is
\lambda
-convex with
V(x)\leq C(1+\left\|x\right\|^{2})
, the proper convex function
W\coloneqq V-\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2}
is bounded above by
C_{1}(1+\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2})
, hence finite, and therefore continuous and subdifferentiable, on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
\partial V(x)=\partial W(x)+\lambda x
. Fix
p_{0}\in\partial W(0)
. Since
p_{0}
is a subgradient of
W
at
0
, the subgradient inequality
W(x)\geq W(0)+\left\langle p_{0},x\right\rangle
and Cauchy–Schwarz give the lower bound
W(x)\geq W(0)-\left\|p_{0}\right\|\left\|x\right\|
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Now fix
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
p\in\partial W(x)
; we may assume
p\neq 0
, as the bound below is trivial otherwise. By definition of subgradient,
W(y)\geq W(x)+\left\langle p,y-x\right\rangle
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
; evaluating this inequality at
y=x+t\tfrac{p}{\left\|p\right\|}
for
t>0
and using
\left\langle p,\tfrac{p}{\left\|p\right\|}\right\rangle=\left\|p\right\|
, we obtain

t\left\|p\right\|=\left\langle p,t\tfrac{p}{\left\|p\right\|}\right\rangle\leq W\big(x+t\tfrac{p}{\left\|p\right\|}\big)-W(x).

To bound the right-hand side, the quadratic growth
W(\cdot)\leq C_{1}(1+\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2})
with
\left\|x+t\tfrac{p}{\left\|p\right\|}\right\|\leq\left\|x\right\|+t
gives
W\big(x+t\tfrac{p}{\left\|p\right\|}\big)\leq C_{1}\big(1+(\left\|x\right\|+t)^{2}\big)
, while the lower bound above gives
-W(x)\leq-W(0)+\left\|p_{0}\right\|\left\|x\right\|
; hence

t\left\|p\right\|\leq C_{1}\big(1+(\left\|x\right\|+t)^{2}\big)-W(0)+\left\|p_{0}\right\|\left\|x\right\|.

Choosing
t=1+\left\|x\right\|
, so that
\left\|x\right\|+t=1+2\left\|x\right\|
, and dividing the previous inequality by
1+\left\|x\right\|
,

\displaystyle\left\|p\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{1}\big(1+(1+2\left\|x\right\|)^{2}\big)-W(0)+\left\|p_{0}\right\|\left\|x\right\|}{1+\left\|x\right\|}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{1}\big(2+4\left\|x\right\|+4\left\|x\right\|^{2}\big)-W(0)+\left\|p_{0}\right\|\left\|x\right\|}{1+\left\|x\right\|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4C_{1}(1+\left\|x\right\|)^{2}}{1+\left\|x\right\|}+\frac{\absolutevalue{W(0)}}{1+\left\|x\right\|}+\frac{\left\|p_{0}\right\|\left\|x\right\|}{1+\left\|x\right\|}

\displaystyle\leq 4C_{1}(1+\left\|x\right\|)+\absolutevalue{W(0)}+\left\|p_{0}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}(1+\left\|x\right\|),

where
c_{0}\coloneqq 4C_{1}+\absolutevalue{W(0)}+\left\|p_{0}\right\|
: the third line uses
2+4\left\|x\right\|+4\left\|x\right\|^{2}\leq 4(1+\left\|x\right\|)^{2}
and
-W(0)\leq\absolutevalue{W(0)}
, the fourth uses
1+\left\|x\right\|\geq 1
and
\left\|x\right\|\leq 1+\left\|x\right\|
, and the last uses
\absolutevalue{W(0)}+\left\|p_{0}\right\|\leq\big(\absolutevalue{W(0)}+\left\|p_{0}\right\|\big)(1+\left\|x\right\|)
. Consequently, since
\partial V(x)=\partial W(x)+\lambda x
, every
s\in\partial V(x)
satisfies
\left\|s\right\|\leq c_{0}^{\prime}(1+\left\|x\right\|)
with
c_{0}^{\prime}\coloneqq c_{0}+\absolutevalue{\lambda}
. By
\lambda
-convexity,
V(x)-V(y)\leq\left\langle s_{x},x-y\right\rangle-\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}
for
s_{x}\in\partial V(x)
, and symmetrically; hence

\absolutevalue{V(x)-V(y)}\leq c_{0}^{\prime}(1+\left\|x\right\|+\left\|y\right\|)\left\|x-y\right\|+\tfrac{\absolutevalue{\lambda}}{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2},

which is the condition of Step 1 with
\phi=V
. Thus
\mathcal{J}
is finite and Lipschitz on bounded sets.

Step 3 (interaction energy).

Since
U\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
has bounded Hessian,
\left\|\nabla{U}(z)\right\|\leq a_{0}+M\left\|z\right\|
with
a_{0}\coloneqq\left\|\nabla{U}(0)\right\|
, and
\absolutevalue{U(z)}\leq\absolutevalue{U(0)}+a_{0}\left\|z\right\|+\tfrac{M}{2}\left\|z\right\|^{2}
; in particular
\mathcal{J}
is finite on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Coupling
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{1}
with
\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2}
through
\gamma\times\gamma
,

\mathcal{J}(\mu_{1})-\mathcal{J}(\mu_{2})=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(U(x-x^{\prime})-U(y-y^{\prime})\big)\,\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\gamma(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}).

A point of the segment between
x-x^{\prime}
and
y-y^{\prime}
has norm at most
\left\|x\right\|+\left\|x^{\prime}\right\|+\left\|y\right\|+\left\|y^{\prime}\right\|
, and
\left\|(x-x^{\prime})-(y-y^{\prime})\right\|\leq\left\|x-y\right\|+\left\|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\right\|
, so the mean value theorem gives

\absolutevalue{U(x-x')-U(y-y')}\leq\big(a_{0}+M(\left\|x\right\|+\left\|x^{\prime}\right\|+\left\|y\right\|+\left\|y^{\prime}\right\|)\big)\big(\left\|x-y\right\|+\left\|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\right\|\big).

By Cauchy–Schwarz over
\gamma\times\gamma
, using
\left\|\left\|x-y\right\|+\left\|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\right\|\right\|_{L^{2}(\gamma\times\gamma)}\leq 2W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)
and
\left\|a_{0}+M(\left\|x\right\|+\left\|x^{\prime}\right\|+\left\|y\right\|+\left\|y^{\prime}\right\|)\right\|_{L^{2}(\gamma\times\gamma)}\leq a_{0}+2M(m_{2}(\mu_{1})^{1/2}+m_{2}(\mu_{2})^{1/2})
,

\absolutevalue{\objective(\varGeneric_1)-\objective(\varGeneric_2)}\leq\big(a_{0}+2M(m_{2}(\mu_{1})^{1/2}+m_{2}(\mu_{2})^{1/2})\big)W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right),

so
\mathcal{J}
is Lipschitz on bounded sets.

Step 4 (Wasserstein distance).

The triangle inequality gives
\absolutevalue{\wassersteinDistance{\varGeneric_1}{\varReference}{2}-\wassersteinDistance{\varGeneric_2}{\varReference}{2}}\leq W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)
, so

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\objective(\varGeneric_1)-\objective(\varGeneric_2)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\absolutevalue{\wassersteinDistance{\varGeneric_1}{\varReference}{2}-\wassersteinDistance{\varGeneric_2}{\varReference}{2}}\big(W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\hat{\mu}\right)+W_{2}\left(\mu_{2},\hat{\mu}\right)\big)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\big(W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\hat{\mu}\right)+W_{2}\left(\mu_{2},\hat{\mu}\right)\big)W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right).

The prefactor is bounded on bounded sets, so
\mathcal{J}
is Lipschitz on bounded sets.

Step 5 (variance).

Write
\mathcal{J}=g\circ(\mathcal{J}_{1},\mathcal{J}_{2})
, where
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
, and
g(a,b)\coloneqq a-b^{2}
. Since
V
and
V^{2}
have uniformly bounded Hessians, Taylor expansions around
0
give, for some
A,B,c>0
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the quadratic bounds
\absolutevalue{V(x)}\leq A+B\left\|x\right\|^{2}
and
V(x)^{2}\leq A+B\left\|x\right\|^{2}
, together with the gradient bounds
\left\|\nabla{V}(x)\right\|\leq c(1+\left\|x\right\|)
and
\left\|\nabla{(V^{2})}(x)\right\|\leq c(1+\left\|x\right\|)
. By the mean value theorem—a point of the segment between
x
and
y
having norm at most
\left\|x\right\|+\left\|y\right\|
—both
\absolutevalue{V(x)-V(y)}
and
\absolutevalue{V^2(x)-V^2(y)}
are at most
c^{\prime\prime}(1+\left\|x\right\|+\left\|y\right\|)\left\|x-y\right\|
, so
V
and
V^{2}
satisfy the condition of Step 1. Hence,
\mathcal{J}_{1}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
are Lipschitz on bounded sets; they are also bounded there (by
A+B\,m_{2}(\mu)
), so
(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu),\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu))
ranges in a bounded set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Since
g(a,b)=a-b^{2}
is continuously differentiable, it is Lipschitz on the bounded set
S
: there is a constant
L_{S}\geq 0
such that
\absolutevalue{g(a)-g(a')}\leq L_{S}\big(\absolutevalue{a_1-a_1'}+\absolutevalue{a_2-a_2'}\big)
for all
a=(a_{1},a_{2}),\,a^{\prime}=(a_{1}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime})\in S
. Applying this bound with
a=(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{1}),\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu_{1}))
and
a^{\prime}=(\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mu_{2}),\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mu_{2}))
,

\absolutevalue{\objective(\varGeneric_1)-\objective(\varGeneric_2)}\leq L_{S}\big(\absolutevalue{\objective_1(\varGeneric_1)-\objective_1(\varGeneric_2)}+\absolutevalue{\objective_2(\varGeneric_1)-\objective_2(\varGeneric_2)}\big),

and, since
\mathcal{J}_{1}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
are Lipschitz on bounded sets, so is
\mathcal{J}
.

Proof B.16
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.34
.
We prove necessity first (
Step 1
), and then sufficiency
(
Steps 2–4
).

Step 1 (necessity).
Follows combining the necessary condition on epigraphical normals in
Proposition

2.28
with the general normal cone being a cone and
Propositions

2.29
and
2.30
.
In detail, let

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})

and set

\lambda\coloneqq-\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]
,
so that
\lambda\geq 0
by
Proposition

2.28
. If

\lambda>0
, since the general normal cone is a cone (cf.

Proposition

2.21
),

\frac{1}{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
,
and its last velocity component has expectation
-1
. Then,

Proposition

2.29
gives

\frac{1}{\lambda}{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}={T}_{\#}{\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
,
i.e., condition (i); here, scaling and
{T}_{\#}{\cdot}
commute since,
by
Definition

2.1
, scaling acts on the
velocities only, while
T
drops the last component of positions and
velocities alike. If instead
\lambda=0
, then
\boldsymbol{\xi}

itself certifies

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})

by
Definition

2.30
, i.e., condition
(ii).

We prove sufficiency in four steps.
The difficulty specific to the
Wasserstein setting is that
T
discards the last velocity component, so
conditions (i) and (ii) constrain its law only through its
expectation. The
geometry of the deterministic last coordinate is handled by

Lemma

A.11
; building on it, we first show
that the regular normal cone inequality is insensitive to any further
information carried by the last velocity component (
Step 2
), and then
construct regular epigraphical normals converging to

\boldsymbol{\xi}
(
Steps 3 and 4
).
