Step 2 (the regular normal cone only sees the
expectation of the last velocity component).
Let

\mu\times\delta_{b}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
, let

\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu\times\delta_{b})
,
and let
\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}
be a variation at

\mu\times\delta_{b}
with

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}={T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}

whose last velocity component has the same expectation as that of

\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}
. We claim that

\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu\times\delta_{b})
.
First,

\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}(\mu\times\delta_{b})}

by
Definition

2.20
, so

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\mu)}

by

Lemma

A.11
2
.
Since

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}}={T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}}
,

\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}(\mu\times\delta_{b})}
.
Next, let

\mu^{\prime}\times\delta_{\beta}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
and

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\mu\times\delta_{b},\mu^{\prime}\times\delta_{\beta})}
.
By

Lemma

A.11
1
,
the last velocity component of
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
equals

\beta-b
almost everywhere and

\varsigma\coloneqq{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})}
,
so that

\left\|\varsigma\right\|_{\mu}=W_{2}\left(\mu,\mu^{\prime}\right)

because
\varsigma
is induced by an optimal transport plan; by

Lemma

A.11
3
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\|_{\mu\times\delta_{b}}^{2}=\left\|\varsigma\right\|_{\mu}^{2}+\absolutevalue{\beta-b}^{2}
.
Moreover, for every variation
\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}^{\prime}
at

\mu\times\delta_{b}
,
(
A.1
) gives

\left\langle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\rangle_{\mu\times\delta_{b}}=\left\langle{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}^{\prime}},\varsigma\right\rangle_{\mu}+(\beta-b)\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}^{\prime}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right].

The right-hand side of
(
A.1
) takes the same value for

\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}
and for

\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}
. Therefore,

\left\langle\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\rangle_{\mu\times\delta_{b}}=\left\langle\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\rangle_{\mu\times\delta_{b}}\leq o\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\|_{\mu\times\delta_{b}}\right)
,
where the inequality holds because

\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu\times\delta_{b})
.
By
Definition

2.20
, we conclude

\boldsymbol{\bar{\xi}}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu\times\delta_{b})
.

Step 3 (sufficiency: an approximating sequence of
regular normals).
Suppose now that
\boldsymbol{\xi}
satisfies
condition (i) (with
\lambda>0
) or condition
(ii) (with
\lambda\coloneqq 0
). We
claim that in both cases there exist

\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
and

\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}})

whose last velocity component has expectation
-\lambda_{n}
, such that

\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}\to\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})},\qquad{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}}\to{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\qquad\lambda_{n}\to\lambda.

(B.10)

Under condition (ii),

Definition

2.30
provides

\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})

with

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}={T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}

and last velocity component with zero expectation, and

Definition

2.20
provides

\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
with

\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}\to\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}

and

\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}})

with

\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}
.
Then, (
B.10
) holds: the second
convergence because
T
is
1
-Lipschitz (cf.

Lemma

A.11
2
),
and the third by the continuity of the expected value in Wasserstein.
Under condition (i), by

Definition

2.2
2
, there
are
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
with

\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})\to\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
(in particular,

\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{R}
and

\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
)
and
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
with

\xi_{n}\to\frac{1}{\lambda}{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
.
Set
\beta_{n}\coloneqq\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})
and let

\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}
be the law of

((x,\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})),(\lambda\upsilon,-\lambda))
for

(x,\upsilon)\sim\xi_{n}
; i.e., the variation at

\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}
obtained by scaling

\xi_{n}
by
\lambda
and appending the deterministic last velocity
component
-\lambda
, so that
\lambda_{n}=\lambda
for all
n
. Then,
(
B.10
) holds: the first convergence
since

W_{2}\left(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})},\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}\right)^{2}=W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\bar{\mu}\right)^{2}+\absolutevalue{\objective(\varGeneric_n)-\objective(\varFixed)}^{2}\to 0

(cf.
Lemma

A.11
1
),
and the second from

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}}=\lambda\xi_{n}
,
since scaling the velocity components by
\lambda
is

\max\{1,\lambda\}
-Lipschitz with respect to

W_{2}\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)
. It remains to show that

\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})})
.
Since the regular normal cone is a cone (cf.

Proposition

2.21
) and

\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}=\lambda\boldsymbol{\hat{\xi}}_{n}
,
with
\boldsymbol{\hat{\xi}}_{n}
the law of

((x,\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})),(\upsilon,-1))
for

(x,\upsilon)\sim\xi_{n}
, it suffices to show that

\boldsymbol{\hat{\xi}}_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})})
;
i.e., that regular subgradients lift to regular epigraphical normals with
deterministic last velocity component
-1
.
For the tangency requirement in

Definition

2.20
:

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\hat{\xi}}_{n}}=\xi_{n}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\mu_{n})}

by

Definition

2.2
1
,
so

\boldsymbol{\hat{\xi}}_{n}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})})}

by

Lemma

A.11
2
.
For the inequality, fix
\varepsilon>0
. Unpacking the

o\left(\cdot\right)
notation in

Definition

2.2
1
,
there exists
\delta>0
such that

\left\langle\xi_{n},\varsigma\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}}\leq\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\prime})-\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})+\varepsilon W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\mu^{\prime}\right)

for all
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with

W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\mu^{\prime}\right)<\delta
and all

\varsigma\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{n},\mu^{\prime})}
.
Consider now any
\mu^{\prime}\times\delta_{\beta}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
and

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})},\mu^{\prime}\times\delta_{\beta})}

with

\left\|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\|_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}}<\delta
.
With
\varsigma\coloneqq{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}

as in
Step 2
, we have

W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\mu^{\prime}\right)=\left\|\varsigma\right\|_{\mu_{n}}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\|_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}}<\delta

(cf.

Lemma

A.11
3
)
and, by (
A.1
) together with

\beta\geq\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{\hat{\xi}}_{n},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\xi_{n},\varsigma\right\rangle_{\mu_{n}}-(\beta-\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n}))

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\prime})-\beta+\varepsilon W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\mu^{\prime}\right)\leq\varepsilon\left\|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\|_{\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})}}.

As
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, the inequality in

Definition

2.20
holds, and

\boldsymbol{\hat{\xi}}_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu_{n})})
.

Step 4 (sufficiency: conclusion).
Loosely speaking, we
now transport the conditional law of the last velocity component of

\boldsymbol{\xi}
onto the approximating regular normals of

Step 3
and correct its expectation by a vanishing shift;
Step 2

guarantees that the resulting variations are still regular normals. Let

\{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{(x,\upsilon)}\}_{(x,\upsilon)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

be the disintegration with respect to
(x,\upsilon)
of the law of

((x,\upsilon),u)
under
\boldsymbol{\xi}
, where

(x,\upsilon)\sim{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
and

u\in\mathbb{R}
denotes the last velocity component (cf.

[
35
, Theorem 5.3.1]
). For each
n
, let

\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma_{o}({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}})
,
define
\hat{\gamma}_{n}
via

\mathrm{d}\hat{\gamma}_{n}((x_{n},\upsilon_{n}),(x,\upsilon),u)\coloneqq\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{(x,\upsilon)}(u)\mathrm{d}\gamma_{n}((x_{n},\upsilon_{n}),(x,\upsilon))
,
and let
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}
be the law of

((x_{n},\beta_{n}),(\upsilon_{n},u+\lambda-\lambda_{n}))
under

\hat{\gamma}_{n}
. Then,
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}
is a variation
at
\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}
with

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}}={T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}}
,
and its last velocity component has expectation

-\lambda+(\lambda-\lambda_{n})=-\lambda_{n}
, the same as that of

\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}
. By
Step 2
,

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}})
.
Moreover, the law of

\bigl(((x_{n},\beta_{n}),(\upsilon_{n},u+\lambda-\lambda_{n})),((x,\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})),(\upsilon,u))\bigr)

under
\hat{\gamma}_{n}
is a transport plan between

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}
, where for the
second marginal we used that the law of

((x,\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})),(\upsilon,u))
under

\hat{\gamma}_{n}
reconstructs
\boldsymbol{\xi}
, since

(x,\upsilon)\sim{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
and

u\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}_{(x,\upsilon)}
. Thus,

W_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n},\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)^{2}\leq W_{2}\left({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\xi}}_{n}},{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right)^{2}+\absolutevalue{\beta_n-\objective(\varFixed)}^{2}+\absolutevalue{\lambda-\lambda_n}^{2},

which vanishes as
n\to\infty
by
(
B.10
). Hence,

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}

with

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}\in\hat{\normal}_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}})

and

\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\beta_{n}}\in\epi\mathcal{J}
converging to

\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}
, and

Definition

2.20
yields

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
,
concluding the proof.

Proof B.17
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.35
.
The statement for
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}
so that

\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})<0
follows by continuity of
\mathcal{J}
and

Proposition

2.22
.
For the strong cones in the case
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})<0
: since

\mathcal{J}
is continuous,
\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,|\,\mathcal{J}(\mu)<0\}
is open, so every

\mu\in\mathcal{C}
close to
\bar{\mu}
lies in the interior of

\mathcal{C}
and

\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)\subseteq\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)=\{\mathbf{0}_{\mu}\}

by
Lemma

2.25
and

Proposition

2.22
2
;
hence the witnesses in
Definition

2.24
satisfy

\xi_{n}=\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}\to\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
,
and
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})=\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}

as well.
Thus, we focus on the case
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})=0
.

We start by proving the statement for the regular normal cone. If
\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})=\emptyset
, the statement trivially holds so we suppose
\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\neq\emptyset
. Fix
\xi\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
and
\lambda\geq 0
. If
\lambda=0
, the statement holds in virtue of
Proposition

2.21
; i.e.,
\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
. Thus, suppose
\lambda>0
. We have that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mu\in\mathcal{C},\mu\to\bar{\mu}}\\
\gamma\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}\end{subarray}}\frac{\left\langle\lambda\xi,\gamma\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}=\limsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mu\in\mathcal{C},\mu\to\bar{\mu}}\\
\gamma\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}\end{subarray}}\frac{\lambda\left\langle\xi,\gamma\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)},

which is upper bounded by

\limsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mu\in\mathcal{C},\mu\to\bar{\mu}}\\
\gamma\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}\end{subarray}}\hskip-14.22636pt\frac{\lambda\left(\left\langle\xi,\gamma\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}-\mathcal{J}(\mu)+\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\right)}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}\leq\limsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mu\to\bar{\mu}}\\
\gamma\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)}\end{subarray}}\hskip-14.22636pt\frac{\lambda\left(\left\langle\xi,\gamma\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}-\mathcal{J}(\mu)+\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\right)}{W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)}

and, thus, is nonpositive.
This concludes that
\lambda\xi\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
.
The same argument establishes the strong statement. For

\lambda=0
,

\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
,
since

\left\langle\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}},\gamma\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=0
for
every variation
\gamma
(cf.

Proposition

A.9
6
) and

\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}
. For

\lambda>0
and
\xi\in\hat{\partial}^{s}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
,
it suffices to repeat the display above with

\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)
in place of

\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\mu)
and

\left\|\gamma\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}
in place of

W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\mu\right)
, using the defining
inequality of

Definition

2.23
1

in the last step; this yields

\lambda\xi\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
by

Definition

2.24
.

For the second inclusion, we observe that the set

\mathcal{C}
can equivalently be expressed as

\mathcal{C}={(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})}_{\#}{(\epi\mathcal{J}\cap D)}
,
with

D\coloneqq\left\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})\,|\,\mathbb{E}^{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\boldsymbol{\mu}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{x}\right\rangle\right]\leq\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\right\}
;
i.e.,
D
collects the probability measures whose last coordinate has
expected value at most
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
, a sublevel set of the
linear functional
l
of
Section

2.5.2
.
Write
E\coloneqq\epi\mathcal{J}\cap D
and recall that
T
, as in
(
2.9
), drops the last component of
positions and velocities. Since the elements of
\epi\mathcal{J}

have deterministic last coordinate, we have

E=\left\{\mu^{\prime}\times\delta_{\beta^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})\,|\,\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\prime})\leq\beta^{\prime}\leq\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\right\}
.
In particular,
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
for every

\mu^{\prime}\times\delta_{\beta^{\prime}}\in E
, and

\mu\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}\in E
for every

\mu\in\mathcal{C}
, which together yield the expression for

\mathcal{C}
above. We collect three auxiliary facts.

Fact 1 (normals of
\mathcal{C}
lift to normals of

E
).
Let
\mu\in\mathcal{C}
and

\xi\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)
. Then,

\boldsymbol{\xi}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}),\pi_{2},0)}_{\#}{\xi}
,
i.e., the lift of
\xi
with vanishing last velocity component,
belongs to

\hat{\normal}^{s}_{E}(\mu\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
.
To see this, fix
\mu^{\prime}\times\delta_{\beta^{\prime}}\in E
and

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma(\mu\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})},\mu^{\prime}\times\delta_{\beta^{\prime}})}
.
Since the last coordinates of both endpoints are deterministic, the last
velocity component of
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
is almost everywhere
constant, equal to
\beta^{\prime}-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
,

\varsigma\coloneqq{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma(\mu,\mu^{\prime})}

with
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
, and

\left\|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\|_{\mu\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}}^{2}=\left\|\varsigma\right\|_{\mu}^{2}+\absolutevalue{\beta'-\objective(\varFixed)}^{2}\geq\left\|\varsigma\right\|_{\mu}^{2}

(cf.

Lemma

A.11
1

and
3
).
By (
A.1
) (cf.

Lemma

A.11
3
),
applicable since the last velocity component of

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
is almost everywhere constant, and since the
last velocity component of
\boldsymbol{\xi}
vanishes almost
surely (so that, in particular, its expectation is zero),

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\rangle_{\mu\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\xi,\varsigma\right\rangle_{\mu}+(\beta^{\prime}-\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}))\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\left\langle\xi,\varsigma\right\rangle_{\mu}\leq o\left(\left\|\varsigma\right\|_{\mu}\right)\leq o\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\|_{\mu\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}}\right),

where the first inequality is the defining property of

\xi\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)
in

Definition

2.24
(applicable since

\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
), and the second holds because

\left\|\varsigma\right\|_{\mu}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\right\|_{\mu\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}}
. Moreover,
\boldsymbol{\xi}

is a variation at
\mu\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}
with

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\xi\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\mu)}
,
so

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}(\mu\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})}

by
Lemma

A.11
2
.
Hence,

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{E}(\mu\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
.
Consequently, if
\xi\in\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})
,
witnessed by
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
in

\mathcal{C}
and

\xi_{n}\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n})
with

\xi_{n}\to\xi
, then the lifts

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}),\pi_{2},0)}_{\#}{\xi_{n}}

are strong regular normals of
E
at

\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}\to\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}
,
and appending the deterministic coordinates to near-optimal transport plans
between
\xi_{n}
and
\xi
shows

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}
;
thus, the lift

\boldsymbol{\xi}={(\pi_{1},\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}),\pi_{2},0)}_{\#}{\xi}

of
\xi
belongs to

\normal_{E}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
.

Fact 2 (variational geometry of
D
).
The set
D
is
the sublevel set, at level
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}
, of the linear
functional on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
induced by

c=(0,\ldots,0,1)\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\setminus\{0\}
. Thus,

Lemma

C.5
(applied in

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
) shows that
D
is closed and
SNC
at
every point and, since

l(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})=\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})

(i.e.,
\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}
lies at the
boundary of
D
),

\normal_{D}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})=\hat{\normal}^{s}_{D}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})=\left\{(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\times\delta_{(0,\ldots,0,\lambda)}\,|\,\lambda\geq 0\right\}.

In particular, every

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}\in\normal_{D}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})

satisfies

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}}=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
.

Fact 3 (intersection rule for the pair

(\epi\mathcal{J},D)
).
If

\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\notin\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
, then

\normal_{E}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\subseteq\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})+\normal_{D}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
;
i.e., for every

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{E}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})

there are

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
,

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}\in\normal_{D}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
,
and

\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Gamma^{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2})

with

\boldsymbol{\xi}=\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}\,+_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}
.
This is
Theorem

D.13
applied to

\Omega_{1}=\epi\mathcal{J}
and
\Omega_{2}=D
at

\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}\in\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}
,
whose assumptions we now verify. First, both sets are closed, hence
locally closed around
\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}
:
the set
D
by Fact 2, and
\epi\mathcal{J}
since
\mathcal{J}
is
continuous, the maps

\boldsymbol{\mu}\mapsto{(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

and

\boldsymbol{\mu}\mapsto{\pi_{d+1}}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

are
1
-Lipschitz with respect to
W_{2}
, and a
Wasserstein limit of Dirac deltas is a Dirac delta.
Second,
D
is
SNC
at

\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}
by Fact 2, so
assumption (ii) of
Theorem

D.13
holds.
Third, for assumption (i), let

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\cap\left(-\normal_{D}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\right)
.
By Fact 2,

\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\times\delta_{(0,\ldots,0,-\lambda)}

for some
\lambda\geq 0
; in particular,

{\pi_{1}}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}
,
the last velocity component of
\boldsymbol{\xi}
has expectation

-\lambda
, and

{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
.
Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that
\lambda>0
. Since

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})\subseteq\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})

by
Lemma

2.25
,

Proposition

2.34
applies (recall

\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})=0\in\mathbb{R}
) and, the expectation of the last
velocity component being
-\lambda<0
, it yields

\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}=\frac{1}{\lambda}{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
,
contradicting

\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\notin\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
. Thus,

\lambda=0
and

\boldsymbol{\xi}=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})}}
,
so that assumption (i) of
Theorem

D.13
holds as
well.

We are now ready to prove the desired inclusion. Let

\xi\in\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})
and let

\boldsymbol{\xi}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu}),\pi_{2},0)}_{\#}{\xi}

be its lift with vanishing last velocity component, so that

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\normal_{E}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})

and
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\xi
by Fact 1. By
Fact 3, there are

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
,

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}\in\normal_{D}^{s}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
,
and

\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Gamma^{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2})

with

\boldsymbol{\xi}=\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}\,+_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}
.
Pushing this identity forward by
T
(the computation with test functions in
the proof of
Theorem

3.2
below applies
verbatim, with
\hat{T}
as defined there) yields

\xi={T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}={T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}}\,+_{{\hat{T}}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\,{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}}
.
By Fact 2,
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}}=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
,
so
\xi={T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}}
by

Proposition

A.9
8
. By

Lemma

2.25
,

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}\in\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})})
,
so
Proposition

2.34
applies (recall

\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})=0\in\mathbb{R}
): either the last velocity component of

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}
has expectation
-\lambda
with
\lambda>0
and

\frac{1}{\lambda}\xi=\frac{1}{\lambda}{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
,
so that

\xi\in\left\{\lambda\xi^{\prime}\,|\,\lambda\geq 0,\xi^{\prime}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\right\}
,
or it has zero expectation and

\xi={T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}}\in\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
.
In both cases,

\xi\in\left\{\lambda\xi^{\prime}\,|\,\lambda\geq 0,\xi^{\prime}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\right\}\cup\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
,
which establishes the second inclusion.

Proof B.18
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.36
.
We prove the statements separately.

1.

The statement follows directly from
Proposition

2.22
.
For the strong normal cone, since the interior of

\mathcal{C}
is open, every
\mu\in\mathcal{C}
close to

\bar{\mu}
is an interior point as well, so

\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)\subseteq\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)=\{\mathbf{0}_{\mu}\}

by
Lemma

2.25
and

Proposition

2.22
2
; hence the witnesses in

Definition

2.24
satisfy

\xi_{n}=\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}\to\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
,
and
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})=\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
.

2.

We use
Proposition

2.35
.
We write
\mathcal{C}=\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,|\,\mathcal{J}(\mu)\leq 0\}

with
\mathcal{J}(\mu)\coloneqq W_{2}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}
,
which is proper and continuous and satisfies
\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})=0
since
\bar{\mu}

lies at the boundary.
By
Proposition

2.8
,
\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})
is not empty and is contained in

{(\pi_{1},2\pi_{1}-2\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})}
,
where the factor 2 arises since there is no
\frac{1}{2}
in front of the Wasserstein distance, and the additive constant
-\varepsilon^{2}
does not affect the subgradient (
Corollary

2.17
).
Since
\bar{\mu}
lies at the boundary, we have
\bar{\mu}\neq\hat{\mu}
and so
\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
does not contain the “identity transport plan”
{(\mathrm{Id},\mathrm{Id})}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}
.
If
\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}={(\pi_{1},2(\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}))}_{\#}{\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
, then
x=\hat{x}
holds
\gamma\text{-a.e.}
, i.e.,
\gamma
is the identity transport plan.
Thus,
\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
does not belong to the subgradient of
\mathcal{J}
, and the constraint qualification in
Proposition

2.35
holds true.
Moreover, by
Proposition

2.32
,
\mathcal{J}
has trivial horizon subgradient.
Here,
Proposition

2.32
covers

\frac{1}{2}W_{2}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)^{2}
, and the property transfers
to
\mathcal{J}
since positive scaling and additive constants do not affect the horizon
subgradient. Indeed,
\epi\mathcal{J}
is the image of

\epi\left(\frac{1}{2}W_{2}\left(\cdot,\hat{\mu}\right)^{2}\right)
under the
map
\mu\times\delta_{\beta}\mapsto\mu\times\delta_{2\beta-\varepsilon^{2}}
;
correspondingly, the (regular) epigraphical normals of the two epigraphs are in bijection,
via rescaling of the last velocity component by
\frac{1}{2}
, which preserves convergence
and the zero-expectation condition in

Definition

2.30
; since the extraction map
(
2.9
) discards that component, the two horizon
subdifferentials coincide.
With this,
Proposition

2.35

bounds the strong normal cone by the cone generated by the subdifferential of

\mathcal{J}
at
\bar{\mu}
, together with its horizon subdifferential; the
latter is trivial by the above, so

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\{\lambda\xi\,|\,\lambda\geq 0,\ \xi\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\bar{\mu})\}

and hence

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\{\xi\,|\,\xi\in{(\pi_{1},2\lambda(\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}))}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})},\lambda\geq 0\}.

In particular, if
\bar{\mu}
is absolutely continuous, then
\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})=\{{(\mathrm{Id},T)}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}\}
, where
T
is the unique optimal transport map
[
53
]
, and

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\{{(\mathrm{Id},2\lambda(\mathrm{Id}-T))}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}}\,|\,\lambda\geq 0\}.

Note that the argument of this item applies verbatim at every
boundary point of
\mathcal{C}
; i.e., for every
\mu\in\mathcal{C}

with
W_{2}\left(\mu,\hat{\mu}\right)=\varepsilon
we have

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu)\subseteq\{\xi\,|\,\xi\in{(\pi_{1},2\lambda(\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}))}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\hat{\mu})},\lambda\geq 0\}
.

3.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\bar{\mu}
lies at the boundary of the Wasserstein ball; otherwise, the result follows directly from (i).
Consider any sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
,
\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
and
\xi_{n}\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n})
so that
\xi_{n}\rightharpoonup\xi
. By the above, since
\xi_{n}
belongs to the strong regular normal cone, we necessarily have
\xi_{n}=2\lambda_{n}{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma_{n}}
for some
\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{n},\hat{\mu})
and
\lambda_{n}\geq 0
.
Indeed,

\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n})\subseteq\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu_{n})
, so if

\mu_{n}
lies at the boundary the representation follows from the
previous item applied at
\mu_{n}
; if instead
\mu_{n}
lies in
the interior, then

\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n})\subseteq\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n})=\{\mathbf{0}_{\mu_{n}}\}

by
Lemma

2.25
and

Proposition

2.22
2
, so the representation holds with

\lambda_{n}=0
and any

\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{n},\hat{\mu})
.
By stability of optimal transport plans
[
51
, Theorem 5.20]
, we have that, up to passing to a subsequence,
\gamma_{n}\rightharpoonup\gamma
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})
.
We now consider two cases separately.

(a)

Suppose that, up to subsequences,
\lambda_{n}
converges to
\lambda\coloneqq\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{n}\geq 0
.
Since
\lambda_{n}\to\lambda
, the maps
(x,\hat{x})\mapsto(x,2\lambda_{n}(x-\hat{x}))

converge to
(x,\hat{x})\mapsto(x,2\lambda(x-\hat{x}))
uniformly on compact sets;
as
\gamma_{n}\rightharpoonup\gamma
,
[
35
, Lemma 5.2.1]

yields

\xi_{n}\rightharpoonup 2\lambda{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma}
.
Since, by assumption,
\xi_{n}\rightharpoonup\xi
, uniqueness of
the narrow limit gives

\xi=2\lambda{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma}
, with
\gamma
as above.
Then, since narrow convergence together with convergence of the
second moment is equivalent to convergence in
Wasserstein
[
51
, Theorem 6.9]
, it suffices to establish
convergence of the second moment to conclude
\xi_{n}\to\xi
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{n}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi_{n}(x_{n},\upsilon)={}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{n}\right\|^{2}+4\lambda_{n}^{2}\left\|x_{n}-\hat{x}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma_{n}(x_{n},\hat{x})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{n}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}(x_{n})+4\lambda_{n}^{2}W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\hat{\mu}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\to{}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\bar{x}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})+4\lambda^{2}W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\bar{x}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi(\bar{x},\upsilon),

where we used convergence in Wasserstein of
\mu_{n}
to
\bar{\mu}
.
Thus,
\xi_{n}\to\xi
.

(b)

We consider the case
\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{n}=+\infty
, and show that this contradicts narrow convergence of
\xi_{n}
.
Since the radius of the ball is strictly positive (i.e.,
\varepsilon>0
) and
\bar{\mu}
lies at the boundary, we necessarily have
\bar{\mu}\neq\hat{\mu}
. Thus, there are
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}>0
so that
\gamma(\{\left\|\bar{x}-\hat{x}\right\|>\eta_{1}\})\geq\eta_{2}
.
By Portmanteau theorem, we have
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\gamma_{n}(\{\left\|x_{n}-\hat{x}\right\|>\eta_{1}\})\geq\gamma(\{\left\|\bar{x}-\hat{x}\right\|>\eta_{1}\})\geq\eta_{2}
, so that
\gamma_{n}(\{\left\|x_{n}-\hat{x}\right\|>\eta_{1}\})\geq\frac{\eta_{2}}{2}
for
n
large enough. The definition of
\xi_{n}
then yields, for
n
large enough (in particular,
\lambda_{n}>0
),

({\pi_{2}}_{\#}{\xi_{n}})(\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>2\lambda_{n}\eta_{1}\})=\gamma_{n}(\{2\lambda_{n}\left\|x_{n}-\hat{x}\right\|>2\lambda_{n}\eta_{1}\})\geq\frac{\eta_{2}}{2}.

However, since it converges narrowly,
\xi_{n}
is tight by Prokhorov theorem
[
35
, Theorem 5.1.3]
, and hence so is
{\pi_{2}}_{\#}{\xi_{n}}
.
Thus, there is
R>0
large enough so that
({\pi_{2}}_{\#}{\xi_{n}})(\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>R\})<\frac{\eta_{2}}{2}
. For
n
large enough,
2\eta_{1}\lambda_{n}>R
and we conclude

\frac{\eta_{2}}{2}\leq({\pi_{2}}_{\#}{\xi_{n}})(\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>2\lambda_{n}\eta_{1}\})\leq({\pi_{2}}_{\#}{\xi_{n}})(\{\left\|\upsilon\right\|>R\})<\frac{\eta_{2}}{2}.

This is a contradiction. The same argument applies along any subsequence with

\lambda_{n}\to+\infty
: extracting, as above, a further subsequence with

\gamma_{n}\rightharpoonup\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu})

[
51
, Theorem 5.20]
, the contradiction persists, since

(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight. Hence,

(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded.

Every subsequence of

(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
then admits a further subsequence along which

\lambda_{n}
and
\gamma_{n}
converge, and by case (a) it converges to

\xi
in Wasserstein. Since this limit does not depend on the
subsequence, the whole sequence satisfies

\xi_{n}\to\xi
.
Overall, we can therefore conclude that the closed Wasserstein
ball is
SNC
at
\bar{\mu}
.

Appendix C
Proofs for
Section

3

Here, we follow the strategy outlined in
Section

2.5.2
, so we start by proving the first-order optimality conditions for a differentiable functional:

Proposition C.1

(First-order optimality conditions, the differentiable case)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
be proper, and
\mu^{\ast}\in\mathcal{C}
be a local minimum of
\mathcal{J}
over
\mathcal{C}
.

1.

If
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable at
\mu^{\ast}
, then
-\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu^{\ast})\subseteq{}\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu^{\ast})
.

2.

If
\mathcal{J}
is strongly differentiable at
\mu^{\ast}
, then
-\nabla^{s}{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu^{\ast})\subseteq{}\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})
.

Proof C.2
.

Proof.
For
1
, let
\mathcal{J}
be differentiable at
\mu^{\ast}
. By
Proposition

2.4
3
,
-\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\in\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\mu^{\ast})
, so, applying
Definition

2.2
1
to
-\mathcal{J}
, for any
\mu\in\mathcal{C}
sufficiently close to
\mu^{\ast}
and
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\mu^{\ast},\mu)}
we have

\left\langle-\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast}),\xi\right\rangle_{\mu^{\ast}}\leq-\bigl(\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\bigr)+o\left(W_{2}\left(\mu^{\ast},\mu\right)\right)\leq o\left(W_{2}\left(\mu^{\ast},\mu\right)\right),

where the last inequality follows from local optimality of
\mu^{\ast}
.
Hence,
-\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\in\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu^{\ast})\subseteq\normal_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu^{\ast})
, which proves
1
.

The proof of
2
is analogous, replacing differentiability with strong differentiability (
Definition

2.26
), so that
-\nabla^{s}{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\in\hat{\partial}^{s}(-\mathcal{J})(\mu^{\ast})
,
\Gamma_{o}(\mu^{\ast},\mu)
with
\Gamma(\mu^{\ast},\mu)
, and the regular normal cone with its strong counterpart (
Definition

2.24
).
Since
W_{2}\left(\mu^{\ast},\mu\right)\leq\left\|\xi\right\|_{\mu^{\ast}}
for every
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma(\mu^{\ast},\mu)}
, the
o\left(\left\|\xi\right\|_{\mu^{\ast}}\right)
condition still forces
\mu\to\mu^{\ast}
as
\left\|\xi\right\|_{\mu^{\ast}}\to 0
, and the same argument applies.

The proof of our main result lifts the problem to the epigraph, where the objective becomes a linear functional; the next lemma shows that such functionals are strongly differentiable, so that
Proposition

C.1
applies.

Lemma C.3

(Strong differentiability of linear functionals)
.

Let
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
l:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
be the expected value
l(\mu)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[\left\langle c,x\right\rangle\right]
. Then,
l
is strongly differentiable at every
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with strong gradient
\nabla^{s}{l}(\bar{\mu})=\nabla{l}(\bar{\mu})=\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c}
.

Proof C.4
.

Proof.
To start, membership of
\pm(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c})
to the tangent space follows from
Proposition

2.12
applied to
\pm l
.
Then, fix
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
For every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\gamma\in\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)
, the variation
\xi={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma}
satisfies

l(\mu)-l(\bar{\mu})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle c,x\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\mu(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle c,x\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle c,\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)=\left\langle\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c},\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}},

where the last equality holds because the arrow of
\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c}
equals
c
almost everywhere, so the integral in (
2.4
) takes the value
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle c,\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)
for every coupling
\alpha\in\Gamma^{1}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c},\xi)
, and the maximum trivializes.
As this identity holds with no remainder, applying it to
l
and to
-l
gives
\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c}\in\hat{\partial}^{s}l(\bar{\mu})
and
-\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c}\in\hat{\partial}^{s}(-l)(\bar{\mu})
, respectively. Hence,
\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c}\in-\hat{\partial}^{s}(-l)(\bar{\mu})\cap\hat{\partial}^{s}l(\bar{\mu})
, and
l
is strongly differentiable with
\nabla^{s}{l}(\bar{\mu})=\nabla{l}(\bar{\mu})=\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c}
.

We record a companion to
Lemma

C.3
: the normal
cone and
SNC
property of a constraint set defined as the level set of an expected value of a linear function. We will prove this result from the definition and use it to prove
Proposition

2.35
.

Lemma C.5

(Normal cone of a linear constraint)
.

Let
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, let
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, and let

l:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
be the linear functional

l(\mu)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\left[\left\langle c,x\right\rangle\right]
. Consider

\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,|\,l(\mu)\leq\gamma\right\}.

Then,
\mathcal{C}
is closed and
SNC
at every

\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}
, and

\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})=\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})=\begin{cases}\left\{\lambda\,(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c})\,|\,\lambda\geq 0\right\}&\text{if }l(\bar{\mu})=\gamma,\\[2.0pt]
\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}&\text{if }l(\bar{\mu})<\gamma.\end{cases}

Proof C.6
.

Proof.
We divide the proof into steps.

Step 1 (two preliminaries).
For a variation
\boldsymbol{a}
at a point

\hat{\mu}
whose arrow equals a constant
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
almost everywhere, the
maximization in (
2.4
) is vacuous, as in the proof of
Lemma

C.3
. Thus,

\left\langle\boldsymbol{a},\xi\right\rangle_{\hat{\mu}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle a,\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)\qquad\text{for every variation }\xi\text{ at }\hat{\mu}.

(C.1)

This applies in
particular to
\hat{\mu}\times\delta_{a}
, which lies in

{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\hat{\mu})}
by

Lemma

C.3
.
Moreover,
l
is
\left\|c\right\|
-Lipschitz
with respect to
W_{2}
(its integrand

x\mapsto\left\langle c,x\right\rangle
is
\left\|c\right\|
-Lipschitz), hence continuous, so

\mathcal{C}=l^{-1}((-\infty,\gamma])
is closed.

Step 2 (interior points).
If
l(\bar{\mu})<\gamma
, then, by continuity of

l
,
\bar{\mu}
lies in the interior of
\mathcal{C}
, and

Lemma

2.25
with

Proposition

2.22
2

give

\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\hat{\normal}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})=\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
.
We assume
l(\bar{\mu})=\gamma
throughout Steps 3 and 4.

Step 3 (the ray consists of normals).
Let
\lambda\geq 0
. For every

\mu\in\mathcal{C}
and every

\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu)}
,
the arrow of
\lambda\,(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c})
is the constant
\lambda c
, so
(
C.1
) gives

\left\langle\lambda\,(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c}),\xi\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle c,\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)=\lambda\bigl(l(\mu)-l(\bar{\mu})\bigr)=\lambda\bigl(l(\mu)-\gamma\bigr)\leq 0,

where the second equality holds for any transport plan because

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle c,\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle c,x^{\prime}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\mu(x^{\prime})-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle c,x\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}(x)=l(\mu)-l(\bar{\mu})
.
As
\lambda\,(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c})\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu})}

by Step 1, this shows

\lambda\,(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c})\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
.

Step 4 (every strong regular normal lies on the ray).
Let

\bar{\xi}\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
and
set
m\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle c,\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\bar{\xi}(x,\upsilon)
. We first
record which variations point into
\mathcal{C}
: the endpoint

\mu_{\xi}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1}+\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi}

of a variation
\xi
at
\bar{\mu}
satisfies

l(\mu_{\xi})=\gamma+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle c,\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)
,
so
\mu_{\xi}\in\mathcal{C}
if and only if

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle c,\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi(x,\upsilon)\leq 0
, and then

\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma(\bar{\mu},\mu_{\xi})}
.

(i) The multiplier is nonnegative.
For
s>0
, the variation

\xi_{s}\coloneqq\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{-sc}
has constant arrow
-sc
, so

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle c,\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi_{s}(x,\upsilon)=-s\left\|c\right\|^{2}\leq 0
; hence

\xi_{s}
points into
\mathcal{C}
, with

\left\|\xi_{s}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}=s\left\|c\right\|
. By
(
C.1
),

\left\langle\bar{\xi},\xi_{s}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}=-s\,m
, and the defining
inequality of
\bar{\xi}
(with
\mu_{\xi_{s}}\to\bar{\mu}
as

s\searrow 0
) yields

-s\,m\leq o\left(\left\|\xi_{s}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\right)=o\left(s\right)
; dividing by

s
gives
m\geq 0
.

(ii) The normal is deterministic and aligned with
c
.
Write

\hat{c}\coloneqq c/\left\|c\right\|
and let

\bar{\xi}_{0}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\tfrac{m}{\left\|c\right\|^{2}}c)}_{\#}{\bar{\xi}}

be the variation with the same base as
\bar{\xi}
and arrow

\upsilon_{0}\coloneqq\upsilon-\tfrac{m}{\left\|c\right\|^{2}}c
. Its mean

c
-component vanishes,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle c,\upsilon_{0}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\bar{\xi}_{0}(x,\upsilon_{0})=m-m=0
, so

s\bar{\xi}_{0}
points into
\mathcal{C}
for every
s\geq 0
.
Since

{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{2}-\tfrac{m}{\left\|c\right\|^{2}}c)}_{\#}{\bar{\xi}}\in\Gamma^{1}(\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi}_{0})
,
(
2.4
) and the split

\upsilon=\left\langle\hat{c},\upsilon\right\rangle\hat{c}+\upsilon^{\perp}

into components parallel and orthogonal to
c
give, using

m=\left\|c\right\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\hat{c},\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\bar{\xi}(x,\upsilon)
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi}_{0}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\upsilon,\upsilon-\tfrac{m}{\left\|c\right\|^{2}}c\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\bar{\xi}(x,\upsilon)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\xi}(x,\upsilon)-\frac{m^{2}}{\left\|c\right\|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\hat{c},\upsilon\right\rangle^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\xi}(x,\upsilon)-\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\hat{c},\upsilon\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\bar{\xi}(x,\upsilon)\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon^{\perp}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{\xi}(x,\upsilon).

Both terms are nonnegative (the first by Jensen’s inequality).
On the other hand, the defining inequality of
\bar{\xi}
along

s\bar{\xi}_{0}
(
s\searrow 0
), with homogeneity
(
Proposition

A.9
4
), gives

\left\langle\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi}_{0}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}}\leq 0
. Hence both
nonnegative terms vanish:
\upsilon^{\perp}=0
almost everywhere, and

\left\langle\hat{c},\upsilon\right\rangle
is
\bar{\xi}\text{-a.e.}
equal to its
mean
\tfrac{m}{\left\|c\right\|}
. Therefore
\upsilon=\tfrac{m}{\left\|c\right\|^{2}}c

almost everywhere, i.e.,
\bar{\xi}=\tfrac{m}{\left\|c\right\|^{2}}(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c})

with
\tfrac{m}{\left\|c\right\|^{2}}\geq 0
by (i). With Step 3, this proves

\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})=\{\lambda\,(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c})\,|\,\lambda\geq 0\}
.

Step 5 (
SNC
and the strong normal cone).
Fix

\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}
and take

\mu_{n}\to\bar{\mu}
in
\mathcal{C}
and

\xi_{n}\in\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu_{n})

with
\xi_{n}\rightharpoonup\xi
. By Steps 2–4,

\xi_{n}=\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\lambda_{n}c}
for some
\lambda_{n}\geq 0
.
Narrow convergence makes
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
tight (Prokhorov,

[
35
, Theorem 5.1.3]
); as the arrow marginal of
\xi_{n}

is
\delta_{\lambda_{n}c}
and
c\neq 0
, this forces
(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
to
be bounded. Along any subsequence with
\lambda_{n}\to\lambda
, coupling

\xi_{n}=\mu_{n}\times\delta_{\lambda_{n}c}
and

\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\lambda c}
through an optimal transport plan of

(\mu_{n},\bar{\mu})
and the (deterministic) arrows gives

W_{2}\left(\xi_{n},\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\lambda c}\right)^{2}\leq W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\bar{\mu}\right)^{2}+\left\|\lambda_{n}c-\lambda c\right\|^{2}\to 0,

so
\xi_{n}\to\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\lambda c}
; as
convergence in Wasserstein implies narrow convergence and the narrow limit is unique,

\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{\lambda c}=\xi
, which determines
\lambda
uniquely
(
c\neq 0
). Thus every subsequence of the bounded
(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
has a
further subsequence converging to this same
\lambda
, whence
\lambda_{n}\to\lambda
and

\xi_{n}\to\xi=\lambda\,(\bar{\mu}\times\delta_{c})
.
This is the
SNC
property. Finally,

\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})

always, while the argument just made shows every element of

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})
lies on the ray. At boundary points (
l(\bar{\mu})=\gamma
) this
yields
\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})=\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{\mu})
;
at interior points (
l(\bar{\mu})<\gamma
), continuity of
l
gives

l(\mu_{n})<\gamma
for
n
large, so
\lambda_{n}=0
by Step 2 and

\xi=\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}
, whence

\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})=\{\mathbf{0}_{\bar{\mu}}\}
,
in agreement with Step 2.

Armed with
Proposition

C.1
and
Lemma

C.3
, we are ready to prove our main result and its differentiable case:

Proof C.7
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.2
.
The proof strategy follows
[
46
, Theorem 8.15]
.

Step 1 (reduction to a linear program over the epigraph).
Consider in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
the sets

D=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})\,|\,{(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{C}\}

and
E=\epi\mathcal{J}\cap D
.
4
4

4

Since
every element of
\epi\mathcal{J}
has a Dirac last marginal,

E=\epi\mathcal{J}\cap D=\epi\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{C}_{e}
. We work
here with
D\supsetneq\mathcal{C}_{e}
rather than
\mathcal{C}_{e}

because
D
forces the last velocity component of its (strong) normals to vanish
and inherits
SNC
from
\mathcal{C}
via

Proposition

D.6
1
, whereas

\mathcal{C}_{e}
constrains only the mean of the last velocity component
(
Proposition

D.6
2
with

B=\mathbb{R}
); its normals may thus have arbitrary zero-mean vertical mass, so

\mathcal{C}_{e}
is not
SNC
. This is what makes assumption (ii) of

Theorem

D.13
verifiable.

We have already argued that for
\mu^{\ast}\in\mathcal{C}
to be optimal for
\mathcal{J}
over
\mathcal{C}
,
\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})}
needs to be optimal for
l(\boldsymbol{\mu})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\boldsymbol{\mu}}\left[\left\langle(0,\ldots,0,1),\boldsymbol{x}\right\rangle\right]
over
E
. We can deploy the optimality conditions in
Proposition

C.1
, since
l
is strongly differentiable (
Lemma

C.3
) with gradient
\nabla^{s}{l}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})=\nabla{l}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})=(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})\times\boldsymbol{\delta_{(0,\ldots,0,1)}}
.

Step 2 (the normal cones of the epigraph and the constraint set).
To derive the result from
Proposition

C.1
, we need to characterize
\normal_{E}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})
.
For this, we use
Theorem

D.13
.
To start, we note that
\epi\mathcal{J}
is locally closed by local lower semicontinuity of
\mathcal{J}
and
D
is locally closed since
\mathcal{C}
is locally closed and the first-marginal map is continuous.
Before verifying assumptions (i) and (ii) in
Theorem

D.13
we evaluate the strong normal cones of
\epi\mathcal{J}
and
D
.
For
\epi\mathcal{J}
,
Proposition

2.34
and
Lemma

2.25
yield

\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})\subseteq\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})=G\cup H

where

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\coloneqq\biggl\{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})_{\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})}}\,|\,\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle\boldsymbol{(0,\ldots,0,1)},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=-\lambda<0,\frac{1}{\lambda}{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\biggr\}

(C.2)

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\coloneqq\biggl\{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})_{\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})}}\,|\,\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle\boldsymbol{(0,\ldots,0,1)},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=0,{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\biggr\}.

For the strong normal cone to
D
, we use

Proposition

D.6
1
(with
the unconstrained factor in the last coordinate; the relabeling of the
coordinates is immaterial) to obtain

\normal_{D}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})\subseteq F

where

F\coloneqq\{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1})_{\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})}}\,|\,{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}),{(\pi_{d+1},\pi_{2d+2})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\mathbf{0}_{\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})}}\}.

Step 3 (verifying the intersection rule).
We now check assumption (i) of
Theorem

D.13
, namely that
\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})\cap\left(-\normal_{D}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})\right)=\{\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})}}\}
.
By Step 2 and (
C.2
),

\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})\cap\left(-\normal_{D}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})\right)\subseteq(G\cup H)\cap(-F)
.
We observe that
G\cap(-F)=\emptyset
. Indeed, every
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in-F
satisfies
{(\pi_{d+1},\pi_{2d+2})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\mathbf{0}_{\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})}}
, so
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle\boldsymbol{(0,\ldots,0,1)},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]=0
, whereas every element of
G
satisfies
\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left[\left\langle\boldsymbol{(0,\ldots,0,1)},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]<0
.
Thus,
(G\cup H)\cap(-F)=H\cap(-F)
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in H\cap(-F)
.
Since membership in
H
gives
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})
and membership in
-F
gives
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in-\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})
, the constraint qualification
\partial^{\infty}\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\cap\left(-\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})\right)=\{\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\}
forces
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}
. Membership in
-F
also gives
{(\pi_{d+1},\pi_{2d+2})}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\mathbf{0}_{\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})}}
and so
\boldsymbol{\xi}=\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})}}
.
Consequently,
H\cap(-F)=\{\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})}}\}
, whence, since the zero variation is a strong regular normal to every set at every point (its pairing with any variation vanishes, and it belongs to the tangent cone),
\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})\cap\left(-\normal_{D}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})\right)=\{\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})}}\}
, which is assumption (i).
It remains to check assumption (ii):
D
is
SNC
in virtue of
Proposition

D.6
1
, as
\mathcal{C}
is
SNC
by assumption. Thus,
Theorem

D.13
yields the inclusion

\normal_{E}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})\subseteq\normal_{D}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})+\normal_{\epi\mathcal{J}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})\subseteq F+(G\cup H).

(C.3)

Step 4 (ruling out the
F+H
decomposition).
In light of
Proposition

C.1
, we need to show that

-\nabla{l}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})\in\normal_{E}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})
implies

\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})+\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})
.
Given (
C.3
), there are two possibilities:
(a) there are
\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\in F,\boldsymbol{\xi_{H}}\in H
, and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Gamma^{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}},\boldsymbol{\xi_{H}})
so that
\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\,+_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi_{H}}=-\nabla{l}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})
or
(b) there are
\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\in F,\boldsymbol{\xi_{G}}\in G
, and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Gamma^{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}},\boldsymbol{\xi_{G}})
so that
\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\,+_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi_{G}}=-\nabla{l}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})
. We now show that the former is not possible.
To this end, we observe that, for all
\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\in F,\boldsymbol{\xi_{H}}\in H
, there is no
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Gamma^{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}},\boldsymbol{\xi_{H}})
so that
\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\,+_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi_{H}}=-\nabla{l}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})
. This follows by contradiction accounting for the linearity of the expected value:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim(\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\,+_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi_{H}})}\left[\left\langle\boldsymbol{(0,\ldots,0,1)},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\left\langle\boldsymbol{(0,\ldots,0,1)},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}(\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\,+_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi_{H}})(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\left\langle\boldsymbol{(0,\ldots,0,1)},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{F}}+\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{H}}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{F}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{H}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\left\langle\boldsymbol{(0,\ldots,0,1)},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{F}}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{F}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{H}})

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\left\langle\boldsymbol{(0,\ldots,0,1)},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{H}}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{F}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{H}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}}\left[\left\langle\boldsymbol{(0,\ldots,0,1)},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim\boldsymbol{\xi_{H}}}\left[\left\langle\boldsymbol{(0,\ldots,0,1)},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=0\neq-1=\mathbb{E}^{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})\sim-\nabla{l}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})}\left[\left\langle\boldsymbol{(0,\ldots,0,1)},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rangle\right].

We thus conclude that
-\nabla{l}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})\in\normal_{E}^{s}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})
implies that
\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\in F,\boldsymbol{\xi_{G}}\in G
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Gamma^{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}},\boldsymbol{\xi_{G}})
exist so that
-\nabla{l}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})=\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\,+_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi_{G}}
.

Step 5 (deriving the optimality condition).

Since
{T}_{\#}{\nabla{l}(\mu^{\ast}\times\delta_{\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})})}=\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}
, it necessarily holds
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}={T}_{\#}{(\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\,+_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi_{G}})}
.
We now show that
{T}_{\#}{(\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\,+_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi_{G}})}={T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}}\,+_{{\hat{T}}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\,{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{G}}}
, where
\hat{T}:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined as

\hat{T}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{1}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{2}})=((\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})(\boldsymbol{x}),(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})(\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{1}}),(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})(\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{2}}))
. Indeed, for all
\phi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x^{\ast},\upsilon)\mathrm{d}({T}_{\#}{(\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\,+_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi_{G}})})(x^{\ast},\upsilon)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\phi(T(\boldsymbol{x^{\ast}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}))\mathrm{d}(\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}\,+_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi_{G}})(\boldsymbol{x^{\ast}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\phi(T(\boldsymbol{x^{\ast}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{1}}+\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{2}}))\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{x^{\ast}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{1}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{2}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\phi((\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})(\boldsymbol{x^{\ast}}),(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})(\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{1}}+\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{2}}))\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{x^{\ast}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{1}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{2}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\phi((\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})(\boldsymbol{x^{\ast}}),(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})(\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{1}})+(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d})(\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{2}}))\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{x^{\ast}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{1}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon_{2}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x^{\ast},\upsilon_{1}+\upsilon_{2})\mathrm{d}({\hat{T}}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})(x^{\ast},\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle={\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x^{\ast},\upsilon)\mathrm{d}({T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}}\,+_{{\hat{T}}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\,{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{G}}})(x^{\ast},\upsilon).}

The structure of
F
and
G
implies that
{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{F}}}\in\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})
and
\frac{1}{\lambda}{T}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\xi_{G}}}\in\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})
for some
\lambda>0
.
Thus,
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\in\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})+\lambda\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})
.
Since the strong normal cone is a cone (the defining inequality of
Definition

2.24
is positively homogeneous in the normal, and the velocity scaling
{(\pi_{1},\lambda\pi_{2})}_{\#}{}
is Lipschitz with respect to
W_{2}
, so positive scaling is also preserved along the limits), we can scale by
\frac{1}{\lambda}
and obtain
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\in\normal_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(\mu^{\ast})+\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})
, proving the statement.

Step 6 (the (strongly) differentiable case).

To conclude, if
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable (resp. strongly differentiable), then
Proposition

C.1
1
(resp.
Proposition

C.1
2
) directly establishes the result.

Proof C.8
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.3
.
Since
\mu^{\ast}
is a local minimum,
\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\geq 0
for all
\mu
in a neighborhood of
\mu^{\ast}
of radius
r>0
.
Recall from

Proposition

A.9
6
that

\left\langle\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}},\xi\right\rangle_{\mu^{\ast}}=0
for

every

\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\mu^{\ast}}
and, in particular, for all

\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and all
\xi\in{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\Gamma(\mu^{\ast},\mu)}
induced by an
arbitrary (not necessarily optimal) plan.
Since

\mathcal{J}
is proper with
\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\in\mathbb{R}
, the function

h(\mu)\coloneqq\min\{0,\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\}
is real-valued, satisfies
\mathcal{J}(\mu)-\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\geq\left\langle\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}},\xi\right\rangle_{\mu^{\ast}}+h(\mu)
for all such
\mu
and
\xi
, and vanishes
whenever
W_{2}\left(\mu^{\ast},\mu\right)<r
.
Because
\left\|\xi\right\|_{\mu^{\ast}}\geq W_{2}\left(\mu^{\ast},\mu\right)
for every
\xi

induced by a plan between
\mu^{\ast}
and
\mu
, the condition

\left\|\xi\right\|_{\mu^{\ast}}<r
forces
h(\mu)=0
; hence

h(\mu)=o\left(\left\|\xi\right\|_{\mu^{\ast}}\right)
.
As this bound holds along all plans,
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\in\hat{\partial}^{s}\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\subseteq\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})

(
Lemma

2.25
1
),
which readily implies
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\in\partial^{s}\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})\subseteq\partial\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})
.
Finally, if
\mathcal{J}
is differentiable (resp. strongly differentiable)
at
\mu^{\ast}
, then, by

Proposition

2.4
3

(resp.
Definition

2.26
and

Lemma

2.25
1
),

\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\in-\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\mu^{\ast})

(resp.
\nabla^{s}{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})\in-\hat{\partial}^{s}(-\mathcal{J})(\mu^{\ast})\subseteq-\hat{\partial}(-\mathcal{J})(\mu^{\ast})
).
Combining this with
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}\in\hat{\partial}\mathcal{J}(\mu^{\ast})
and

Proposition

2.4
1

yields
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}=\nabla{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})

(resp.
\mathbf{0}_{\mu^{\ast}}=\nabla^{s}{\mathcal{J}}(\mu^{\ast})
).

Appendix D
Extremal principle and intersection rule

In this section, we establish the intersection rule for the strong normal cone: Under an appropriate constraint qualification, we have

\normal_{\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})\subseteq\normal_{\Omega_{1}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})+\normal_{\Omega_{2}}^{s}(\bar{\mu})
.
This intersection rule plays a fundamental role in variational analysis and, among others, in the proof of our first-order optimality conditions in
Theorem

3.2
.
In the Euclidean case and when all sets are convex, the intersection rule follows from a convex separation principle, which however fails to apply to the infinite-dimensional and nonconvex case presented here.
We therefore follow the strategy of variational analysis in linear spaces (in particular, Banach spaces and Asplund spaces)
[
47
,
48
]
and prove the intersection rule via an extremal principle.
An extremal principle provides necessary conditions for local extremal points of general (nonconvex) sets in terms of the normal cone at those points. It can be interpreted as the “variational version” of the convex separation principle; see the discussion in
[
47
, Section 2]
.
Importantly, the Wasserstein space is not a linear space (as opposed to Banach and Asplund spaces), but is a metric space, so the results
[
47
,
48
]
do not directly apply.
Accordingly, in
Section

D.1
, we establish an extremal principle in the Wasserstein space, which we believe to be of independent interest.
Then, we establish the product rule in
Section

D.2
and the intersection rule in
Section

D.3
.

D.1
Extremal principle

We start with a definition of the extremal principle in the Wasserstein space:

Definition D.1

(Extremal system)
.

Let
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be nonempty sets and let
\bar{\mu}\in\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}
.
The probability measure
\bar{\mu}
is a local extremal point of
(\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2})
if there is a neighborhood
U
of
\bar{\mu}
so that for all
\varepsilon>0
there is
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left\|a\right\|<\varepsilon
such that

\left({(\mathrm{Id}+a)}_{\#}{\Omega_{1}}\right)\cap\Omega_{2}\cap U=\emptyset
.
In this case,
(\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},\bar{\mu})
is called extremal.

Informally, local extremality means that two sets of probability measures can be separated by “moving”, via a pushforward operation, one of them.
In the case of delta distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we recover the definition of extremal system in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; e.g., see
[
49
]
or
[
47
, Definition 2.1]
in the finite-dimensional case.
We next turn our focus to necessary conditions for
(\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},\bar{\mu})
to be extremal.

Theorem D.2

(Extremal principle)
.

Let
(\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},\bar{\mu})
be extremal with

\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}
locally closed around
\bar{\mu}
.
Then, for every
\varepsilon>0
, there are probability measures
\bar{\mu}_{i}\in\Omega_{i}
with
W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu}_{i},\bar{\mu}\right)<\varepsilon
, an optimal transport plan
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu}_{1},\bar{\mu}_{2})
, and a shift
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
so that, with
c\coloneqq W_{2}\left({(\mathrm{Id}+a)}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}_{1}},\bar{\mu}_{2}\right)>0
, the tangent vectors

\xi_{1}\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{c}{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1}-a)}_{\#}{\gamma}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu}_{1})}

and

\xi_{2}\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{c}{(\pi_{2},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}+a)}_{\#}{\gamma}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu}_{2})},

with
\left\|\xi_{1}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}_{1}}=\left\|\xi_{2}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}_{2}}=1
, are
\varepsilon
-close with respect to
W_{\bar{\mu}_{i}}
to the strong regular normal cones
\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\Omega_{1}}(\bar{\mu}_{1})
and
\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\Omega_{2}}(\bar{\mu}_{2})
, respectively. In particular, there is
\gamma_{\xi}\in\Gamma(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
with
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\gamma_{\xi}}=\gamma
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{1}+\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma_{\xi}(x_{1},\upsilon_{1},x_{2},\upsilon_{2})=0
.

Informally,
Theorem

D.2
shows that if a probability measure
\bar{\mu}
is a locally extremal point of
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
then at probability measures arbitrarily close to
\bar{\mu}
there are unit tangent vectors, arbitrarily close to the strong regular normal cones of
\Omega_{1}
and of
\Omega_{2}
, which oppose each other.
The proof of
Theorem

D.2
relies on the following technical lemma:

Lemma D.3

(Fuzzy optimality condition)
.

Let
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be nonempty sets and let
(\bar{\mu}_{1},\bar{\mu}_{2})\in\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
{(\mathrm{Id}+a)}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}_{1}}\neq\bar{\mu}_{2}
, so that
c\coloneqq W_{2}\left({(\mathrm{Id}+a)}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}_{1}},\bar{\mu}_{2}\right)>0
.
Consider sequences
((\tilde{\mu}_{1,j},\tilde{\mu}_{2,j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}
so that

W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu}_{i},\tilde{\mu}_{i,j}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{2^{-j}}{4}\varepsilon^{2}
.
Suppose that
(\bar{\mu}_{1},\bar{\mu}_{2})
is a local minimizer of

\psi(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\coloneqq W_{2}\left({(\mathrm{Id}+a)}_{\#}{\mu}_{1},\mu_{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\left(W_{2}\left(\mu_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{1,j}\right)^{2}+W_{2}\left(\mu_{2},\tilde{\mu}_{2,j}\right)^{2}\right)

relative to the set
\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}
.
Then, there is an optimal transport plan
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu}_{1},\bar{\mu}_{2})
so that

\xi_{1}\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{c}{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1}-a)}_{\#}{\gamma}\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu}_{1})

and

\xi_{2}\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{c}{(\pi_{2},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}+a)}_{\#}{\gamma}\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu}_{2}),

with
\left\|\xi_{1}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}_{1}}=\left\|\xi_{2}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}_{2}}=1
, are
\varepsilon
-close with respect to
W_{\bar{\mu}_{i}}
to the strong regular normal cones
\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\Omega_{1}}(\bar{\mu}_{1})
and
\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\Omega_{2}}(\bar{\mu}_{2})
, respectively.
In particular, there is
\gamma_{\xi}\in\Gamma(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
with
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\gamma_{\xi}}=\gamma
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{1}+\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma_{\xi}(x_{1},\upsilon_{1},x_{2},\upsilon_{2})=0
.

Proof D.4
.

Proof.
Fix
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu}_{1},\bar{\mu}_{2})
and let
c
,
\xi_{1}
, and
\xi_{2}
be as in the statement; we show they enjoy the asserted properties (so, in particular, such a transport plan and such variations exist). Write
W_{2}^{a}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\coloneqq W_{2}\left({(\mathrm{Id}+a)}_{\#}{\mu_{1}},\mu_{2}\right)
for the separation, so that
c=W_{2}^{a}(\bar{\mu}_{1},\bar{\mu}_{2})
. We proceed in six steps.

Step 1 (Unit norm and tangent-cone membership).

We start with the norm.
Since the shift by the constant
a
preserves cyclical monotonicity of the support,
{(\pi_{1}+a,\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma}
is optimal between
{(\mathrm{Id}+a)}_{\#}{\bar{\mu}_{1}}
and
\bar{\mu}_{2}
, whence
\left\|c\xi_{1}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}_{1}}=\left\|c\xi_{2}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}_{2}}=W_{2}^{a}(\bar{\mu}_{1},\bar{\mu}_{2})=c
; that is,
\left\|\xi_{1}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}_{1}}=\left\|\xi_{2}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}_{2}}=1
.
We now show that
\xi_{1}\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu}_{1})
;
the proof for
\xi_{2}
is analogous.
Since
\gamma
is optimal, it is supported on the subdifferential of a convex function
\phi
.
Then, the transport plan
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\tau\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi_{1}}={(\pi_{1},(1-\tfrac{\tau}{c})\pi_{1}+\tfrac{\tau}{c}\pi_{2}-\tfrac{\tau}{c}a)}_{\#}{\gamma}

is supported on the subdifferential of the function
x\mapsto\frac{1-\tau/c}{2}\left\|x\right\|^{2}+\tfrac{\tau}{c}\phi(x)-\tfrac{\tau}{c}\left\langle a,x\right\rangle
, which is clearly convex for all
\tau\in[0,c)
, which proves optimality of
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\tau\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\xi_{1}}
between its marginals for all
\tau
sufficiently small and, with it, that
\xi_{1}\in\tangent^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu}_{1})
.

Step 2 (Cancellation).

Let

\gamma_{\xi}\coloneqq{(\pi_{1},\tfrac{1}{c}(\pi_{2}-\pi_{1}-a),\pi_{2},\tfrac{1}{c}(\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}+a))}_{\#}{\gamma}.

By construction,
\gamma_{\xi}\in\Gamma(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
and
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\gamma_{\xi}}=\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu}_{1},\bar{\mu}_{2})
. Moreover,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\upsilon_{1}+\upsilon_{2}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma_{\xi}(x_{1},\upsilon_{1},x_{2},\upsilon_{2})=\tfrac{1}{c^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|-(x_{1}-x_{2}+a)-(x_{2}-x_{1}-a)\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(x_{1},x_{2})=0
.

Step 3 (The candidate normal
\bar{\xi}
).

Let
\xi_{i,j}\in-{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu}_{i},\tilde{\mu}_{i,j})}
for
i\in\{1,2\},j\in\mathbb{N}
.
By
Proposition

2.5
, we have that
\xi_{i,j}\in\hat{\partial}\left(-\frac{1}{2}W_{2}\left(\cdot,\tilde{\mu}_{i,j}\right)^{2}\right)(\bar{\mu}_{i})
. Define

\bar{\xi}\coloneqq(\xi_{1}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\xi_{1,j},\xi_{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\xi_{2,j})

where, for simplicity of notation, we omit the sum couplings in
\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{i},\xi_{i,0},\xi_{i,1},\xi_{i,2},\ldots)
,
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Since
\left\|\xi_{i,j}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}_{i}}=W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu}_{i},\tilde{\mu}_{i,j}\right)\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}2^{-j/2}
,
\bar{\xi}
is well defined, as detailed below.

Step 4
(
\bar{\xi}
belongs to the tangent space).
We show that the first component
\bar{\xi}_{1}=\xi_{1}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\xi_{1,j}
lies in
{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu}_{1})}
; the argument for
\bar{\xi}_{2}
is identical.
By Step 1, it suffices to exhibit, for all sufficiently small
\tau\geq 0
, a convex function whose subdifferential supports the transport plan
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\tau\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\bar{\xi}_{1}}
, as this renders the transport plan optimal.
Each constituent variation is induced by an optimal transport plan:
\xi_{1}=\tfrac{1}{c}{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1}-a)}_{\#}{\gamma}
with
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu}_{1},\bar{\mu}_{2})
supported on
\partial\phi
, and
\xi_{1,j}={(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma_{j}}
with
\gamma_{j}\in\Gamma_{o}(\bar{\mu}_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{1,j})
supported on
\partial\phi_{j}
, where
\phi
and the
\phi_{j}
are convex.
Gluing
\gamma
and the
\gamma_{j}
along
\bar{\mu}_{1}
yields the sum coupling
\alpha_{1}^{\star}\in\Gamma^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{1,0},\xi_{1,1},\ldots)
of Step 3, a law of tuples
(x,x_{2},y_{0},y_{1},\ldots)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\times_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{R}^{d})
with first marginal
\bar{\mu}_{1}
,
(x,x_{2})\in\supp\gamma
, and
(x,y_{j})\in\supp\gamma_{j}
for every
j
. Thus,
x_{2}\in\partial\phi(x)
and
y_{j}\in\partial\phi_{j}(x)
, and the arrows of
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{1,j}
over
x
are
\tfrac{1}{c}(x_{2}-x-a)
and
y_{j}-x
.
We now proceed in two steps.
First, we show that the series
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\phi_{j}
is a proper convex function.
Normalize each
\phi_{j}
by
\phi_{j}(x_{0})=0
at a base point
x_{0}\in\supp\bar{\mu}_{1}
carrying support points
(x_{0},y_{j,0})\in\supp\gamma_{j}
with
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\left\|y_{j,0}\right\|<\infty
; such an
x_{0}
exists because
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\times_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\left\|y_{j}-x\right\|\mathrm{d}\alpha_{1}^{\star}(x,x_{2},y_{0},y_{1},\ldots)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|v\right\|\mathrm{d}\xi_{1,j}(x,v)\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\left\|\xi_{1,j}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}_{1}}<\infty
, where the equality marginalizes
\alpha_{1}^{\star}
onto each
\xi_{1,j}
and the inequality is Jensen’s (
L^{1}\leq L^{2}
). Hence,
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\left\|y_{j}-x\right\|<\infty

\alpha_{1}^{\star}\text{-a.e.}
, and so
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\left\|y_{j,0}\right\|\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\left\|y_{j,0}-x_{0}\right\|+2\left\|x_{0}\right\|<\infty
for
\alpha_{1}^{\star}
-almost every
(x_{0},y_{0,0},y_{1,0},\ldots)
.
The subgradient inequality then gives
\phi_{j}(z)\geq\phi_{j}(x_{0})+\left\langle y_{j,0},z-x_{0}\right\rangle\geq-\left\|y_{j,0}\right\|\left\|z-x_{0}\right\|
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, whence
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\phi_{j}(z)\geq-\bigl(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\left\|y_{j,0}\right\|\bigr)\left\|z-x_{0}\right\|>-\infty
. As the series vanishes at
x_{0}
, it is a proper convex function.
Second, we construct the desired convex function.
The arrow of
\bar{\xi}_{1}
is
w=\tfrac{1}{c}(x_{2}-x-a)+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}(y_{j}-x)

\alpha_{1}^{\star}\text{-a.e.}
, and for
\tau\in[0,\tfrac{c}{1+2c}]
we set
h_{\tau}\coloneqq\tfrac{1-\tau(\tfrac{1}{c}+2)}{2}\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2}+\tfrac{\tau}{c}\phi+\tau\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\phi_{j}-\tfrac{\tau}{c}\left\langle a,\cdot\right\rangle
.
Since
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\left\|y_{j}-x\right\|<\infty
, summing the subgradient inequalities of the
\phi_{j}
weighted by
2^{-j}
gives
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}y_{j}\in\partial(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\phi_{j})(x)
.
With
x_{2}\in\partial\phi(x)
this yields
x+\tau w=\bigl(1-\tau(\tfrac{1}{c}+2)\bigr)x+\tfrac{\tau}{c}x_{2}+\tau\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}y_{j}-\tfrac{\tau}{c}a\in\partial h_{\tau}(x)
.
Moreover,
h_{\tau}
is proper and convex, its quadratic coefficient
1-\tau(\tfrac{1}{c}+2)
being nonnegative for
\tau\leq\tfrac{c}{1+2c}
(note
\tfrac{1}{c}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}=\tfrac{1}{c}+2
) and
\phi,\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\phi_{j}
proper convex.
Hence
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}+\tau\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\bar{\xi}_{1}}
is supported on
\partial h_{\tau}
and thus optimal for every
\tau\in[0,\tfrac{c}{1+2c}]
, so
\bar{\xi}_{1}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu}_{1})}
.
The same argument gives
\bar{\xi}_{2}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu}_{2})}
.

Step 5 (
\bar{\xi}
is a strong regular normal to
\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}
).

By Step 4,
\bar{\xi}\in{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu}_{1})}\times{\tangent_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\bar{\mu}_{2})}
, so by the definition of strong regular normal cone (cf.
Definition

2.24
) it remains to verify that
\bar{\xi}
satisfies the defining inequality of
\hat{\normal}^{s}_{\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}}((\bar{\mu}_{1},\bar{\mu}_{2}))
.
For
\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the strong regular normal cone of
Definition

2.24
is understood componentwise, with
\left\langle\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi}^{\prime}\right\rangle_{(\bar{\mu}_{1},\bar{\mu}_{2})}\coloneqq\left\langle\bar{\xi}_{1},\bar{\xi}_{1}^{\prime}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}_{1}}+\left\langle\bar{\xi}_{2},\bar{\xi}_{2}^{\prime}\right\rangle_{\bar{\mu}_{2}}
and
\left\|\bar{\xi}^{\prime}\right\|_{(\bar{\mu}_{1},\bar{\mu}_{2})}^{2}\coloneqq\left\|\bar{\xi}_{1}^{\prime}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}_{1}}^{2}+\left\|\bar{\xi}_{2}^{\prime}\right\|_{\bar{\mu}_{2}}^{2}
, where
\bar{\xi}=(\bar{\xi}_{1},\bar{\xi}_{2})
denotes the two components of
\bar{\xi}
.
For this, consider any
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
{(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})}_{\#}{\alpha}=\bar{\xi}_{1}
,
{(\pi_{2},\pi_{4})}_{\#}{\alpha}=\bar{\xi}_{2}
,
{(\pi_{1}+\pi_{5})}_{\#}{\alpha}\eqqcolon\mu_{1}\in\Omega_{1}
,
{(\pi_{2}+\pi_{6})}_{\#}{\alpha}\eqqcolon\mu_{2}\in\Omega_{2}
.
We want to show that

\displaystyle\heartsuit\coloneqq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\bar{\upsilon}_{1},\upsilon_{1}^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle\bar{\upsilon}_{2},\upsilon_{2}^{\prime}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\alpha(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2},\bar{\upsilon}_{1},\bar{\upsilon}_{2},{\upsilon}_{1}^{\prime},{\upsilon}_{2}^{\prime})
